\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Treatment exposure: binary versus continuous}

\textbf{Rule (\code{NOTATION.md} Rule 1).} $D_{it}$ is binary;
$D_{bc,it}$ is continuous. These are different objects and must never
be substituted for one another:
\begin{equation}
D_{bc,it} = \begin{cases} 1 & D_{it} = 1 \text{ (on drug)} \\
  \exp(-\lambda\, t_{sd,it}) & D_{it} = 0,\ t_{sd,it} > 0
    \text{ (off drug after exposure)} \\
  0 & D_{it} = 0 \text{ before any exposure},
\end{cases}
\label{eq:dbc}
\end{equation}
with $t_{sd,it}$ the time since discontinuation. The third case
matters for placebo-first crossover paths, where $t_{sd} = 0$ before
the first exposure and the exponential would otherwise return 1
(\code{exposure\_weight()} in
\code{analysis/\allowbreak scripts/\allowbreak
carryover-sensitivity/\allowbreak simulation-core.R},
inspected; Paper 01, Appendix B.4). In the limit of an assumed zero
half-life, the continuous predictor collapses onto the binary one,
$D_{bc,it} \to D_{it}$ as $\hat t_{1/2} \to 0$ (Paper 02); in this
sense the ``Unadjusted'' analysis specification is the boundary member
of the continuous-exposure family.

\note{2026-10-08: the before-exposure case was added; the earlier
version of Equation~\eqref{eq:dbc} had only two cases. \code{docs/52}
(open items) records that it is not established whether Paper 02's
crossover cells were rerun with the corrected exposure coding.}

\textbf{Pre-exposure visits.} As noted above, a decay weight evaluated
at $t_{sd} = 0$ equals 1, so a rule $D_{bc} = 1$ on drug and $2^{-t_{sd}/t_{1/2}^a}$ off
drug codes off-drug visits before first exposure as fully exposed. The
correct coding is Equation~\eqref{eq:dbc}, with
$\lambda = \ln 2/t_{1/2}^a$ at the half-life $t_{1/2}^a$ that the
analysis assumes (Source: \code{docs/45}, Section 8; compare the note
after Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-dmm}). In CO the placebo-first path is off drug for
its first four visits; under the defective coding its indicator is 1 at
every visit, $B_i D_{bc,it}$ is collinear with $B_i$ within each such
participant, and half the participants contribute no within-participant
contrast. The information for $\beta_{bm:D}$ then roughly halves and its
standard deviation rises by about $\sqrt 2 = 1.41$ (derived, approximate:
those participants still inform the other coefficients); the source
reports 1.44 to 1.47 and a power loss of 0.26 to 0.30 (quoted). At the
first CO off-drug visit, 2.5 weeks after discontinuation,
$D_{bc} = 0.031$ at $t_{1/2}^a = 0.5$ and $0.420$ at $t_{1/2}^a = 2$
(derived).

\subsection{Time arguments of the components, the shared clock, and the
phase contrasts}
\label{sec:invb-clocks}

\textbf{Clocks.} Let visit $t$ follow the previous visit by
$\Delta w_t$ weeks ($\Delta w_1$ is the first visit time). The design
builder (\code{buildtrialdesign()} in \code{R/buildtrialdesign.R},
inspected) defines three running clocks by recursion:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t_{od,t} &= D_t\,(t_{od,t-1} + \Delta w_t), \\
t_{sd,t} &= (1 - D_t)\,(t_{sd,t-1} + \Delta w_t)\,
  \mathbf 1\Big[\textstyle\sum_{s \le t} D_s > 0\Big], \\
t_{pb,t} &= \mathbf 1[\eta_t > 0]\,(t_{pb,t-1} + \Delta w_t),
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:invb-clocks}
\end{equation}
with all three zero before the first visit. Thus $t_{od}$ is the time
since the current exposure began and restarts at re-exposure; $t_{sd}$
is the time since the last on-drug visit, and zero on drug and before
any exposure (the third case of Equation~\eqref{eq:dbc}); and $t_{pb}$
accumulates over consecutive visits with positive expectancy and
resets to zero at a visit with $\eta_t = 0$. With $G_C$ the
modified-Gompertz curve of Equation~\eqref{eq:gompertz-05} with
component $C$'s parameters, the component means are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E[BR_{it}] &= m_{BR}\,G_{BR}(t_{od,t}) \quad \text{on drug}, \\
E[PB_{it}] &= \eta_t\, m_{PB}\, G_{PB}(t_{pb,t}), \\
E[TV_{it}] &= m_{TV}\, G_{TV}(w_t),
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:invb-component-means}
\end{equation}
with $w_t$ calendar time, $\mathrm{SD}(PB_{it}) = \eta_t\sigma_{PB}$,
and the off-drug $BR$ mean given by anchored carryover
(Equation~\eqref{eq:carryover-anchored}). $m_{TV}$ may be negative
(natural worsening). (Source: \code{docs/24}, Components 1 to 3 and
``The math behind it''; \code{docs/25}, Section 4; the code
inspected.) \note{\code{docs/24} describes $t_{pb}$ as ``time
accumulated while the expectancy is positive''; the code resets it to
zero at every visit with $\eta_t = 0$, which matters only for designs
with covert visits, such as the balanced-placebo design of
\S\ref{sec:invb-collinearity}.}

\textbf{The shared clock.} In every design in which $\eta_t > 0$ at
every visit (OL, OL+BDC, CO and Hybrid as coded), $t_{pb,t} = w_t$, so
$PB$ and $TV$ are functions of one clock and
\begin{equation}
E[PB_{it} + TV_{it}] = \eta_t\, m_{PB}\, G_{PB}(w_t) + m_{TV}\,
  G_{TV}(w_t),
\label{eq:invb-shared-clock}
\end{equation}
which under the published parameters, where $G_{PB} = G_{TV}$
(Table~\ref{tab:invb-rp}), is $(\eta_t m_{PB} + m_{TV})\,G(w_t)$.
The two components are then distinguished only through the change of
$\eta_t$ (at week 9 in the Hybrid design) and through their assumed
curve shapes (derived; Source: \code{docs/24}, ``How the trial design
lets us identify the components''). Every off-drug visit of these
designs is blinded, so $TV$ is never observed without $PB$.

\textbf{Components by phase.} Table~\ref{tab:invb-phases} gives the
mean structure of each Hybrid phase. Because $\eta_t$ and the
$PB$ and $TV$ means depend on the visit and not on the path, the
on-drug minus off-drug difference between paths at a common blinded
visit is $E[BR]$ on drug minus its carryover residual, with $PB$ and
$TV$ canceling exactly (derived). The open-label to blinded change
within drug, from week 8 to week 9, is
$m_{BR}[G_{BR}(9) - G_{BR}(8)] + m_{PB}[0.5\,G_{PB}(9) - G_{PB}(8)] +
m_{TV}[G_{TV}(9) - G_{TV}(8)]$, which approximates $-0.5\,E[PB]$ only
to the extent that the curves change little over that week (derived;
\code{docs/24} states the qualification, \code{docs/28}, Section 3.2,
omits it).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering\small
\caption{Mean components present by Hybrid phase (Source:
\code{docs/24}, \code{docs/28}, Section 3.1; expectancy vector
$(1, 1, 0.5, \ldots, 0.5)$ inspected).}
\label{tab:invb-phases}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule
Phase (weeks) & Drug & $\eta_t$ & Mean reduction $E[BR + PB + TV]$ \\
\midrule
Open label (4, 8) & on & 1 & $m_{BR}G_{BR}(t_{od}) + m_{PB}G_{PB}(w_t) +
  m_{TV}G_{TV}(w_t)$ \\
Blinded discontinuation (9--12) & by path & 0.5 & $E[BR_{it}] +
  0.5\,m_{PB}G_{PB}(w_t) + m_{TV}G_{TV}(w_t)$ \\
Blinded crossover (16, 20) & by path & 0.5 & $E[BR_{it}] +
  0.5\,m_{PB}G_{PB}(w_t) + m_{TV}G_{TV}(w_t)$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\note{\code{docs/28} (superseded by \code{docs/31}) states that the
lumped slope is unbiased ``if and only if'' the biomarker is
orthogonal to $PB$ and $TV$. By Equation~\eqref{eq:lumped-slope} the
condition is $w_{PB}\beta_{bm}^{PB} + w_{TV}\beta_{bm}^{TV} = 0$, which
orthogonality implies but which a cancellation could also satisfy;
the statement in \S\ref{sec:partition} is the correct one.}

%=============================================================
\section{Carryover on the mean}
\label{sec:carryover-mean}
%=============================================================

This section collects the carryover decay families and the rules by
which carryover acts on the off-drug mean of the drug response. Its
action on the biomarker-response correlation is treated with the
covariance encoding (\S\ref{sec:cov-carryover}).

\subsection{Carryover decay families}

Four decay forms are implemented (\code{carryover\_decay()} in
\code{implementations/\allowbreak nof1power/\allowbreak
R/\allowbreak carryover\_models.R}, inspected); the
first three satisfy $\phi(t_{1/2}) = 0.5$:
\begin{align}
\text{Exponential:} \qquad & \phi_{\exp}(t_{sd}) = \exp(-\lambda\,
  t_{sd}), \qquad \lambda = \frac{\ln 2}{t_{1/2}}
  \label{eq:decay-exp} \\
\text{Linear:} \qquad & \phi_{\mathrm{lin}}(t_{sd}) = \max\!\left(0,\,
  1 - \frac{t_{sd}}{2\,t_{1/2}}\right)
  \label{eq:decay-lin} \\
\text{Weibull:} \qquad & \phi_{\mathrm{weib}}(t_{sd}) =
  \exp\!\big(-(\lambda_w t_{sd})^k\big), \qquad \lambda_w =
  \frac{(\ln 2)^{1/k}}{t_{1/2}}
  \label{eq:decay-weib} \\
\text{Power:} \qquad & \phi_{\mathrm{pow}}(t_{sd}) = \max\!\left(0,\,
  \Big(1 - \frac{t_{sd}}{3\,t_{1/2}}\Big)^{\!p}\right)
  \label{eq:decay-pow}
\end{align}
\note{2026-10-08: corrected. The section previously stated that all
four forms satisfy $\phi(t_{1/2}) = 0.5$, as the function's
documentation also does. The power form gives $\phi(t_{1/2}) =
(2/3)^p$, which equals 0.5 only at $p = \ln 2/\ln 1.5 \approx 1.71$;
at the default $p = 1.5$ it is 0.544 (derived), so its nominal
half-life is not the time at which half the effect remains.}

The Weibull family recovers the exponential exactly at shape $k=1$; a
value $k<1$ produces a heavier elimination tail than exponential, and
$k>1$ a lighter one. Paper 02, whose remit is this decay family, tests
$k \in \{0.25, 0.5, 2.0, 4.0\}$ against the exponential default. Its
ranking of the specifications is largely unchanged, but the advantage
of the exposure-weighted specification over the unadjusted one, where
it leads (Hybrid and OL+BDC), widens under the light-tailed shapes and
narrows to statistical insignificance under the two heavy-tailed
shapes $k \in \{0.25, 0.5\}$ (Paper 02, Abstract).

\note{2026-10-08: corrected. This paragraph previously said Paper 02
finds the conclusions ``materially unchanged'' across Weibull shapes;
Paper 02 reports the narrowing just described.}

\subsection{Anchored versus compounding carryover on the mean}
\label{sec:cg-anchor}

Paper 05 states a carryover \emph{recursion} for the
biological-response mean during off-drug periods,
\begin{equation}
\mu[t] = \mathrm{base}[t] + \mu[t-1]\cdot\phi(t_{sd}),
\label{eq:carryover-recursion}
\end{equation}
and the published process of Hendrickson et al.\ uses the same
recursion, $\mu_{BR,t} = \mu_{BR,t-1}\,2^{-t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}}$ (Paper
01, Methods). Because $t_{sd}$ is the \emph{cumulative} time since
discontinuation, the recursion counts elapsed time twice: at the
$j$-th consecutive off-drug visit it applies
$\prod_{l \le j}\phi(t_{sd,l})$ instead of $\phi(t_{sd,j})$, and so
over-decays every off-drug visit after the first in a run (derived;
Paper 01, Appendix B.3). The package now uses \emph{anchored}
carryover, decaying from the last on-drug value:
\begin{equation}
\mu_{BR,t} = \mathrm{base}_t + \mu_{BR,\mathrm{last}}\,
  \phi(t_{sd,t}), \qquad \phi(t_{sd}) = 2^{-t_{sd}/t_{1/2}}
  \ \text{(exponential)},
\label{eq:carryover-anchored}
\end{equation}
with $\mu_{BR,\mathrm{last}}$ the drug-response mean at the most
recent on-drug visit (\code{R/generateData.R}, inspected; Paper 01,
Methods, refinement step 3).

\note{2026-10-08: superseded in part. Equation~\eqref{eq:carryover-recursion}
was previously presented as the carryover mechanism without
qualification. It is retained as Paper 05's statement; the current
mechanism is Equation~\eqref{eq:carryover-anchored} (Paper 01 as
restated, \code{docs/51}). Whether the stored results of Paper 05 were
generated under the recursion or under anchored carryover was not
checked.}

With the exponential decay of the published recursion, the
$k$-th consecutive off-drug visit after a last on-drug mean
$\mu_{\mathrm{last}}$ has
\begin{equation}
\mu_{BR}^{\mathrm{comp}} = \mu_{\mathrm{last}}\,
  2^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k} t_{sd,j}/t_{1/2}}
\quad\text{against the anchored}\quad
\mu_{BR}^{\mathrm{anch}} = \mu_{\mathrm{last}}\,2^{-t_{sd,k}/t_{1/2}}:
\label{eq:cg-anchor}
\end{equation}
the decay compounds from the second off-drug visit and runs faster
than the stated half-life (Paper~01, Appendix~B.3; \code{docs/40},
Section 3; derived). On path 1 at $t_{1/2} = 1$ with
$\mu_{\mathrm{last}} = 10.19$, both give $5.09$ at $t_{sd} = 1$; at
$t_{sd} = 2$ the recursion gives $1.27$ and the anchored form $2.55$
(verified). Paper~01's refinement 3 adopts the anchored form.

\note{Equation~\eqref{eq:carryover-recursion} as Paper~05 writes it
leaves unstated whether $\phi$ takes the cumulative $t_{sd}$ or the
one-visit increment; with the increment it would not compound. The
published code takes the cumulative value, so it compounds as stated
above (inspected: \code{R/buildtrialdesign.R} and
\code{R/generateData.R}, line 88, at commit 58b32a9; \code{docs/53},
Sections 4 and 5). A paired simulation in \code{docs/53} (Section 8.3;
Hybrid, $N = 70$, $c_{bm} = 0.25$, 500 replicates) found that replacing
the recursion by the anchored form changes the interaction estimate by
at most 0.005 and at most 4 of 500 test decisions at
$t_{1/2} \le 2$ weeks. With the restart of the Gompertz clock at re-exposure it produces
a downward step in the mean at the first re-exposure visit when the
residual exceeds the restarted curve (Paper~01, Appendix~B.3).}

\subsection{The 2024 revision of the published code: a scale factor on
the carryover exponent}
\label{sec:invd-scalefactor}

The vendored copy of the Hendrickson et al.\ code that served as Paper
01's earlier comparison arm is upstream HEAD \code{06dac83}
(2024-11-27), not the published commit \code{58b32a9}
(\code{docs/54}, review note and Section 2).
Its recursion multiplies the carryover exponent by a scale factor
$s_f$ (code \code{scalefactor}, default 2):
\begin{equation}
\mu_{BR,t} = \mu^{\mathrm{raw}}_{BR,t} + \mu_{BR,t-1}\,
  2^{-s_f\, t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}}
\qquad \text{off drug after exposure,}
\label{eq:invd-scalefactor}
\end{equation}
with $\mu^{\mathrm{raw}}_{BR,t} = 0$ off drug. At the $k$-th consecutive
off-drug visit after a last on-drug mean $\mu_{\mathrm{last}}$, with
cumulative times $t_{sd,1} < \cdots < t_{sd,k}$,
\begin{equation}
\mu_{BR}^{(s_f)} = \mu_{\mathrm{last}}\,
  2^{-s_f\sum_{j \le k} t_{sd,j}/t_{1/2}},
\qquad
\frac{\mu_{BR}^{(s_f)}}{\mu_{BR}^{\mathrm{anch}}} =
  2^{-\left(s_f \sum_{j\le k} t_{sd,j} - t_{sd,k}\right)/t_{1/2}},
\label{eq:invd-scalefactor-ratio}
\end{equation}
which is Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-anchor} at $s_f = 1$ (derived). With
$s_f = 1$ the first off-drug visit of a run is exact and later ones
compound; with $s_f = 2$ even the first is understated, by the factor
$2^{-t_{sd,1}/t_{1/2}}$. Table~\ref{tab:invd-scalefactor} gives the
values on Hybrid path 1 (Source: \code{docs/54},
Section 3.5, as corrected 2026-10-07; every entry reproduced,
\code{check-stress-test-construct.R}, Section 3).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering\small
\caption{Drug-response mean at the off-drug visits of Hybrid path 1,
$t_{1/2} = 1$ week, under the recursion with $s_f = 1$ and $s_f = 2$
and under anchored decay (Source:
\code{docs/54}, Section 3.5; reproduced).}
\label{tab:invd-scalefactor}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}
\toprule
Visit & $t_{sd}$ & $s_f = 1$ & $s_f = 2$ & Anchored &
  Ratio, $s_f = 1$ & Ratio, $s_f = 2$ \\
\midrule
BD3 (week 11) & 1 & 5.094 & 2.547 & 5.094 & 1.000 & 0.500 \\
BD4 (week 12) & 2 & 1.273 & 0.159 & 2.547 & 0.500 & 0.0625 \\
COp (week 20) & 4 & 0.268 & 0.017 & 0.268 & 1.000 & 0.0625 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent The last on-drug means are 10.187 (week 10) for BD3 and BD4,
and 4.281 (week 16, after the Gompertz clock restarts) for COp. The
scale factor acts on the mean channel and does not enter the
correlation matrix; its counterpart in the correlation matrix is the
ratio-graded coupling of \S\ref{sec:invd-ce-2024}. \note{The
whitepaper's general statement that ``at $s_f = 2$ even the first
occasion is wrong (0.500x)'' holds at $t_{sd} = t_{1/2}$; in general
the first-visit ratio is $2^{-(s_f - 1)t_{sd,1}/t_{1/2}}$, 0.0625 at
COp, which is the first off-drug visit of its run.}

%=============================================================
\section{Two architectures for a biomarker-treatment interaction}
\label{sec:architectures}
%=============================================================

Paper 01 states the two ways in which the target estimand,
$\beta_{bm:D}$, is encoded into the data-generating process of the
compendium. The version of Paper 01 this document first drew on (the
pre-restatement version, now the file
\code{report-2026-10-07-pre-restatement.Rmd} in the \code{archive/}
folder of the Paper 01 directory) presented
them as structurally different encodings whose power diverges under
carryover. The restated Paper 01 (October 2026) shows that, conditional
on the biomarker, the covariance encoding is mean moderation with a
coupling-shaped exposure profile and a reduced residual variance
(Equation~\eqref{eq:archB-conditional} below), so that the size of the
carryover loss is set by the exposure profile of the interaction, not
by the channel that carries it.

\subsection{Mean moderation}

In the mean-moderation architecture (formerly Architecture A) the
biomarker shifts the drug response in the conditional mean. The
package (\code{dgp\_architecture = 'mean\_moderation'}, default
\code{moderation\_scaling = 'constant'}; \code{R/generateData.R},
inspected) and the restated Paper 01 (Methods) apply the shift
\begin{equation}
BR_{it} \;\leftarrow\; BR_{it} + \beta_{bm}\, b_i\, \sigma_{BR}\, h_t,
\qquad
h_t = \begin{cases}
  1 & D_{it} = 1 \\
  0 \ \text{(plain)} \ \text{or}\ 2^{-t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}}
    \ \text{(decayed)} & D_{it} = 0,
\end{cases}
\label{eq:archA}
\end{equation}
after the multivariate-normal draw, with $b_i$ the standardized
biomarker. The package implements the plain profile; the decayed
profile is Paper 01's comparison arm. Because $Y_{it} = \mathrm{BL}_i
- (TV_{it} + PB_{it} + BR_{it})$, the implied on-drug interaction
coefficient on the raw biomarker is
\begin{equation}
\beta_{bm:D} = -\beta_{bm}\,\frac{\sigma_{BR}}{\sigma_{bm}}
\label{eq:archA-kent}
\end{equation}
(Paper 01, Estimand), the coefficient of $D_{it}B_i$ in the standard
additive effect-modification form $Y_{it} = \beta_0 + \beta_D D_{it} +
\beta_B B_i + \beta_{bm:D} D_{it} B_i + \ldots$ of Kent et al. The
contrast between two participants at a common exposure state is
\begin{equation}
E[Y_{it} \mid b_1] - E[Y_{it} \mid b_2] = -\beta_{bm}\,\sigma_{BR}\,
  h_t\,(b_1 - b_2) + (\text{terms not involving } h_t),
\label{eq:archA-contrast}
\end{equation}
so carryover enters the moderation contrast only through the profile
$h_t$. Under the plain profile the moderation stops when the drug
stops, and power is flat in $t_{1/2}$ (0.681 at every half-life for
the paired-difference statistic E9, Hybrid, $N=70$,
$\beta_{bm}=0.25$, compound symmetry; Paper 01, Results). Paper 01
calls this flatness a coding choice rather than a finding, because
the drug effect itself carries over.

\note{2026-10-08: superseded. This subsection previously stated mean
moderation as $Y_{it} = \mu_0 + \beta_1 D_{it}(1 + \beta_{bm}B_i) +
\varepsilon_{it}$ with interaction $\beta_3 = \beta_1\beta_{bm}$, the
multiplicative form of the pre-restatement Paper 01. That form survives only as
the trajectory-scaled option of Paper 07
(Equation~\eqref{eq:gompertz-mean-mod}); it is not the package default,
and it used the raw biomarker $B_i$ where the implemented shift uses
$b_i$.}

\subsection{Covariance moderation}

In the covariance-moderation architecture (formerly Architecture B)
the biomarker and the response components, over all visits, are drawn
jointly from one multivariate normal whose biomarker-response
correlation, not whose mean, depends on exposure state:
\begin{equation}
\begin{pmatrix} B_i \\ BR_{i\cdot} \end{pmatrix} \sim
\mathrm{MVN}\!\left(
  \begin{pmatrix}\mu_{bm} \\ \mu_{BR,\cdot}\end{pmatrix},\,
  \Sigma\right), \qquad
\mathrm{Cor}(B_i, BR_{it}) = c_{bm}\, g_t,
\label{eq:archB}
\end{equation}
with the graded coupling $g_t$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:cor-carryover}:
1 on drug, $e^{-\lambda t_{sd}} = 2^{-t_{sd}/t_{1/2}}$ off drug after
exposure, 0 before any exposure. Without carryover the estimand is
$\beta_{bm:D} = -c_{bm}\,\sigma_{BR}/\sigma_{bm}$ (Paper 01,
Estimand). Conditioning on the biomarker (Paper 01, Discussion
``Covariance-based and mean-based binding''), with
$b_i = (B_i - \mu_{bm})/\sigma_{bm}$ standardized by the population
moments,
\begin{equation}
E(BR_{it} \mid B_i) = \mu_{BR,t} + c_{bm}\, g_t\, \sigma_{BR}\, b_i,
\qquad
\mathrm{Cov}(BR_{i\cdot} \mid B_i) = \Sigma_{BR} - c_{bm}^2
  \sigma_{BR}^2\, g g^\top,
\label{eq:archB-conditional}
\end{equation}
so the covariance encoding is mean moderation
(Equation~\eqref{eq:archA}) with exposure profile $h_t = g_t$ and
strength $\beta_{bm} = c_{bm}$, and a drug-response covariance reduced
by a rank-one term. Here $\Sigma_{BR}$ is the drug-response covariance
block, $g$ the vector of the $g_t$ ($g = (g_1, \ldots, g_8)$ in the
Hybrid design), and $\mathrm{Var}(BR_{it}) = \sigma_{BR}^2$ at each
visit (derived: re-checked here from the conditional-normal formulas,
since $\mathrm{Cov}(BR_{it}, B_i) = c_{bm} g_t
\sigma_{BR}\sigma_{bm}$; verified to machine precision on Hybrid path
1 under graded coupling at $t_{1/2} = 1$ week,
\code{check-coupling-closed-form.R}). The $TV$ and $PB$ blocks and
their covariances with $BR$ are unchanged, since neither covaries with
$B$. The consequences for the slope, the residual variance and the
power of the interaction test, and for the positive-definiteness
ceiling, are derived in \S\ref{sec:cg-equiv} (Paper 01, Discussion).

Under the published step rule ($g_t = 1$ wherever the drug-response
mean is nonzero), the coupling gap $1 - \bar g_{\mathrm{off}}$ is zero
at every positive half-life, and closed-form E9 power falls from
0.721 to 0.050, the test size (\S\ref{sec:cov-carryover} and
\S\ref{sec:cg-step}; Paper 01, Results).

\note{2026-10-08: superseded. This subsection previously gave the
off-drug correlation as $c_{bm}e^{-\lambda t_{sd}}$ at every off-drug
visit (no zero before exposure, no distinction from the published step
rule) and reported, as Paper 01's headline, Hybrid carryover losses of
``1.4--6.9 percentage points under mean moderation but 5.1--15.4
points under covariance moderation'' with $z = 2.80$ (Hybrid) and
$z = 7.64$ (OL+BDC). Two corrections apply. First, those figures were
misquoted: \code{NOTATION.md} (Part 2) reports that matching every
design to $N = 70$ moved the Hybrid relative losses from 1.4\% to
6.9\% (mean moderation) and from 5.1\% to 15.4\% (covariance
moderation); they are before-and-after values of relative percent
loss, not ranges of percentage points. Second, the comparison itself
is superseded: the restated Paper 01 shows that a larger loss under
covariance binding than under plain mean binding compares a fading
interaction with a non-fading one. Which coupling rule and carryover
recursion the pre-restatement runs used was not checked here. The $z$
statistics are kept only as a record of the earlier version.}

\subsection{Covariance moderation with carryover}
\label{sec:cov-carryover}

Under the covariance-moderation architecture
(Equation~\eqref{eq:archB}), carryover acts
on the biomarker-response correlation itself, not only
on the mean, through the coupling profile $g_t$:
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Cor}(B_i, BR_{it}) = c_{bm}\, g_t, \qquad
g_t = \begin{cases}
  1 & D_{it} = 1 \\
  \phi(t_{sd,it}) & D_{it} = 0 \text{ after first exposure} \\
  0 & \text{before first exposure},
\end{cases}
\label{eq:cor-carryover}
\end{equation}
with $\phi$ any of Equations \eqref{eq:decay-exp}--\eqref{eq:decay-pow}
(Paper 02). With exponential decay the off-drug coupling is
$2^{-t_{sd}/t_{1/2}} = e^{-\lambda t_{sd}}$. This is the \emph{graded}
coupling of Paper 01 (as restated) and \code{docs/37}, and the rule
the package implements (\code{buildSigma()} in \code{R/generateData.R},
inspected).

The process published by Hendrickson et al.\ used a \emph{step}
coupling instead: $g_t = 1$ wherever the drug-response mean is nonzero
and $0$ elsewhere (Paper 01, Methods). Under carryover every off-drug
visit that follows an exposure has a positive drug-response mean, so
it keeps the full on-drug correlation $c_{bm}$. The biomarker then
moderates the on-drug and off-drug responses equally, and the
on-minus-off interaction vanishes at \emph{any} positive half-life,
however small (Paper 01, Results; \code{docs/37}). Graded coupling
restores a decline that is smooth and, for the paired-difference
statistic, monotone in $t_{1/2}$, and that vanishes faster than any
power of $t_{1/2}$ as $t_{1/2} \to 0$ (Paper 01, Appendix A.3).

The conditional-mean reading of this construction, which connects it
to the mean-moderation architecture, is the conditional mean of
Equation~\eqref{eq:archB-conditional}, which in the raw biomarker reads
$E[BR_{it} \mid B_i] = \mu_{BR}(t) + c_{bm}\, g_t\,
(\sigma_{BR}/\sigma_{bm})\,(B_i - \mu_{bm})$ (Paper 01, Discussion), with
$b_i$ computed from the population mean and SD of the biomarker.

\note{2026-10-08: corrected. The section previously gave only the
decayed correlation $c_{bm}\phi(t_{sd})$ for every off-drug visit,
without the zero before first exposure, and did not distinguish the
published step rule from graded coupling.
The conditional mean was previously given for
$D_{it}=1$ only, with the off-drug case described in words.}

\subsection{The estimand and the coupling rules}
\label{sec:cg-estimand}

The biomarker is correlated with the drug response only,
$\mathrm{Cor}(B_i, BR_{it}) = c_{bm} g_t$ with $0 \le g_t \le 1$ and
$g_0 = 0$ at baseline; $g_t$ is the \emph{coupling} (\code{docs/36}
writes $r_t = c_{bm} g_t$). The estimand is the on-drug moderation of
the drug response, which without carryover is
\begin{equation}
\beta_{bm:D} = -c_{bm}\,\sigma_{BR}/\sigma_{bm}
  \ \text{(covariance binding)}, \qquad
\beta_{bm:D} = -\beta_{bm}\,\sigma_{BR}/\sigma_{bm}
  \ \text{(mean binding)},
\label{eq:cg-estimand}
\end{equation}
both $-0.1302$ at the matched value $0.25$ with $\sigma_{BR} = 8$ and
$\sigma_{bm} = 15.36$, the setting of \S\ref{sec:cg} (Paper~01, Methods
``Estimand''; derived). An analysis built on the binary $D_{it}$ estimates this only
when no moderation remains off drug; under carryover it estimates an
attenuated on-minus-off contrast, made precise by
Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-slope}. With
$\phi_t = 2^{-t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}} = \phi_{\exp}(t_{sd,t})$
(Equation~\eqref{eq:decay-exp}) at an off-drug visit after exposure,
and $\phi_t = 0$ before any exposure or when $t_{1/2} = 0$, four rules
set the off-drug interaction (Source: \code{docs/37}, Section 2;
Paper~01, Table~1):
\begin{align}
\text{Step (published):}\quad & g_t = \mathbf 1[\mu_{BR,t} \neq 0];
  \label{eq:cg-step} \\
\text{Graded:}\quad & g_t = 1 \text{ on drug}, \ \phi_t \text{ off drug};
  \label{eq:cg-graded} \\
\text{Mean moderation:}\quad & g_t = 0;\ BR_{it} \text{ shifted by }
  \beta_{bm}\sigma_{BR} b_i h_t,\ h_t = D_{it};
  \label{eq:cg-mm} \\
\text{Decayed mean moderation:}\quad & g_t = 0;\ h_t = 1 \text{ on
  drug}, \ \phi_t \text{ off drug},
  \label{eq:cg-dmm}
\end{align}
with $\mu_{BR,t}$ the mean drug response and $b_i$ the standardized
biomarker. Off-drug visits before first exposure carry coupling and
moderation $0$ under every rule.

\note{The pre-exposure zero is a condition, not a detail: a rule
``coupling equals the decay weight'' with $e^{-\lambda t_{sd}} = 1$ at
$t_{sd} = 0$ gives full coupling at baseline and at the placebo-first
crossover visits. Equations~\eqref{eq:dbc} and~\eqref{eq:cor-carryover}
state the zero; \code{docs/37}, Appendix~A, notes its absence from
Paper~02's indicator.}

\subsection{How trajectory shape interacts with carryover and the
two architectures}

Under the \emph{mean-moderation} architecture Paper 07 uses the
trajectory-scaled form, which it writes schematically as
\begin{equation}
Y_{it} = \mu_0 + \beta_D D_{it}\,(1 + \beta_{bm} B_i) + \varepsilon_{it},
\label{eq:gompertz-mean-mod}
\end{equation}
so that the biomarker-dependent component of the treatment effect
rides the trajectory. The package implements it
(\code{moderation\_scaling = 'trajectory'} in \code{R/generateData.R},
inspected) as an on-drug shift of $BR_{it}$ by
\begin{equation}
\beta_{bm}\, b_i\, \sigma_{BR}\, \frac{\mu_{BR}(t)}{\bar\mu_{BR}^{\,\mathrm{on}}},
\label{eq:gompertz-mean-mod-code}
\end{equation}
where $\mu_{BR}(t)$ is the drug-response mean curve and
$\bar\mu_{BR}^{\,\mathrm{on}}$ its mean over on-drug visits, so that
the mean on-drug magnitude of the shift per unit $b_i$ equals
$\sigma_{BR}$. A family with a slower early rise therefore places less
of the interaction signal on the earliest on-drug visits, while the
marginal interaction effect size is held fixed.
Equation~\eqref{eq:gompertz-mean-mod} uses the raw biomarker $B_i$;
the implemented shift uses the standardized $b_i$ and is additive in
$BR$, not a multiple of a scalar treatment effect $\beta_D$.
\note{2026-10-09: notation. Paper 07 writes the treatment effect as
$\beta_1$; it is the canonical treatment main effect $\beta_D$ here.}

Under the \emph{covariance-moderation} architecture, by contrast, the
interaction lives in the biomarker-response correlation
$\mathrm{Cor}(B_i, BR_{it}) = c_{bm}\, g_t$ of
Equation~\eqref{eq:cor-carryover}, whose coupling profile
$g_t$ is set by exposure state and, off drug,
by $t_{sd}$ and $t_{1/2}$, not by the mean curve $BR$ follows. The
biomarker-response block of the correlation matrix is constructed
directly, so trajectory-shape misspecification cannot move the
covariance-channel interaction test; Paper 07 confirms this with a
matched-seed check. This is the structural reason Paper 07's 16-cell
factorial (four families $\times$ two architectures $\times$ two
biomarker-effect levels, $N=70$, OL+BDC design, $t_{1/2}=0$ to isolate
the trajectory effect from carryover) finds power differences of only
a few percentage points across families under both architectures: the
mean channel is mildly shape-sensitive, and the covariance channel is
not shape-sensitive by construction. Paper 07 is explicit that the
grid fixes $t_{1/2}=0$; at $t_{1/2}>0$ the off-drug decay would
interact with each family's own profile, a combination that paper
does not evaluate.

\note{2026-10-08: the correlation $c_{bm} g_t$
replaces a derivative $\partial\,\mathrm{Cor}(BM,BR)/\partial D_{bc}$
that had no defined meaning. The independence from the trajectory
holds for the graded coupling now in the package. Under the published
step rule (\S\ref{sec:cov-carryover}) the coupling was switched on
wherever the drug-response mean was nonzero, so at $t_{1/2} > 0$ it
depended on the mean curve through its support; at $t_{1/2} = 0$, the
case Paper 07 evaluates, the two rules agree.}

\subsection{The 2024 revision of the published code: ratio-graded
coupling and an uncoupled first visit}
\label{sec:invd-ce-2024}

\textbf{Ratio-graded coupling.} In the 2024 code
(\S\ref{sec:invd-scalefactor}) an off-drug visit after exposure
receives the coupling $c_{bm}\,\mu_{BR,t}/\mu_{BR,t-1}$, the ratio of
consecutive adjusted means of Equation~\eqref{eq:invd-scalefactor}. The
history cancels, so
\begin{equation}
g_t = \begin{cases}
  1, & D_{it} = 1,\ t \ge 2, \\
  2^{-s_f\, t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}}, & D_{it} = 0 \text{ after exposure}, \\
  0, & \text{before first exposure, or } t = 1,
\end{cases}
\label{eq:invd-ratio-coupling}
\end{equation}
an exponential decay at half-life $t_{1/2}/s_f$ that does not compound
across consecutive off-drug visits, although the mean does (derived;
\code{docs/54}, Section 3.3, as corrected). The
first visit is never assigned ($g_1 = 0$), because the loop that forms
the ratio starts at the second visit (Section 3.4 of the source;
inspected in the vendored file
\code{vendored-\allowbreak hendrickson-\allowbreak generateData.R}). On Hybrid
path 1 at $t_{1/2} = 1$ week and $c_{bm} = 0.45$, the off-drug
couplings are $0.45 \cdot 2^{-2} = 0.1125$, $0.45 \cdot 2^{-4} =
0.0281$ and $0.45 \cdot 2^{-8} = 0.0018$ at $t_{sd} = 1, 2, 4$, against
the graded $0.45 e^{-\ln 2} = 0.225$ at $t_{sd} = 1$ (verified,
\code{check-stress-test-construct.R}, Section 3). At $t_{1/2} = 0$ the off-drug mean
is zero, so Equation~\eqref{eq:invd-ratio-coupling}, the step rule and
graded coupling coincide off drug.

\subsection{The pharmacokinetic argument for graded coupling, and the
historical coupling rules}
\label{sec:inva-pk}

\textbf{The spread argument.} On drug, joint normality gives
$E(BR_{it} \mid b_i = \pm1) = \mu_{BR,t} \pm c_{bm}\sigma_{BR}$, a
spread $\Delta_{\mathrm{on}} = 2c_{bm}\sigma_{BR}$ between participants
one standard deviation above and below the biomarker mean. If carryover
is pharmacokinetic, every participant's drug effect decays by the same
factor $\phi_t = 2^{-t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}}$, so the off-drug conditional
means are $\phi_t(\mu_{BR,t_0} \pm c_{bm}\sigma_{BR})$, with $t_0$ the
last on-drug visit, and the spread is
$\Delta_{\mathrm{off}} = \phi_t\Delta_{\mathrm{on}}$. Matching it to the
conditional-normal spread $2\,c_{bm}g_t\,\sigma_{BR}$, which holds when
$\mathrm{Var}(BR_{it}) = \sigma_{BR}^2$ at every visit, gives
\begin{equation}
g_t = \phi_t = 2^{-t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}} = e^{-\lambda t_{sd,t}},
\qquad \lambda = \frac{\ln 2}{t_{1/2}},
\label{eq:inva-pk}
\end{equation}
the graded coupling of Equation~\eqref{eq:cor-carryover}, with the
coupling decay rate equal to the elimination rate. (Derived. Source:
\code{docs/23}, Sections~5.2--5.4, from \code{docs/08}.) The premise,
a common decay of each participant's conditional mean, is decayed mean
moderation, so the argument is the conditional-mean identity of
Equation~\eqref{eq:archB-conditional} read in reverse: it fixes the conditional
mean and says nothing about the conditional variance. The construct
keeps $\mathrm{Var}(BR_{it} \mid B_i) = \sigma_{BR}^2(1 - c_{bm}^2g_t^2)$,
nearly $\sigma_{BR}^2$ off drug, whereas a decay of the whole
drug-dependent deviation would shrink it by $\phi_t^2$ (\code{docs/23},
Section~5.8; argued). \note{\code{docs/23} writes the coupled
correlation $r_t = c_{bm}g_t$ and calls $\phi_t$ the decay factor, as
\code{docs/37} does.}

Table~\ref{tab:inva-pk} evaluates the rules at the first off-drug
visit of Hybrid path~1 at $t_{1/2} = 1$ week ($t_{sd} = 1$, with
$\mu_{BR} = 10.19$ at the last on-drug visit and $5.09$ off drug, the
values of \S\ref{sec:cg-anchor}), $c_{bm} = 0.5$, $\sigma_{BR} = 8$.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering\small
\caption{Conditional mean of $BR$ at $b = \pm1$ under alternative
off-drug coupling rules, Hybrid path~1, $t_{1/2} = 1$, $c_{bm} = 0.5$
(Source: \code{docs/23}, Sections~5.5--5.6; verified,
\code{check-historical-results.R}, block G).}
\label{tab:inva-pk}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
\toprule
Visit, rule & $c_{bm}g_t$ & $b = +1$ & $b = -1$ & Spread / mean \\
\midrule
On drug (week 10) & 0.50 & 14.19 & 6.19 & 0.785 \\
Off drug, graded, $\lambda = \ln 2/t_{1/2}$ & 0.25 & 7.09 & 3.09 & 0.785 \\
Off drug, zero coupling & 0 & 5.09 & 5.09 & 0 \\
Off drug, slow decay, $\lambda = 1$ & 0.184 & 6.56 & 3.62 & 0.578 \\
Off drug, full coupling (published step rule) & 0.50 & 9.09 & 1.09 & 1.57 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Only graded coupling keeps the ratio of the biomarker spread to the mean
response at its on-drug value (verified). At the published
$t_{1/2} = 0.1$ week the drug-response mean one week after
discontinuation is $10.19\cdot2^{-10} = 0.010$, about 0.1 percent of
its last on-drug value; the step rule keeps $c_{bm} = 0.6$ there, so the
conditional means at $b = \pm1$ (biomarker 139.7 and 109.0) are
$0.010 \pm 0.6\cdot8 = +4.81$ and $-4.79$, a biomarker-specific spread
around a near-zero mean, against $0.010 \pm 0.005$ under graded
coupling (derived; verified, block G; Source: \code{docs/23},
Section~5.7). \note{\code{docs/23} quotes $\pm4.8$ with mean $+0.01$.
Its account of the consequence for power (added noise inflating the
standard error) is superseded by the exact one of \S\ref{sec:cg-step}:
the coupling gap is zero and the slope vanishes. Its quoted
publication figure ``0.95 to 0.18'' was not re-verified; the figure
verified in \code{docs/36} is 0.74 to 0.12 (Hybrid, $N = 70$,
$c_{bm} = 0.3$).}

\textbf{The proportional rule.} An intermediate version of the
published code (commit \code{8609f12}) set the off-drug coupling to the
ratio of successive drug-response means, $c_{bm}g_t = c_{bm}\,
\mu_{BR,t}/\mu_{BR,t-1}$ (\code{docs/23}, Section~5.1), and introduced
a factor 2 in the decay exponent, $2^{-2t_{sd}/t_{1/2}}$, which halves
the effective half-life (\code{docs/23}, Section~2.4). The same rule,
with the factor written as a scale factor $s_f = 2$, is in the 2024
upstream code, where it is derived in \S\ref{sec:invd-ce-2024}
(Equation~\eqref{eq:invd-ratio-coupling}): with no drug-response base
off drug the ratio is $2^{-s_f t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}}$ at every off-drug
visit of a run, so the rule is graded coupling at half-life $t_{1/2}/s_f$
(derived; also checked on a three-visit run, \code{check-historical-results.R},
block L). \note{Derived from the formulas as \code{docs/23} states
them; commit \code{8609f12} was not inspected, and whether its off-drug
mean had a zero base was not checked. The ratio is undefined where
$\mu_{BR,t-1} = 0$, at the first visit, which is consistent with the
\code{if (p > 1)} guard that \code{docs/23}, Section~2.5, describes
(argued) and with the uncoupled first visit of the 2024 code
(\S\ref{sec:invd-ce-2024}).}

\subsection{Historical mean-moderation formulations and the calibration
check}
\label{sec:inva-history}

\code{docs/19} records three formulations of mean moderation that
preceded Equation~\eqref{eq:archA}. Each is a post-draw shift of
$BR_{it}$, and each has a conditional-mean reading in terms of the
profile $h_t$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:archA}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*), itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Multiplicative scaling,}
  $BR_{it}\leftarrow BR_{it}(1 + \beta_{bm}b_i)$. The shift is
  $\beta_{bm}b_iBR_{it}$, with $E(\beta_{bm}b_iBR_{it} \mid b_i) =
  \beta_{bm}\sigma_{BR}b_i\,(\mu_{BR,t}/\sigma_{BR})$ when $BR$ is drawn
  independently of $b_i$: mean moderation with profile
  $h_t = \mu_{BR,t}/\sigma_{BR}$, which tracks the drug-response mean
  and so decays off drug with the carryover, plus a
  biomarker-by-noise term $\beta_{bm}b_i(BR_{it} - \mu_{BR,t})$
  (derived). It is a trajectory-scaled shift
  (Equation~\eqref{eq:gompertz-mean-mod-code}) without the
  normalization. A profile that decays off drug loses power with the
  half-life, as decayed mean moderation does (\S\ref{sec:cg-equiv},
  Table~\ref{tab:cg-power}; argued for this profile, which was not
  evaluated in closed form), which is consistent with the source's
  report of a loss of about 60 percent under carryover (50 replicates;
  quoted).
\item \textbf{Additive with exposure decay,}
  $BR_{it}\leftarrow BR_{it} + \beta_{bm}b_i\sigma_{BR}h_t$ with
  $h_t = 2^{-t_{sd}/t_{1/2}}$ off drug: decayed mean moderation,
  Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-dmm}. On the crossover schedule (2.5-week
  spacing, $t_{1/2} = 1$) the off-drug profile is 0.177, 0.031, 0.0055
  and 0.0010, and the per-SD shift at $\beta_{bm} = 0.45$,
  $\sigma_{BR} = 8$ is 0.64 falling to 0.0035 (verified, block C).
\item \textbf{Raw biomarker times time on drug,}
  $BR_{it}\leftarrow BR_{it} + \beta_{bm}B_it_{od,it}$. With
  $B_i = \mu_{bm} + \sigma_{bm}b_i$ the shift splits into a
  biomarker-free drift and a moderation term,
  \begin{equation}
  \beta_{bm}B_it_{od,it} = \beta_{bm}\mu_{bm}t_{od,it}
    + \beta_{bm}\sigma_{bm}t_{od,it}\,b_i
  \label{eq:inva-attempt3}
  \end{equation}
  (derived). At $\beta_{bm} = 0.45$, $\mu_{bm} = 124.33$,
  $\sigma_{bm} = 15.36$ and $t_{od} = 10$ the drift is 559.5 and the
  moderation is 69.1 per standard deviation of the biomarker, 8.6 times
  $\sigma_{BR}$ and 19.2 times the calibrated 3.6 (verified, block C).
\end{enumerate}
(Source: \code{docs/19}, \code{.md} and \code{.tex}, Section~3;
\code{docs/23}, Section~8.1.) \note{Correction. \code{docs/19} and
\code{docs/23} compare the total shift $124\times10\times0.45 = 558$ with
the noise SD 8 (``about 70 to 1''). Almost all of that shift is the
biomarker-free drift, which the drug and time terms absorb and which
carries no interaction information; the part that the interaction term
sees is the per-SD moderation of 69.1, about 8.6 standard deviations of
$BR$ rather than 70, and it grows with $t_{od}$. The conclusion, a
grossly overpowered signal, stands. The source's shift of 0.65 in (ii)
uses the rounded profile 0.18.}

\textbf{The calibration check.} The adopted shift
$\beta_{bm}\sigma_{BR}b_i$ on drug matches the covariance-moderation
conditional mean $c_{bm}\sigma_{BR}b_i$ (Equation~\eqref{eq:archB-conditional})
when $\beta_{bm} = c_{bm}$, giving $0.45\times8 = 3.6$ per standard
deviation (\S\ref{sec:notation}, Rule 2). \code{docs/19}
(\code{.tex}, Section~4.1) checks this on 500 crossover participants by
regressing the mean on-drug $BR$ on the biomarker, and reports slopes
3.36 and 3.48 and residual SDs 5.23 and 3.89 (mean and covariance
moderation). For the mean $\overline{BR}_{i,\mathrm{on}}$ over the
$n_{\mathrm{on}} = 4$ on-drug visits of a crossover path at 2.5-week
spacing, with $A_{\mathrm{on}}$ their AR(1) correlation block
($\rho = 0.7$ per week, \code{buildSigma()}, inspected),
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Var}\big(\overline{BR}_{i,\mathrm{on}}\big) =
  \frac{\sigma_{BR}^2\,\mathbf 1^\top A_{\mathrm{on}}\mathbf 1}
       {n_{\mathrm{on}}^2},
\qquad
\sigma^2_{\mathrm{res}} = \begin{cases}
  \mathrm{Var}\big(\overline{BR}_{i,\mathrm{on}}\big) &
    \text{mean moderation}, \\
  \mathrm{Var}\big(\overline{BR}_{i,\mathrm{on}}\big)
    - c_{bm}^2\sigma_{BR}^2\,\bar g_{\mathrm{on}}^2 &
    \text{covariance moderation},
\end{cases}
\label{eq:inva-calib}
\end{equation}
with slope $c_{bm}\sigma_{BR} = \beta_{bm}\sigma_{BR}$ in both and
$\bar g_{\mathrm{on}} = 1$ (derived: the mean shift adds no residual
variance; the covariance coupling removes the explained part, as in
Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-tau}). The values are
$\sigma_{\mathrm{res}} = 5.39$ and $4.02$. A 2,000-replicate simulation
of the 500-participant regression gives sampling SDs of 0.25 and 0.18
for the slopes and 0.17 and 0.13 for the residual SDs, so all four
reported values lie within one sampling SD of
Equation~\eqref{eq:inva-calib} (verified, block D).
\note{Correction to \code{docs/23}, Section~8.2, which states that the
residual SDs could not be checked and that a regression of a single
$BR_{it}$ would give about 8 and 7.1. The \code{.tex} version of
\code{docs/19} specifies the regression as one of the mean on-drug
$BR$, and under that reading the reported values are reproduced. That
the source used this exact schedule and $\rho$ is inferred from the
match and from the package defaults, not from the source run, which was
not located.}

%=============================================================
\section{The dual-channel architecture}
\label{sec:dualchannel}
%=============================================================

Paper 11 activates the mean and covariance channels of
\S\ref{sec:architectures} simultaneously, with independent weights
$c_{bm,A}$ (mean channel) and $c_{bm,B}$ (covariance channel).

\subsection{Construction}

The covariance channel is built into the MVN correlation matrix
exactly as in Equation~\eqref{eq:archB}, with $c_{bm}$ replaced by
$c_{bm,B}$. The mean channel is then applied as a post-draw additive
shift at on-drug timepoints, Equation~\eqref{eq:archA} with the plain
profile and $\beta_{bm}$ replaced by $c_{bm,A}$:
\begin{equation}
\Delta BR_{it} = c_{bm,A}\, b_i\, \sigma_{BR}\,
  \mathbf{1}[D_{it} = 1], \qquad b_i =
  \frac{B_i - \mu_{bm}}{\sigma_{bm}}
\label{eq:dualchannel-shift}
\end{equation}
(\code{dgp\_architecture = 'combined'} in \code{R/generateData.R},
inspected). Paper 11 writes the standardized biomarker as $z_{B,i}$;
the canonical symbol is $b_i$. Note the asymmetry this construction
builds in: the covariance channel carries the graded off-drug coupling,
while the mean channel stops when the drug stops.

\subsection{The channels add on the covariance scale}

Each channel's on-drug contribution to $\mathrm{Cov}(B, BR)$ can be
computed separately:
\begin{align}
\text{Covariance channel alone:} \qquad &
  \mathrm{Cov}(B, BR \mid \text{on-drug}) = c_{bm,B}\,\sigma_{bm}\sigma_{BR}
  \label{eq:dc-covB} \\
\text{Mean channel alone:} \qquad &
  \mathrm{Cov}(B, \Delta BR) = c_{bm,A}\,\sigma_{bm}\sigma_{BR}
  \label{eq:dc-covA}
\end{align}
and, because the combined-architecture on-drug response is the sum of
the correlated draw and the mean shift, the two contributions add:
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Cov}(B, BR_C \mid \text{on-drug}) = (c_{bm,A} +
  c_{bm,B})\,\sigma_{bm}\sigma_{BR}.
\label{eq:dc-covC}
\end{equation}
To first order the residual variance is unchanged (the mean channel
adds $\sigma_{BR}^2(c_{bm,A}^2 + 2c_{bm,A}c_{bm,B})$ to the on-drug
variance, a second-order term; derived), so the interaction test's
noncentrality is additive across channels, $\Lambda_C \approx \Lambda_A
+ \Lambda_B$. Paper 11 then argues that power, as a function
$\pi(\Lambda)$ of noncentrality, is convex over the sub-ceiling regime,
which gives \emph{super}-additivity of power relative to the
probability-scale benchmark:
\begin{equation}
\pi(\Lambda_A + \Lambda_B) - \pi(0) \; > \;
  \big[\pi(\Lambda_A) - \pi(0)\big] + \big[\pi(\Lambda_B) - \pi(0)\big].
\label{eq:dc-superadditivity}
\end{equation}
At $(c_{bm,A}, c_{bm,B}) = (0.22, 0.22)$ in the Hybrid design, at the
matched total $N=70$, observed power is $0.577$ against an additive
benchmark $\hat\pi(0.22,0)+\hat\pi(0,0.22)-\hat\pi(0,0) =
0.214+0.138-0.040 = 0.312$, an excess of $0.265$, consistent with
Equation~\eqref{eq:dc-superadditivity} (Paper 11, Section 4.3;
simulated).

\note{2026-10-08: notation. Paper 11 writes the noncentrality as
$\eta$, which \code{NOTATION.md} reserves for expectancy; it is written
$\Lambda$ here, as in \code{docs/26}. Convexity of $\pi(\Lambda)$ holds
only below the inflection of the power curve; the statement is
argued in Paper 11, not derived for a specific test.}

\subsection{Boundary reductions and an identifiability consequence,
now stated}

Setting either weight to zero recovers the corresponding pure
architecture exactly, by direct substitution into
Equations~\eqref{eq:dualchannel-shift} and~\eqref{eq:archB}:
$c_{bm,B}=0$ recovers mean moderation; $c_{bm,A}=0$ recovers
covariance moderation. Paper 11 treats these boundaries as internal
validation gates: a correctly implemented combined driver must
reproduce the companion manuscripts' pure-architecture power values
there, run under matched design, sample-size, and calibration
settings. Equation~\eqref{eq:dc-covC} shows that the combined on-drug
covariance, and hence the fitted $bm{:}D_{bc}$ coefficient to first
order, depends on $c_{bm,A}$ and $c_{bm,B}$ only through their
\emph{sum}, so a single fitted interaction coefficient cannot
separately recover the two channel weights. Paper 11 now states this
non-identifiability explicitly in its \S3, after the additive-covariance
derivation. Off drug the two channels are not interchangeable, since
only the covariance channel carries graded coupling
(Equation~\eqref{eq:dualchannel-shift}); at $t_{1/2} > 0$ the sum is
therefore not the only quantity that matters (argued; not examined in
Paper 11).

\note{A second gap, investigated rather than left flagged.} Paper
11's driver (\code{analysis/scripts/dgp-combined/
01-run-combined-factorial.R}) already allocates $N$ as a total, via
the same \code{generate\_data\_multi\_path()} helper the companion
paper uses, and its stored output already includes the matched
$N=70$ cells, so the sample-size-convention explanation an earlier
version of \S4.2 gave for the boundary mismatch does not hold.
Reading the $N=70$, E2-specification boundary cells directly from
that stored output and comparing them to Paper 01's $N=70$ baselines
shows the two still do \emph{not} agree: at $t_{1/2}=0$ the
mean-moderation boundary reads $0.595$/$0.738$/$0.612$ (CO/Hybrid/
OL+BDC) against Paper 01's $0.739$/$0.842$/$0.751$, a gap of 10--19
percentage points, far beyond the $\approx0.013$--$0.016$ MCSE at
these cell sizes. The manuscript's \S4.2 has been rewritten to report
this finding rather than the superseded $N$-convention explanation,
and now names the likely locus of the mismatch (a calibration
difference in \texttt{resp\_param}, \texttt{baseline\_param}, or the
nuisance correlations \texttt{c.cf1t}/\texttt{c.cfct}/\texttt{rho}
between the two drivers) as an open item for a follow-up revision.
Paper 11's own $3\times3$ channel-weight power table has likewise been
recomputed on the $N=70$ data in that same output file, in place of
the earlier $N=35$ table; Paper 11 states that it should be treated as
not yet validated against the companion baselines.

\note{2026-10-08: the Paper 01 baselines quoted here (0.739, 0.842,
0.751 and 0.745, 0.840, 0.777 at $c_{bm} = 0.45$) are those of the
pre-restatement version of Paper 01. The restated Paper 01 does not report
them; it works at $c_{bm} = 0.25$ with the paired-difference statistic
and Hendrickson et al.'s binary-indicator model, so the boundary check
of Paper 11 now has no current reference values in Paper 01.}

\subsection{The dual channel conditional on the biomarker, and a
matched-total parameterization}
\label{sec:invd-dc-cond}

Let the covariance channel carry a coupling profile $g_t$
(Equation~\eqref{eq:cor-carryover}) with weight $c_{bm,B}$, and let the
mean channel add the on-drug shift of
Equation~\eqref{eq:dualchannel-shift} with weight $c_{bm,A}$, so that
$BR^{C}_{it} = BR_{it} + c_{bm,A}\, b_i\, \sigma_{BR}\, D_{it}$ with
$(B_i, BR_{i\cdot})$ jointly normal as in Equation~\eqref{eq:archB}.
Because the shift is a fixed linear function of $b_i$, conditioning on
the biomarker gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
E\big(BR^{C}_{it} \mid B_i\big) = \mu_{BR,t} +
  \big(c_{bm,A}\, D_{it} + c_{bm,B}\, g_t\big)\, \sigma_{BR}\, b_i, \\
\mathrm{Cov}\big(BR^{C}_{i\cdot} \mid B_i\big) = \Sigma_{BR} -
  c_{bm,B}^2\, \sigma_{BR}^2\, g g^{\top},
\end{gathered}
\label{eq:invd-dc-cond}
\end{equation}
the dual-channel counterpart of Equation~\eqref{eq:archB-conditional}
(derived; verified to $10^{-14}$ against the conditional-normal
formulas on Hybrid path 1 at $t_{1/2} = 1$ week, $\rho = 0.7$, and
consistent within Monte Carlo error with a $4 \times 10^5$-draw
simulation, \code{check-stress-test-construct.R}, Section 7). Three consequences
follow.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*), itemsep=2pt]
\item On drug the conditional slope is exactly $(c_{bm,A} +
  c_{bm,B})\sigma_{BR}$, so the additivity of
  Equation~\eqref{eq:dc-covC} holds exactly in the conditional mean,
  not only to first order. The conditional on-drug variance is
  $\sigma_{BR}^2(1 - c_{bm,B}^2)$ and does not involve $c_{bm,A}$:
  only the covariance channel reduces the residual variance, while the
  marginal variance $\sigma_{BR}^2(1 + c_{bm,A}^2 + 2c_{bm,A}c_{bm,B})$
  of \S\ref{sec:dualchannel} rises with the mean channel.
\item Off drug after exposure the conditional slope is
  $c_{bm,B}\, g_t\, \sigma_{BR}$: only the covariance channel carries
  carryover, which makes exact the statement of
  \S\ref{sec:dualchannel} that the sum $c_{bm,A} + c_{bm,B}$ is not the
  only quantity that matters at $t_{1/2} > 0$.
\item Positive definiteness constrains $c_{bm,B}$ alone, since the
  mean shift is applied after the draw: a cell $(c_{bm,A}, c_{bm,B})$
  is feasible exactly when $(0, c_{bm,B})$ is, that is when
  $c_{bm,B} < c_{bm}^{\ast}$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-ceiling}.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Matched total.} The original plan for the dual-channel
architecture proposes, besides the factorial grid, holding the total at
$0.45$ with a mixing weight (Source:
\code{docs/plan-}\hspace{0pt}\code{architecture-}\hspace{0pt}\code{c.md},
Parameterization). Writing that weight
$w_A \in [0,1]$,
\begin{equation}
c_{bm,A} = w_A\, c_{\mathrm{tot}}, \qquad
c_{bm,B} = (1 - w_A)\, c_{\mathrm{tot}}, \qquad c_{\mathrm{tot}} = 0.45,
\label{eq:invd-matched-total}
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:invd-dc-cond} gives an on-drug conditional slope of
$c_{\mathrm{tot}}\sigma_{BR}$ for every $w_A$, an off-drug slope
$(1 - w_A)c_{\mathrm{tot}}\,g_t\,\sigma_{BR}$, and a rank-one variance
reduction $(1 - w_A)^2 c_{\mathrm{tot}}^2 \sigma_{BR}^2 gg^{\top}$.
Without carryover ($g_t = D_{it}$) the conditional mean is the same
for every $w_A$, so the matched-total family fixes the on-drug
estimand of Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-estimand} and varies only the
residual covariance, which is smallest at $w_A = 0$; it is feasible
for $(1 - w_A)c_{\mathrm{tot}} < c_{bm}^{\ast}$ (derived).
\note{The plan writes the mixing weight as $\alpha$, the canonical
significance level; it is $w_A$ here. The plan's statement that the
$(0.45, 0.45)$ cell carries ``approximately double'' the interaction of
either pure architecture is exact on the conditional-mean scale, by
consequence (i). The matched-total family was not run in Paper~11.}

%=============================================================
\section{A latent-variable reading of covariance moderation}
\label{sec:latent}
%=============================================================

Paper 03 asks what mechanism a fitted covariance-moderation model
could be a moment-matched summary of, if the true underlying
process is a discrete responder/non-responder dichotomy rather than a
continuously varying correlation.

\subsection{The faithful latent-class data-generating process}

If a candidate predictive biomarker indexes an unobserved
responder/non-responder class,
\begin{equation}
Z_i \mid B_i \sim \mathrm{Bernoulli}\big(\pi(B_i)\big), \qquad
  BR_{it} \mid Z_i = z \sim f_z(t, D_{bc,it}),
\label{eq:latent-class}
\end{equation}
with $Z_i$ the unobserved class label and $\pi(B_i) = \Pr(Z_i=1\mid
B_i)$ the class-membership probability, now given an explicit closed
form in Paper 03's Notation section,
\begin{equation}
\pi(B_i) = \mathrm{expit}(\psi_0 + \psi_g\, b_i), \qquad b_i =
  \frac{B_i - \bar B}{s_B},
\label{eq:gating-logistic}
\end{equation}
with $\psi_g$ the \textbf{gating slope} (called \code{gs} in the
code, and now given the symbol $\psi_g$ in the manuscript rather than
left unnamed) and $\psi_0$ fixed so $\pi(0)=0.5$ throughout. $f_0,
f_1$ are class-conditional response distributions differing in
drug-response parameters. Within-class temporal structure follows the
same modified-Gompertz form of \S\ref{sec:gompertz}, with class-
specific parameters substituted.

\note{Notation caveat, resolved.} Paper 03 previously referred to
gating-function steepness only informally, as \code{gs} in prose and
code, with no manuscript symbol attached and no closed form for
$\pi(B_i)$ ever written out. This compendium originally flagged that
gap because a symbol $\gamma$ \emph{does} appear elsewhere in the
compendium (Paper 06, \S\ref{sec:partition}) as the unrelated $BR
\times PB$ subadditivity coefficient, inviting confusion between the
two. Paper 03 now introduces $\psi_g$ and
Equation~\eqref{eq:gating-logistic} explicitly, and its Notation
section states in so many words that $\psi_g$ is not to be confused
with Paper 06's $\gamma$. \code{NOTATION.md} has been updated to match:
$\gamma$ is now documented compendium-wide as Paper 06's coefficient
only, and $\psi_g$ is added as its own entry for Paper 03's gating
slope. The class-membership probability $\pi(B_i)$, and the marginal
class proportion $\pi$ below, are local to this section and are not the
power $\pi$ of \S\ref{sec:performance}; \code{NOTATION.md} uses the
same $\pi(B_i)$.

\subsection{The MVN approximation to mixture-induced moderation}

Under within-class independence between $B_i$ and $BR_{it}$, a two-
component mixture with class-dependent means and common within-class
covariance produces a marginal joint distribution of $(B_i, BR_{it})$
whose first two moments are reproduced, to leading order, by a single
MVN with $\mathrm{Cor}(B, BR) = c_{bm}$, where
\begin{equation}
c_{bm}^2 = \underbrace{\frac{\pi(1-\pi)(\mu_{bm}^1 - \mu_{bm}^0)^2}
  {\mathrm{Var}(B)}}_{f_B:\ \text{between-class fraction in } B} \; \cdot \;
  \underbrace{\frac{\pi(1-\pi)(\mu_{BR}^1 - \mu_{BR}^0)^2}
  {\mathrm{Var}(BR)}}_{f_{BR}:\ \text{between-class fraction in } BR}.
\label{eq:latent-mvn-approx}
\end{equation}
That is, $c_{bm}^2 = f_B f_{BR}$ is the \emph{product} of the two
between-class variance fractions, so under within-class independence
$|c_{bm}|$ equals their geometric mean $\sqrt{f_B f_{BR}}$ (derived:
the covariance is carried by class membership alone,
$\mathrm{Cov}(B, BR) = \pi(1-\pi)(\mu_{bm}^1-\mu_{bm}^0)(\mu_{BR}^1-\mu_{BR}^0)$).
If either class separation is small, the achievable $c_{bm}$ is small.
Paper 03 phrases this as a bound by ``the geometric mean of the two
intra-class correlations''; under its stated assumption the relation
is an equality. Under this reading the covariance-moderation
architecture is the second-moment signature of a latent-class mixture
whose class-conditional means separate on drug and whose separation is
eroded by carryover off drug, not necessarily a generative covariance
mechanism in its own right. The restated Paper 01 (Discussion) adopts
the same identity, $c_{bm}^2 = f_B f_{BR}$, and adds that the match is
limited to second moments: the mixture's conditional mean is nonlinear
in the biomarker, and its higher moments differ. The approximation
breaks down for strongly bimodal responses, a step-function (rather
than smooth) $\pi(B_i)$, or class-varying (rather than common)
within-class covariance.

\subsection{Estimation and its two competing test statistics}

The class-\emph{unaware} analysis is the compendium's common model,
Equation~\eqref{eq:common-analysis}. The class-\emph{aware} analysis
fits a two-class growth-mixture model (\code{lcmm::hlme}) and extracts
two candidate signals: a Wald test on the biomarker's coefficient in
the class-gating equation, and a within-class Wald test on
$bm{:}D_{bc}$. A model-comparison gate is applied before either is
trusted:
\begin{equation}
\text{prefer } ng=2 \text{ over } ng=1 \iff \Delta\mathrm{BIC} > 0
  \ \ (\text{or, more conservatively,} > 6),
\label{eq:bic-gate}
\end{equation}
following Raftery (1995), with the gating test interpreted only
conditional on that selection. In Paper 03's 240-replicate pilot at
$N=70$, the unconditional class-gating Wald test is markedly
anti-conservative under the null (Type~I error $0.197$, 95\% CI
$[0.145, 0.250]$), while the BIC-conditional version is
severely under-powered (Type~I error $0.004$; power $0.025$ at gating
slope $\psi_g = 1.0$ and $0.004$ at $\psi_g = 1.5$, against $0.325$ and
$0.542$ for the class-unaware $bm{:}D_{bc}$ Wald test on the same
cells). In this pilot the BIC step selects $ng=1$ in nearly every
replicate, so the conditional procedure approximates a never-reject
rule; the class-aware tests are dominated by the class-unaware one
(Paper 03, Pilot results). These are pilot estimates (MCSE of power
about 0.030); Paper 03's production program has not been run.

\note{2026-10-08: corrected. The power $0.025$ was previously attributed
to gating slope 1.5; Paper 03 reports it at 1.0, with $0.004$ at 1.5.
The previous summary, that the class-aware procedure ``buys correct
calibration at a substantial power cost'', was replaced by Paper 03's
own reading.}

%=============================================================
\section{Validity of covariance binding: the coupling ceiling and the
separable response covariance}
\label{sec:ce}
%=============================================================

The covariance-moderation architecture (Equation~\eqref{eq:archB})
writes the interaction into a correlation, and a correlation matrix
must be positive definite. This bounds the moderation parameter
$c_{bm}$ from above, by a ceiling that depends on the response
covariance, the visit schedule, and the coupling pattern. This section
states that ceiling exactly, evaluates it for the published
compound-symmetry construct of Hendrickson et al.\ and for the
separable AR(1) construct that Paper~01 now adopts as its reference
structure, and records why a generator must stop, rather than repair,
when a requested $c_{bm}$ exceeds it. Mean moderation
(Equation~\eqref{eq:archA}) writes nothing into the correlation matrix
and has no ceiling. Throughout, $c_1$ and $c_\times$ denote the
cross-component correlations at the same visit and at different visits
(code \code{c.cf1t}, \code{c.cfct}), and every numerical statement is
made at a single value of $\rho$. The compound-symmetry correlation is
$\rho_{cs}$ and the AR(1) correlation per week is $\rho$
(\code{NOTATION.md}); where a table or a sentence compares the two
constructs at a common value, as in Table~\ref{tab:ce-ceilings}, the
$\rho$ it states is $\rho_{cs}$ for the compound-symmetry rows and the
AR(1) $\rho$ for the others. \note{2026-10-09: notation. The
compound-symmetry correlation was written $\rho$; it is $\rho_{cs}$
throughout this document, as in \code{NOTATION.md}.}

\subsection{The joint correlation matrix and the exact ceiling}

For one participant on one randomization path with $n$ visits at weeks
$w_1 < \cdots < w_n$, the data-generating process draws
$(B_i, \mathrm{BL}_i, TV_{1:n}, PB_{1:n}, BR_{1:n})$ jointly from a
multivariate normal distribution. On the correlation scale,
\begin{equation}
R = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & c_{bm}\tilde g^{\top} \\ 0 & 1 & 0^{\top} \\
  c_{bm}\tilde g & 0 & M \end{pmatrix},
\qquad \tilde g = e_3 \otimes g,
\label{eq:ce-R}
\end{equation}
where $M$ is the $3n \times 3n$ response block in component-major
order $(TV, PB, BR)$, $e_3 = (0,0,1)^{\top}$ selects $BR$, and
$g \in \mathbb{R}^n$ is the coupling pattern. Under graded coupling,
Equation~\eqref{eq:cor-carryover} with exponential decay, $g_t = 1$ on
drug, $2^{-t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}} = e^{-\lambda t_{sd,t}}$ off drug after
first exposure and 0 before first exposure; the published step rule
replaces the middle case by
$\mathbf{1}[g_t > 0]$, which equals 1 at every post-exposure visit
whenever $t_{1/2} > 0$. Variances rescale $R$ to the covariance and do
not affect positive definiteness. By the Schur complement on the
biomarker row, $R \succ 0$ if and only if $M \succ 0$ and
$1 - c_{bm}^2\,\tilde g^{\top} M^{-1} \tilde g > 0$, so
\begin{equation}
c_{bm}^{\ast} = \bigl(\tilde g^{\top} M^{-1} \tilde g\bigr)^{-1/2}.
\label{eq:ce-ceiling}
\end{equation}
The quantity $c_{bm}^2\,\tilde g^{\top} M^{-1}\tilde g$ is the squared
multiple correlation of the biomarker on the whole response
trajectory, so the condition says only that the biomarker cannot be
more than perfectly predictable. The bound is attained: at
$c_{bm} = c_{bm}^{\ast}$ the smallest eigenvalue of $R$ is zero. A
design's ceiling is the minimum over its paths, since all paths share
one $c_{bm}$. (Derived. Source: \code{docs/34}, Section 3.2;
\code{docs/36}, Section 4.2; Paper 01, Methods and Appendix A.4.)
\note{The sources write the padded pattern as $u$ (Paper 01,
\code{docs/36}) or $v$ (\code{docs/34}, \code{docs/32}); $\tilde g$ is
used here because $u_i$ is the canonical random intercept.}

A matrix can therefore fail in two distinct ways: the response block
$M$ is itself indefinite, which no choice of $c_{bm}$ repairs, or $M$
is valid and the coupling exceeds what it supports. On every grid of
\code{docs/36} (Section 6.5) only the second occurs.

\subsection{The published compound-symmetry construct}
\label{sec:ce-cs}

Hendrickson et al.'s construct (commit \code{58b32a9}) sets every
within-component correlation to $\rho_{cs}$ and the cross-component
correlations to $c_1$ and $c_\times$. It decomposes exactly as
\begin{equation}
M_{\mathrm{CS}} = K_o \otimes I_n + K_p \otimes J_n, \qquad
K_p = \rho_{cs} I_3 + c_\times (J_3 - I_3), \qquad
K_o = (1-\rho_{cs}) I_3 + (c_1 - c_\times)(J_3 - I_3),
\label{eq:ce-cs-decomp}
\end{equation}
a person-level term plus an occasion-level term. Since $J_n$ has
eigenvalues $n$ and 0, $M_{\mathrm{CS}} \succ 0$ if and only if
$K_o \succ 0$ and $K_o + nK_p \succ 0$, which with $K_p \succeq 0$
reduces to $(1 - \rho_{cs}) - (c_1 - c_\times) > 0$, independent of $n$ and
of the schedule. At the published values $\rho_{cs} = 0.8$, $c_1 = 0.2$,
$c_\times = 0.1$ this smallest eigenvalue is 0.100 for $n = 4$ to 32.
(Derived; verified, \code{check-ceilings.R}. Source: \code{docs/35},
Section 4.5; Paper 01, Appendix A.5.)

Writing $P_1 = n^{-1}J_n$, $M_{\mathrm{CS}} = K_o \otimes (I_n - P_1) +
(K_o + nK_p) \otimes P_1$, and the two projections invert separately.
With $a = [K_o^{-1}]_{33}$, $a_n = [(K_o + nK_p)^{-1}]_{33}$ and
$\bar g$ the mean of $g$,
\begin{equation}
\frac{1}{c_{bm}^{\ast\,2}} = a \sum_{t=1}^{n} (g_t - \bar g)^2 +
  a_n\, n\, \bar g^{2}
\;\overset{g \in \{0,1\}^n}{=}\;
a\,\frac{n_{\mathrm{on}}(n - n_{\mathrm{on}})}{n} +
  a_n\,\frac{n_{\mathrm{on}}^2}{n},
\label{eq:ce-cs-closed}
\end{equation}
where $n_{\mathrm{on}}$ counts visits with $g_t = 1$. (Derived here;
the binary form is \code{docs/34}, Section 5.1, and agrees with the
full matrix to machine precision, verified.) Three consequences follow.

\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Schedule features are irrelevant; visit counts are
  not.} For a binary pattern only $n$, $n_{\mathrm{on}}$, $\rho_{cs}$,
  $c_1$ and $c_\times$ enter: spacing, the gap at a switch, the number
  of switches and their order do not. The ceiling falls with the
  number of visits at a fixed on-drug share, roughly as $n^{-1/2}$.
  With half the visits on drug, at $\rho_{cs} = 0.8$ it is 0.358, 0.256,
  0.182 and 0.129 for $n = 4$, 8, 16 and 32 (verified,
  \code{check-ceilings.R}). At $\rho_{cs} = 0.7$ it is 0.480, 0.346, 0.247
  and 0.176 (\code{docs/32}, Section 8; verified). The loss comes
  through the coupling vector, not through the response block, whose
  smallest eigenvalue does not depend on $n$.
\item \textbf{The published values are infeasible without carryover.}
  At $\rho_{cs} = 0.8$ and $t_{1/2} = 0$ the ceiling is 0.256 in Hybrid
  (paths with $n_{\mathrm{on}} = 4$ of 8) and CO, and 0.262 in OL+BDC
  (verified). Both published effect sizes, $c_{bm} = 0.3$ and 0.6,
  exceed it; \code{docs/36} (Section 4.1) reports that 40 of the 162
  published design-path-cell matrices fail the code's own check, all
  through the biomarker row.
\item \textbf{The high ceilings under carryover carry no signal.} With
  step coupling and any $t_{1/2} > 0$, every Hybrid and OL+BDC path,
  all of which begin on drug, has $g = \mathbf 1$, so
  $c_{bm}^{\ast} = (n\,a_n)^{-1/2} = 0.893$ at $\rho_{cs} = 0.8$, $n = 8$
  (verified). A constant pattern has no on/off contrast, so the cells
  that become feasible are exactly the cells in which the interaction
  has no population counterpart. CO stays at 0.256, because its
  placebo-first path keeps $g_t = 0$ before exposure. (Source:
  \code{docs/36}, Sections 4.2 and 5.3; \code{docs/32}, Section 3.3.)
\end{enumerate}

Under graded coupling (Equation~\eqref{eq:cor-carryover}) and compound
symmetry at $\rho_{cs} = 0.8$, the Hybrid ceiling rises only slowly with the
half-life: 0.256, 0.257, 0.307 and 0.369 at $t_{1/2} = 0.1$, 0.2, 1
and 2 weeks (verified, \code{check-ceilings-graded.R}; Paper 01, Appendix A.5;
\code{docs/36}, Section 6.5). \note{Paper 01, Appendix A.5, places this
compound-symmetry result under its separable-structure heading and
attributes it to the lower on-to-off step cost, which is an AR(1)
quantity (Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-steps}). Under compound symmetry the
operative mechanism is Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-cs-closed}: graded
coupling reduces the dispersion $\sum_t (g_t - \bar g)^2$ that carries
the larger weight $a$.}

\subsection{The separable response covariance}
\label{sec:ce-sep}

Let $A_{ts} = \rho^{|w_t - w_s|}$ (AR(1) in calendar weeks) and
$K = (1 - c_1) I_3 + c_1 J_3$, and set
\begin{equation}
M = K \otimes A, \qquad
\mathrm{Cor}(X_{c,t}, X_{c',s}) = K_{cc'}\, A_{ts},
\label{eq:ce-sep}
\end{equation}
for components $c, c' \in \{TV, PB, BR\}$. Lagged cross-component
correlation is then $c_1 \rho^{|w_t - w_s|}$ and $c_\times$ is
retired. The eigenvalues of $K \otimes A$ are the products of those of
$K$ and $A$. $K$ has eigenvalues $1 + 2c_1$ (once) and $1 - c_1$
(twice), so $K \succ 0$ for $-\tfrac12 < c_1 < 1$; $A$ is the
correlation matrix of a stationary Ornstein--Uhlenbeck process at the
visit times and is positive definite for any distinct times. Hence
$M \succ 0$ for every schedule. Generatively,
$\mathbf X = L_A \mathbf Z L_K^{\top}$ with $L_A L_A^{\top} = A$,
$L_K L_K^{\top} = K$ and $\mathbf Z$ an $n \times 3$ standard normal
matrix: the components are fixed mixtures of three independent AR(1)
paths on one clock. (Derived. Source: \code{docs/35}, Section 4.2;
Paper 01, Appendix A.5.)

The non-separable AR(1) construct in the package (\code{covar},
\code{buildSigma()}) decomposes as
$M_{\mathrm{covar}} = K_a \otimes A + K_b \otimes I_n$ with
$K_a = (1 - c_\times)I_3 + c_\times J_3$ and
$K_b = (c_1 - c_\times)(J_3 - I_3)$. $K_b$ has zero diagonal and is
indefinite: a same-visit cross-component excess attached to no
variance. Diagonalizing $A$ gives
\begin{equation}
M_{\mathrm{covar}} \succ 0 \iff
\omega_{\min}(A) > \frac{c_1 - c_\times}{1 - c_\times},
\label{eq:ce-covar-pd}
\end{equation}
with $\omega_{\min}(A)$ the smallest eigenvalue of $A$, which falls as
visits become closer or more numerous; at $\rho = 0.7$ and
$(c_1, c_\times) = (0.2, 0.1)$ the condition fails for half-week
spacing. (Derived, and verified to $10^{-15}$ in \code{docs/35},
Section 4.4.) \note{Paper 01, Appendix A.5, writes the eigenvalues of
$A$ as $\lambda_j(A)$; $\omega$ is used here because $\lambda$ is the
canonical carryover decay rate.} The published construct
(Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-cs-decomp}) is a single separable term,
$K \otimes A_{\mathrm{CS}}$, only when $c_\times = c_1\rho_{cs}$
(\code{docs/35}, Section 4.5).

\subsection{The factorized ceiling and the per-step costs}
\label{sec:ce-factor}

Since $M^{-1} = K^{-1} \otimes A^{-1}$, the quadratic form in
Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-ceiling} factorizes, and the equicorrelated $K$
has a closed-form inverse:
\[
\tilde g^{\top} M^{-1} \tilde g = [K^{-1}]_{33}\, g^{\top} A^{-1} g,
\qquad
K^{-1} = (1 - c_1)^{-1}\Bigl(I_3 - \frac{c_1}{1 + 2c_1} J_3\Bigr),
\]
so that
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
c_{bm}^{\ast} = \sqrt{1 - R^2}\;\bigl(g^{\top} A^{-1} g\bigr)^{-1/2},
\\
[K^{-1}]_{33} = \frac{1 + c_1}{(1 - c_1)(1 + 2c_1)} = \frac{1}{1 - R^2},
\qquad R^2 = \frac{2c_1^2}{1 + c_1},
\end{gathered}
\label{eq:ce-factor}
\end{equation}
where $R^2$ is the squared multiple correlation of $BR$ on $TV$ and
$PB$ at one visit: 0.0182, 0.0667 and 0.1385 at $c_1 = 0.1$, 0.2, 0.3
(derived; verified, \code{check-ceilings.R}). The second identity
follows from $(1 - c_1)(1 + 2c_1) = (1 + c_1)(1 - R^2)$.

The sampled Ornstein--Uhlenbeck process is Markov,
$X_t = \rho_t X_{t-1} + \sqrt{1 - \rho_t^2}\,\xi_t$ with step
correlation $\rho_t = \rho^{d_t}$, gap $d_t = w_t - w_{t-1}$ and
independent standard normal innovations $\xi_t$. Hence
$A^{-1} = L^{\top}L$ with $L$ lower bidiagonal ($L_{11} = 1$,
$L_{tt} = (1-\rho_t^2)^{-1/2}$, $L_{t,t-1} = -\rho_t(1-\rho_t^2)^{-1/2}$),
and
\begin{equation}
g^{\top} A^{-1} g = g_1^2 + \sum_{t=2}^{n}
  \frac{(g_t - \rho_t\, g_{t-1})^2}{1 - \rho_t^2},
\label{eq:ce-steps}
\end{equation}
a sum of standardized one-step prediction errors of the coupling
pattern. For a binary pattern the step from visit $t-1$ to $t$ costs
$(1 - \rho_t)/(1 + \rho_t)$ on to on, $\rho_t^2/(1 - \rho_t^2)$ on to
off, $1/(1 - \rho_t^2)$ off to on, and 0 off to off; all four were
checked against a direct inverse (verified). Abrupt switches between
closely spaced visits are expensive, which is why designs with many
on/off switches carry only small effects. For the constant pattern,
$g^{\top}A^{-1}g = 1 + \sum_t (1 - \rho_t)/(1 + \rho_t)$, an effective
number of independent visits, and the ceiling is
$\sqrt{(1 - R^2)/n_{\mathrm{eff}}}$. Under graded coupling the first
off-drug step costs $(g_t - \rho_t)^2/(1 - \rho_t^2)$, smaller than
the binary on-to-off cost when $g_t$ is near $\rho_t$, so the ceiling
rises with the half-life. (Derived. Source: \code{docs/35}, Sections
4.3 and 6; Paper 01, Appendix A.5.) \note{\code{docs/35} writes the
step correlation as $\phi_t$; $\rho_t$ is used here because $\phi$
denotes the carryover decay family (Equation~\eqref{eq:cor-carryover}).
\code{docs/35} also records that \code{docs/34}, Section 5.5, assigned
every switch the off-to-on cost; an on-to-off switch costs exactly one
unit less, as Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-steps} gives.}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering\small

symmetry (the published construct); F: the package's non-separable
