\noindent The results, with how each was established:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*), itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{AR(1) is the change that matters, but it is not better
  than \code{covar}.} Every configuration with \code{ar1} on reaches
  at least 0.438, and every one with it off is at most 0.346. The
  source's original claim that AR(1) alone (0.48) beats the full set
  (0.45) holds only with the first visit uncoupled. With $g_1$
  coupled, AR(1) alone reaches 0.4379 against \code{covar}'s 0.4522
  (worst cell, both in OL+BDC at $t_{1/2} = 0$), and 0.4521 against
  0.4562 in Hybrid (verified; this confirms the 2026-10-07 correction
  of the source and \code{docs/52}).
\item \textbf{Why an uncoupled first visit raises the ceiling.}
  Setting an on-drug $g_1$ to zero removes a term from
  $\tilde g^{\top}M^{-1}\tilde g$ in Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-ceiling};
  under AR(1) it removes the $g_1^2$ term of
  Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-steps} and lowers the first step's cost, so the
  quadratic form falls and the ceiling rises (derived). The gain is
  feasibility for a different model, one with less coupling, not
  additional range for the stated one.
\item \textbf{Under compound symmetry the first visit does not move the
  worst cell.} For a binary pattern the ceiling depends only on $n$
  and $n_{\mathrm{on}}$ (Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-cs-closed}). The binding
  path is CO's placebo-first path, at 0.3460 for every $t_{1/2}$,
  whose first visit is before exposure and so uncoupled under every
  rule; the drug-first path is at 0.360 to 0.363 (verified; the source
  states the binding path as inferred).
\item \textbf{The decaying cross-component block lowers the ceiling
  under compound symmetry} (0.346 to 0.165, or 0.151 with $g_1$
  coupled) and changes it by $-0.001$ to $+0.014$ under AR(1). The
  response block is positive definite in every configuration
  (smallest eigenvalues 0.015 to 0.331 over designs), so the
  biomarker row is always the binding constraint (verified).
\item \textbf{The 2024 construct's exact ceilings} at $\rho = 0.7$ are
  0.346 without carryover and in CO, 0.351 at $t_{1/2} = 0.5$ in Hybrid
  and OL+BDC, and 0.368 (Hybrid) and 0.370 (OL+BDC) at
  $t_{1/2} = 1$ (verified; the first two are quoted in Section 4.2 of
  the source).
\end{enumerate}
\note{``AR(1) alone'' names two different constructs in the sources:
in \code{docs/36} and \code{docs/44} (configuration B) it keeps the
decaying cross-component block, and in \code{docs/54} it keeps
the constant block. At $\rho = 0.7$, no carryover and the first visit
coupled, they give 0.452 and 0.438 in OL+BDC.}

%=============================================================
\section{A pharmacodynamic sensitivity model}
\label{sec:pd-model}
%=============================================================

The constructs of \S\ref{sec:architectures} specify carryover twice:
once for the off-drug mean and once, through a separate rule, for the
biomarker correlation (Equation~\eqref{eq:cor-carryover}). The
proposal of \code{docs/38} specifies it once, in a generative model of
each participant's drug response, from which the mean, the moderation
profile and the covariance all follow. Every result in this section
is derived; none has been implemented in \code{R/generateData.R} or
simulated in the compendium. (Source: \code{docs/38}, Sections 1--6;
\code{docs/44}, Step~8; \code{docs/51}, Features 2--5 and 8.) The
symbols $\omega_i$, $\zeta$, $v_i$ and $\xi_{it}$ are local; they
replace the $\theta$, $\kappa$, $u$ and $\varepsilon$ of the first
draft of \code{docs/38}, which collide with canonical symbols.
\code{docs/51} writes the serial noise as $\varepsilon_{it}$ and its
SD as $\sigma_\varepsilon$; the $\xi_{it}$ of \code{docs/38} is used
here.

\subsection{The drug-response time course: a turnover model}

For a weekly symptom outcome, pharmacokinetic carryover is negligible
for prazosin (plasma half-life about 2.3 hours, Hobbs et al., 1978),
so the half-life that matters is that of the response, written
$t_{1/2}^{R}$ to distinguish it from the $t_{1/2}$ of the published
construct. Let $h(t) \in [0,1]$ be the population drug-response time
course, scaled to 1 at full on-drug effect, and let $h^{\ast}(t)$ be
the drug-driven target, 1 on drug and 0 off drug (written $E(t)$ in
\code{docs/51}). The turnover, or indirect-response, model (Dayneka
et al., 1993) is
\begin{equation}
\frac{dh}{dt} = k_{\text{out}}\big(h^{\ast}(t) - h(t)\big), \qquad
k_{\text{out}} = \frac{\ln 2}{t_{1/2}^{R}}.
\label{eq:pd-turnover}
\end{equation}
For a target constant within each on-drug or off-drug interval the
solution is exact and piecewise. Off drug, from the level
$h(t_{\text{stop}})$ reached at discontinuation,
\begin{equation}
h(t) = h(t_{\text{stop}})\, 2^{-t_{sd}/t_{1/2}^{R}},
\label{eq:pd-offset}
\end{equation}
and on drug, from a starting level $h_0$ at time $t_{\text{in}}$ into the
interval,
\begin{equation}
h(t) = 1 - (1 - h_0)\, 2^{-t_{\text{in}}/t_{1/2}^{R}} .
\label{eq:pd-onset}
\end{equation}
\note{2026-10-09: notation. The drug-response time course was written
$f(t)$, with $f_0$, $f_{\text{res}}$ and $f^{\ast}$; it is $h(t)$,
$h_0$, $h_{\text{res}}$, $h^{\ast}$, as in \code{docs/44} and
\code{docs/51}, because $f$ is the canonical absorbed share
(\S\ref{sec:bp-prog}). $t_{\text{in}}$ (written $\tau_{od}$) is the
time into the current on-drug interval.}
Both follow from the linear first-order equation with constant forcing
(derived; the closed form agrees with an Euler solution of
Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-turnover} to $1.2\times10^{-5}$ on a six-week
on, four-week off schedule, \code{check-pd-features.R}). Three properties
follow, each of which differs from the published process.
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Anchored decay.} Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-offset} decays
  from the level at discontinuation. The published recursion (compare
  Equation~\eqref{eq:carryover-recursion}) multiplies the previous
  visit's already-decayed mean by $2^{-t_{sd}/t_{1/2}}$ with $t_{sd}$
  cumulative, so at off-drug visits one and two weeks after stopping
  the second sits at $2^{-(1+2)/t_{1/2}}$ rather than $2^{-2/t_{1/2}}$
  (derived; \code{docs/37}, Appendix~A).
\item \textbf{Restart from the residual.} On re-exposure,
  Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-onset} applies with $h_0 = h_{\text{res}}$,
  the residual level. The package instead resets $t_{od}$ to zero:
  under the modified Gompertz curve (Equation~\eqref{eq:gompertz-05},
  $m = 10.99$, $d = 5$, $r = 0.42$)
  a participant near full response at week~10 of the Hybrid design is
  credited with $G(4) = 4.28$ of $10.99$ (39\%) at week~16
  (\code{docs/51}, Feature~4; the value $G(4)$ verified in
  \code{check-pd-features.R}, the reset to $t_{od} = 4$ at week~16 taken
  from the source, not rechecked against the design). Anchored offset
  combined with a zero restart can step the mean down at re-exposure.
\item \textbf{One rate for onset and offset.} The turnover onset is
  exponential, not sigmoid; a sigmoid onset arises only if the target
  $h^{\ast}$ itself rises with titration. The Gompertz landmarks it
  replaces are an inflection at $\ln(d)/r =
  3.8$ weeks, half the maximum at 4.7 weeks and 90\% at 9.2 weeks
  (verified, \code{check-pd-features.R}; \code{docs/51}, Feature~2).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Sensitivity binding of the biomarker}

A predictive biomarker acts on drug sensitivity. Write participant
$i$'s drug response as
\begin{equation}
BR_{it} = \omega_i\, h(t) + \xi_{it}, \qquad
\omega_i = \bar\omega\,(1 + \zeta\, b_i) + v_i,
\label{eq:pd-sensitivity}
\end{equation}
with $b_i$ the standardized biomarker, $\zeta$ the strength of
multiplicative moderation, $v_i \sim \mathcal N(0, \sigma_v^2)$ the sensitivity
variation the biomarker does not explain, and $\xi_{it}$ serial noise
with SD $\sigma_\xi$ and correlation $r_\xi(t,s)$, independent of $b_i$
and $v_i$. The mean response is $\bar\omega h(t)$ and the
biomarker-dependent part, $\bar\omega\zeta b_i h(t)$, has the time
course of the response. The \emph{coupling profile} (the fraction of
the full moderation present at visit $t$, $g_t$ in \code{docs/37}) is
therefore
\begin{equation}
g_t = h(t):
\label{eq:pd-profile}
\end{equation}
small early in titration, rising with the response on drug, and
decaying with it off drug. It is neither the published step rule
(full moderation wherever any residual mean remains) nor the
on-drug-only shift of the mean-moderation construct. The second
moments follow (derived; \code{docs/38}, Section~5):
\begin{align}
\mathrm{Var}(BR_t) &= H\, h(t)^2 + \sigma_\xi^2, \qquad
  H = \bar\omega^2\zeta^2 + \sigma_v^2, \label{eq:pd-var}\\
\mathrm{Cov}(BR_t, BR_s) &= H\, h(t) h(s) + \sigma_\xi^2\, r_\xi(t,s),
  \label{eq:pd-cov}\\
\mathrm{Cor}(B, BR_t) &= \frac{\bar\omega\zeta\, h(t)}
  {\sqrt{H h(t)^2 + \sigma_\xi^2}} . \label{eq:pd-cor}
\end{align}
The covariance is a rank-one positive semidefinite term plus a
positive definite noise covariance, so it is valid for every design,
schedule and half-life; correlations are outputs, and there is no
ceiling on the effect size. The drug-response variance shrinks to
$\sigma_\xi^2$ as the effect washes out, whereas every current DGP
keeps the full $\sigma_{BR}$ (8 in Hendrickson et al.) at off-drug
visits with zero mean (\code{docs/51}, Feature~8). The published
$c_{bm}$ becomes a derived quantity, most naturally the correlation at
full effect,
\begin{equation}
c_{bm}^{\text{peak}} = \frac{\bar\omega\zeta}
  {\sqrt{H + \sigma_\xi^2}} .
\label{eq:pd-cpeak}
\end{equation}
Equations~\eqref{eq:pd-var}--\eqref{eq:pd-cor} were confirmed by
simulation of Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-sensitivity} with $4\times10^5$
participants ($\bar\omega = 10$, $\zeta = 0.3$, $\sigma_v = 2$,
$\sigma_\xi = 3$, $r_\xi = 0.5^{|t-s|}$; simulated
$\mathrm{Cor}(B, BR_6) = 0.635$ against $0.635$;
\code{check-pd-features.R}).

\subsection{Consequences for the interaction test}

With the paired-difference statistic E9 (Equation~\eqref{eq:e9-diffs})
written as $\Delta_i = \sum_t a_t Y_{it}$, contrast weights
$a_t = 1/n^{\text{on}}$ on drug and $-1/n^{\text{off}}$ off drug,
define the contrast-weighted exposure
\begin{equation}
F = \sum_t a_t h(t) = \bar h_{\text{on}} - \bar h_{\text{off}} .
\label{eq:pd-F}
\end{equation}
Because $Y = \mathrm{BL} - (TV + PB + BR)$, the drug response enters
$\Delta_i$ as $-\omega_i F - \sum_t a_t \xi_{it}$. Writing $V_0$ for
the variance contributed by the serial noise and the other components,
assumed independent of $\omega_i$,
\begin{equation}
\beta_{bm:D} = -\frac{\bar\omega\zeta\, F}{\sigma_{bm}}, \qquad
\mathrm{Var}(\Delta) = H F^2 + V_0, \qquad
r_{\Delta B} = -\frac{\bar\omega\zeta\, F}{\sqrt{H F^2 + V_0}}
\label{eq:pd-slope}
\end{equation}
(derived; \code{docs/38}, Section~6, which writes $\rho_{\Delta B}$;
$r_{\Delta B}$ is used here, as in \S\ref{sec:cg}, because $\rho$ is
the AR(1) parameter). In the simulation above
($F = 0.605$) the simulated slope, variance and correlation were
$-0.907$, $18.19$ and $-0.426$ against $-0.908$, $18.20$ and $-0.426$
(verified, \code{check-pd-features.R}). \note{\code{docs/38}'s first draft
gave $r_{\Delta B}$ without the minus sign; the sign follows from
that of $\beta_{bm:D}$ and was corrected in the source on 2026-10-07.
Power depends only on $|r_{\Delta B}|$.}

\textbf{Power is monotone in $|F|$.} $|r_{\Delta B}| =
\bar\omega\zeta|F|/\sqrt{HF^2 + V_0}$ has derivative
$\bar\omega\zeta V_0/(HF^2 + V_0)^{3/2} > 0$ in $|F|$ for $V_0 > 0$,
and power is increasing in $|r_{\Delta B}|$ (\code{docs/37},
Section~4.4), so power is strictly increasing in $|F|$ (derived).
Under anchored offset with the on-drug course held fixed, each
off-drug $h(t)$ increases with $t_{1/2}^{R}$, so power falls
monotonically with the response half-life. Under the full turnover
model a longer $t_{1/2}^{R}$ also slows onset and raises the restart
level, so $F$ need not be monotone in $t_{1/2}^{R}$ and must be
checked per design. Unlike the published process, $\mathrm{Var}
(\Delta)$ now depends on carryover through $HF^2$.

\textbf{Reduction to graded coupling.} Suppose $h(t) = 1$ at every
on-drug visit and at discontinuation, and Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-offset}
holds off drug with $t_{1/2}^{R} = t_{1/2}$. Then $g_t$ of
Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-profile} is the graded coupling profile of
\code{docs/37} (1 on drug, $2^{-t_{sd}/t_{1/2}}$ off drug),
$F = 1 - \bar g_{\text{off}}$ is its coupling gap, and
Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-slope} becomes
\begin{equation}
\beta_{bm:D} = -\frac{\bar\omega\zeta}{\sigma_{bm}}
  \big(1 - \bar g_{\text{off}}\big)
  = -c_{bm}\frac{\sigma_{BR}}{\sigma_{bm}}\big(1 - \bar g_{\text{off}}\big),
\label{eq:pd-graded}
\end{equation}
the coupling-gap result of \code{docs/37}, with the identification
$c_{bm}\sigma_{BR} = \bar\omega\zeta$. This identification is exact
when $\sigma_{BR}$ is read as the full-effect SD
$\sqrt{H + \sigma_\xi^2}$, by Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-cpeak} (derived).
The two models still differ in the variance: the graded construct
keeps $\mathrm{Var}(BR_t)$ constant, while
Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-var} lets it shrink off drug. \note{Under the
pure turnover model, onset and offset share $k_{\text{out}}$, so
$h = 1$ at every on-drug visit together with slow offset is not
attainable; the reduction is exact for a construct with saturated
onset and anchored turnover offset, and approximate otherwise. The
mapping of \code{docs/37}'s $(1 - \bar g_{\text{off}})$ onto $F$ is
derived here, not stated in \code{docs/38} beyond its remark that $F$
replaces the coupling gap.}

\textbf{The power cap.} For every $F$,
\begin{equation}
|r_{\Delta B}| < \frac{\bar\omega\zeta}{\sqrt{\bar\omega^2\zeta^2
  + \sigma_v^2}},
\label{eq:pd-cap}
\end{equation}
the square root of the share of sensitivity variation that the
biomarker explains, approached as $HF^2$ dominates $V_0$. Since
$f \in [0,1]$ implies $|F| \le 1$, the attainable bound is the lower
value $\bar\omega\zeta/\sqrt{H + V_0}$ (in the simulation above,
$0.832$ and $0.583$ respectively). No design and no exposure contrast
can exceed Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-cap}; it is a property of the
biology encoded by $\sigma_v$, not of the simulation (derived;
\code{docs/38}, Section~6).

\subsection{Sensitivity binding against the other encodings}
\label{sec:inve-pd-relation}

Sensitivity binding (Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-sensitivity}) can be read in
the terms of both other encodings. Write $G = \bar\omega\zeta$,
$H = G^2 + \sigma_v^2$, $\Sigma_\xi$ for the covariance of the serial
noise ($\sigma_\xi^2 r_\xi(t,s)$, positive definite), and $h$ for the
vector of $h(t)$ over the visits.

\textbf{Conditional on the biomarker it is mean binding plus a random
slope.} Given $b_i$,
\begin{equation}
E(BR_{it} \mid b_i) = \bar\omega h(t) + G\, b_i\, h(t), \qquad
\mathrm{Cov}(BR_{i\cdot} \mid b_i) = \sigma_v^2\, h h^\top + \Sigma_\xi
\label{eq:inve-pd-cond}
\end{equation}
(derived; verified by simulation of $4\times10^5$ participants at
$\bar\omega = 10$, $\zeta = 0.3$, $\sigma_v = 2$, $\sigma_\xi = 3$,
$r_\xi = 0.5^{|t-s|}$ and an arbitrary profile $h$: conditional slope
$3.00$ against $Gf_4 = 3$, conditional covariance within $0.044$ of
Equation~\eqref{eq:inve-pd-cond} on entries of order $10$, within
sampling error). This is mean binding (Equation~\eqref{eq:archA}) with
profile $h_t = h(t)$ and strength $\beta_{bm} = G/\sigma_{BR}$, with one
addition: the unexplained sensitivity $v_i$ contributes the rank-one
term $\sigma_v^2 hh^\top$, a random slope on the exposure profile. With
$\sigma_v = 0$ it is exactly mean binding with profile $h$.

\textbf{Unconditionally it is covariance binding with an implied
coupling.} The joint law of $(B_i, BR_{i\cdot})$ is normal with
$\mathrm{Cov}(B, BR_t) = \sigma_{bm}Gh(t)$ and
$\mathrm{Cov}(BR_{i\cdot}) = \Sigma_{BR} = Hhh^\top + \Sigma_\xi$
(Equations~\eqref{eq:pd-var}--\eqref{eq:pd-cor}), so it is a covariance
construct whose coupling pattern, relative to the full-effect
correlation of Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-cpeak}, is
\begin{equation}
\frac{\mathrm{Cor}(B, BR_t)}{c_{bm}^{\mathrm{peak}}}
= h(t)\sqrt{\frac{H + \sigma_\xi^2}{Hh(t)^2 + \sigma_\xi^2}}
\;\ge h(t),
\label{eq:inve-pd-coupling}
\end{equation}
equal to $h(t)$ only at $h = 0$ and $h = 1$ (derived). The implied
correlation profile is therefore not proportional to the response,
because the drug-response SD shrinks with it; in the simulation above,
$h = 0.5$ gives a relative coupling of $0.670$ (verified). The
covariance encoding of Section~\ref{sec:cov-carryover} holds the SD at
$\sigma_{BR}$ and the coupling at $g_t$; the two coincide only in the
reduction of Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-graded}, where the comparison is made
on the slope scale.

\textbf{The Schur-complement ceiling is never reached.} The squared
multiple correlation of the biomarker on the drug-response trajectory
is, by the Sherman--Morrison formula with
$\mathcal Q_\xi = h^\top\Sigma_\xi^{-1}h$,
\begin{equation}
R^2_{B\mid BR} = G^2 h^\top\big(Hhh^\top + \Sigma_\xi\big)^{-1}h
= \frac{G^2 \mathcal Q_\xi}{1 + H\mathcal Q_\xi} < \frac{G^2}{H}
= \frac{\bar\omega^2\zeta^2}{\bar\omega^2\zeta^2 + \sigma_v^2} \le 1
\label{eq:inve-pd-r2}
\end{equation}
(derived; $0.5280$ both ways in the simulation's parameters, against
the bound $0.6923$, verified). The Schur-complement condition that
defines the ceiling in Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-ceiling},
$1 - R^2_{B\mid BR} > 0$, holds for every schedule, profile and
strength, strictly even when $\sigma_v = 0$ because $\mathcal Q_\xi$ is finite.
Under sensitivity binding the ceiling condition is a consequence of the
model, not a constraint on its parameters. The bound $G^2/H$ is the
square of the power cap of Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-cap}: the share of
sensitivity variation that the biomarker explains bounds both how well
the biomarker can be predicted from the response trajectory and how
strongly it can correlate with any contrast of it. If the other
response components are independent of $\omega_i$ and $b_i$, as
assumed in Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-slope}, adding them leaves
$R^2_{B\mid BR}$ unchanged; correlated serial noise across components
changes $\mathcal Q_\xi$ but not the bound (derived).

\textbf{Feature comparison.} Table~\ref{tab:inve-pd-compare} sets the
proposal against the constructs in use (Source: \code{docs/38}, Section
7; argued, with the \code{covar} mean and restart entries inspected in
the package source by the source authors).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering\small
\caption{Constructs compared (Source: \code{docs/38}, Section 7).
\code{orig}: published \code{58b32a9}; C: graded separable
(\code{docs/36}); D: mean moderation. Updated 2026-10-09: the
generator of C and D (\code{04-power-simulation.R}) now uses anchored
carryover by default; the source's ``recursive'' describes the runs
stored before that date.}
\label{tab:inve-pd-compare}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3.0cm}p{2.0cm}p{2.0cm}p{2.0cm}p{2.0cm}p{2.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
Feature & \code{orig} & \code{covar} & C & D & Sensitivity binding \\
\midrule
Off-drug mean & recursive & anchored & recursive & recursive & anchored, turnover \\
Restart after off-drug & from zero & from zero & from zero & from zero & from residual \\
Moderation on drug & full & full & full & full & proportional to $h$ \\
Moderation off drug & full if any residual (step) & decays & decays & none & proportional to $h$ \\
Drug-response variance off drug & full & full & full & full & shrinks with $h$ \\
Valid for every schedule & no & no & block yes, ceiling remains & yes & yes \\
Ceiling on effect size & yes & yes & yes & none & none \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

%=============================================================
\section{Further features of a data-generating process}
\label{sec:pd-features}
%=============================================================

\code{docs/51}, Features 11--17, identifies seven properties of a trial
that no current DGP of the compendium represents. This section carries
the six with derived results (Features 11--16); Feature~17, the
bounded outcome scale, is argued only in the source and is omitted.
Each result is marked derived (analytic) or argued; the numerical
checks cited are of the formulas themselves, by the script
\code{check-pd-features.R} written for this section, and none of the
features has been simulated in the compendium's DGPs. Two further
options of the data-generating process close the section: the
phase-specific measurement error of \code{docs/51}, Feature 9, and the
prognostic pathways of the biomarker (\code{docs/49}). Here
$\varphi$ and $\Phi$ are the standard normal density and distribution
function ($\phi$ is reserved for decay families,
Equations~\eqref{eq:decay-exp}--\eqref{eq:decay-pow}), and
$C(z) = \varphi(z)/\{1 - \Phi(z)\}$ is the inverse Mills ratio.
(Source: \code{docs/51}, Sections 13--18.)

\subsection{Biomarker reliability}

Let the observed biomarker be $\tilde B_i = B_i + e_{B,i}$ with
independent error and reliability $R_B = \mathrm{Var}(B)/
\mathrm{Var}(\tilde B)$ (as in \code{docs/51}, which wrote the error
$\xi_i$ and the observed biomarker $B^{\ast}_i$ before 2026-10-09;
$B^{\ast}_i$ is the reflected biomarker of \S\ref{sec:invd-symmetry}).

Since $\mathrm{Cov}(\Delta, \tilde B) = \mathrm{Cov}(\Delta, B)$, the
raw-scale interaction coefficient on $\tilde B$ is $R_B\beta_{bm:D}$
(regression dilution; Hutcheon et al., 2010), the coefficient per SD
of the observed biomarker is attenuated by $\sqrt{R_B}$, and
$r_{\Delta \tilde B} = \sqrt{R_B}\,r_{\Delta B}$. The
noncentrality, proportional to $r_{\Delta B}/\sqrt{1-r_{\Delta B}^2}$
(Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-ncp}), therefore
scales by
\begin{equation}
\sqrt{R_B}\,\sqrt{\frac{1 - r_{\Delta B}^2}{1 - R_Br_{\Delta B}^2}}
\;\approx\; \sqrt{R_B},
\label{eq:pd-reliability}
\end{equation}
and the sample size for a given power rises by about $1/R_B$: about
25\% at $R_B = 0.8$ (derived). The approximation holds when the
interaction explains a small share of $\mathrm{Var}(\Delta)$; the
exact factor is slightly below $\sqrt{R_B}$. Under covariance binding
the visible correlation is $c_{bm}\sqrt{R_B}$, and because that
encoding draws $B_i$ inside the MVN, a binary or skewed biomarker can
be represented only under mean or sensitivity binding. (Check: raw
slope $0.800$ at $R_B = 0.8$; simulated noncentrality ratio $0.887$
against exact $0.889$ and $\sqrt{0.8} = 0.894$.) \note{\code{docs/51}
states the $\sqrt{R_B}$ scaling without the exact correction factor
in Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-reliability}; the two agree to first order.}

\subsection{Functional form of moderation}

Write the moderation as a function $\mathcal M(b_i)$, so the on-drug response
is scaled by $1 + \mathcal M(b_i)$; the linear case is $\mathcal M(b) = \beta_{bm} b$.
A linear working model estimates the projection
$\beta_{\text{lin}} = \mathrm{Cov}\{\mathcal M(b), b\}/\mathrm{Var}(b)$. For a
threshold $\mathcal M(b) = \Delta_h\,\mathbb{1}(b > b_{\text{thr}})$ at the median of
a standard normal biomarker ($b_{\text{thr}} = 0$; as in \code{docs/51}
since 2026-10-09, which wrote the effect $\Delta$, the threshold
$b^{\ast}$ and the function $h(b)$; the effect is $\Delta_h$ to avoid
the E9 contrast $\Delta_i$ and the function $\mathcal M$ to avoid the
moderation profile $h_t$),
\begin{equation}
\beta_{\text{lin}} = \Delta_h\, E\{b\,\mathbb{1}(b>0)\} =
  \Delta_h\,\varphi(0) \approx 0.399\,\Delta_h, \qquad
R^2_{\text{lin}} = \frac{\varphi(0)^2}{1/4} = \frac{2}{\pi}
  \approx 0.637,
\label{eq:pd-threshold}
\end{equation}
where $R^2_{\text{lin}}$ is the share of the moderation variance
$\mathrm{Var}\{\mathcal M(b)\} = \Delta_h^2/4$ captured by the linear term.
When the moderation is small relative to the residual variance, the
linear test has $\sqrt{2/\pi} \approx 0.80$ of the noncentrality of a
test using the true form (derived; numerically confirmed:
$0.3989$, $0.6366$, $0.7979$; a 4000-replicate simulation with $N =
200$ gave a mean $t$-ratio of 0.79). A responder mixture,
$\omega_i = \omega_R R_i$ with $\mathrm{logit}\Pr(R_i = 1 \mid b_i) =
\psi_0 + \psi_g b_i$, is the DGP-side counterpart of
\S\ref{sec:latent}; no projection result for it is stated.

\subsection{Exposure versus assignment}

Let exposure be $E_{it} = \mathcal{A}_{it}\,\mathrm{ramp}(t_{od})$,
with adherence $\mathcal{A}_{it} \in [0,1]$ (\code{docs/51} writes
$a_{it}$, which would collide with the contrast weights $a_t$) and
$\mathrm{ramp}(t_{od}) = \min(1, t_{od}/T_{\text{tit}})$, for example
$T_{\text{tit}} = 5$ weeks (Raskind et al., 2018). $E_{it}$ can serve
as the target $h^{\ast}$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-turnover}. If the
response is linear in exposure and adherence is independent of the
biomarker and of $\omega_i$, then
$E(\mathcal{A}_i\omega_i \mid b_i) = \bar{\mathcal{A}}\,E(\omega_i \mid
b_i)$, so the assigned-drug contrast and the interaction are both
scaled by the mean adherence $\bar{\mathcal{A}}$, the noncentrality
falls by at least that factor, and the sample size rises by at least
about $1/\bar{\mathcal{A}}^2$, variable adherence adding between-person
variance (derived; check: slope $2.402$ against $\bar{\mathcal{A}}
\times 3 = 2.401$). If adherence depends on the biomarker, the
interaction is biased in a direction set by that dependence (argued).
\note{The linearity in exposure is exact for the turnover model,
whose solution is linear in $h^{\ast}$, when adherence is constant
within participant; with time-varying adherence $F$ becomes a
participant-specific weighted exposure and the scaling is by its mean.}

\subsection{Integrity of blinding}

In blinded phases let the expectancy be $\eta_{it} = \tfrac12 +
\chi\,(D_{it} - \tfrac12)$, with $\chi = 0$ for intact blinding and
$\chi = 1$ for full unblinding (\code{docs/51} uses $\chi$; the
canonical design weight is $\eta_t = 0.5$ in these phases,
\S\ref{sec:partition}). With $PB_{it} = \eta_{it} PB^{\text{full}}_i(t)$, the
$\chi$-dependent part of a blinded-phase contrast is
$\chi\sum_t a_t (D_{it} - \tfrac12) PB^{\text{full}}_i(t) = \tfrac{\chi}{2}(\overline{PB}^{\text{full}}_{i,\text{on}} + \overline{PB}^{\text{full}}_{i,\text{off}})$, which is $\chi PB^{\text{full}}_i$ when
$PB^{\text{full}}_i(t)$ is constant over the contrasted visits. In the outcome,
which subtracts $PB$, the drug main effect is biased by $-\chi\overline{PB}^{\text{full}}$.
If the placebo response is prognostic, $PB^{\text{full}}_i = \mu_{pb} + s_{pb}\, b_i$,
the interaction acquires
\begin{equation}
-\chi\, s_{pb} \ \text{per SD of } b, \qquad\text{that is,}\qquad
\beta_{bm:D} \mapsto \beta_{bm:D} - \frac{\chi\, s_{pb}}{\sigma_{bm}},
\label{eq:pd-unblind}
\end{equation}
so unblinding converts a prognostic placebo pathway into a spurious
predictive one (derived; check: slope $-0.723$ against $-\chi s_{pb}
= -0.720$). A design-coded expectancy term is constant within the
blinded phase and cannot absorb it. \note{On the compendium's
convention (\code{NOTATION.md} Rule~5) the bias carries a minus sign,
so a positive $s_{pb}$ inflates the magnitude of a negative
interaction. \code{docs/51} stated it without a sign until 2026-10-09
and now gives $-\chi s_{pb}$ per SD of the biomarker.}

\subsection{Between-person variation in carryover}

Let $t_{1/2,i} = t_{1/2}\exp(\sigma_{\log t_{1/2}} x_i)$, $x_i \sim \mathcal N(0,1)$, so
$\lambda_i = \ln 2/t_{1/2,i}$, with off-drug coupling $g_{it} =
2^{-t_{sd}/t_{1/2,i}}$. The population-mean profile
\begin{equation}
\bar g(t_{sd}) = E\{e^{-\lambda_i t_{sd}}\}
\label{eq:pd-mixexp}
\end{equation}
is a mixture of exponentials. Its local decay rate,
$-d\log\bar g/dt_{sd}$, is the mean of $\lambda_i$ under weights
proportional to $e^{-\lambda_i t_{sd}}$; its derivative is minus the
weighted variance of $\lambda_i$, so the rate falls with $t_{sd}$.
It starts at $E(\lambda_i) = \lambda e^{\sigma_h^2/2} > \lambda$, so
$\bar g$ initially decays faster than the median-half-life profile
$2^{-t_{sd}/t_{1/2}}$, and at long $t_{sd}$ it is dominated by the
longest half-lives and decays more slowly (derived). With
$t_{1/2} = 1$ week and $\sigma_h = 0.5$: $\bar g = 0.689$ against
$0.707$ at 0.5 week, $0.266$ against $0.250$ at 2 weeks, $0.018$
against $0.004$ at 8 weeks; initial rate $0.785$ against
$\lambda = 0.693$. A common half-life in $D_{bc}$
(Equation~\eqref{eq:dbc}) therefore misfits the mean profile at both
ends. That power under a binary indicator follows the mean coupling
gap to first order is argued, not derived. \note{\code{docs/51} states
only the long-$t_{sd}$ behavior; the faster initial decay and the
crossing are added here.}

\subsection{Selection on a screening score}

Let the screening score be $Y_{i,\text{scr}} = \mathrm{BL}_i +
e_{i,\text{scr}}$ with between- and within-person variances
$\sigma^2_{\text{btw}}$ and $\sigma^2_{\text{wth}}$, enrollment when
$Y_{i,\text{scr}} > Y_{\text{elig}}$, and $z_{\text{elig}}$ the threshold standardized by
$\sqrt{\sigma^2_{\text{btw}} + \sigma^2_{\text{wth}}}$.

\textbf{Regression to the mean.} Later visits carry independent errors,
so the expected fall after selection is $E(e_{i,\text{scr}} \mid
\text{selected})$, which by normal truncation is (Barnett et al.,
2005)
\begin{equation}
\frac{\sigma^2_{\text{wth}}}{\sqrt{\sigma^2_{\text{btw}} +
  \sigma^2_{\text{wth}}}}\; C(z_{\text{elig}})
\label{eq:pd-rtm}
\end{equation}
(derived; check: $0.555$ simulated against $0.555$ at
$\sigma_{\text{btw}} = 1$, $\sigma_{\text{wth}} = 0.7$, $Y_{\text{elig}} = 1$).
It can be mistaken for natural course or placebo response. Within-
person blinded contrasts are largely protected, since it affects all
post-screening visits alike; contrasts that lean on the baseline visit
are not.

\textbf{Range restriction.} If the standardized biomarker has
correlation $c_{bm,\text{scr}}$ with the standardized screening score
(as in \code{docs/51}; written $r_{B,\text{scr}}$ here before
2026-10-09), then, since
$\mathrm{Var}(S \mid S > z) = 1 - C(z)\{C(z) - z\}$ for standard
normal $S$,
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Var}(b \mid \text{selected}) = 1 - c_{bm,\text{scr}}^2\,
  C(z_{\text{elig}})\{C(z_{\text{elig}}) - z_{\text{elig}}\}
\label{eq:pd-range}
\end{equation}
(derived; check at $z_{\text{elig}} = 1$: $0.930$, $0.797$, $0.487$ simulated
against $0.928$, $0.800$, $0.487$ for $c_{bm,\text{scr}} = 0.3, 0.5,
0.8$, each within Monte Carlo error). Interaction precision is
proportional to this variance, so power falls when the biomarker is
prognostic for baseline severity (\code{docs/51}, Feature~6).

\subsection{Phase-specific measurement error}
\label{sec:pd-me}

Phase-specific measurement error adds a variance
$\sigma^2_{\varepsilon,\text{phase}}$ to each visit's diagonal, which a
homoscedastic working model omits (\code{docs/51}, Feature 9). The
stress-test construct realizes it in one cell
(Equation~\eqref{eq:invd-outcome}).

\subsection{Prognostic pathways and their implied slopes}
\label{sec:bp-pathways}

A biomarker can also be coupled to the natural course, the placebo
response or the baseline. Write $s_0$ and $s_t$ for the true slopes of
$Y$ on the standardized biomarker $b_i$ at baseline and at visit $t$.
From $Y_{it} = \mathrm{BL}_i - (TV_{it} + PB_{it} + BR_{it})$, with
$\sigma_{TV} = 10$, placebo SD $10\eta_t$ and $\sigma_{BR} = 8$, the
slopes per biomarker SD are (Source: \code{docs/49}, Section 2;
re-derived; $\tilde G_{TV} = G_{TV}/\max G_{TV}$ is the normalized
natural-course curve):

\begin{table}[h]
\centering\small
\caption{Biomarker pathways and the slopes they imply.}
\label{tab:bp-pathways}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule
Pathway & Kind & $s_0$ & $s_t$, $t \ge 1$ \\
\midrule
Drug response, $c_{bm}$ & predictive & 0 & $-c_{bm}\sigma_{BR}\,g_t$ \\
Natural course, $c_{bm,TV}$ & prognostic & 0 & $-c_{bm,TV}\sigma_{TV}$ (constant) \\
Natural course, $\beta^{TV}_{bm}$ & prognostic & 0 & $-\beta^{TV}_{bm}\sigma_{TV}\tilde G_{TV}(t)$ (growing) \\
Placebo response, $c_{bm,PB}$ & prognostic & 0 & $-10\,c_{bm,PB}\,\eta_t$ \\
Baseline severity, $c_{bm,BL}$ & prognostic & $c_{bm,BL}\sigma_{BL}$ & $c_{bm,BL}\sigma_{BL}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent Only the first row is the estimand; in every other row a
correct analysis reports no interaction. Write the prognostic slope as
$s_{\text{prog}}$ when it is constant after baseline. The
\emph{constancy assumption} is $s_0 = s_{\text{off}}$: it holds for a
purely predictive biomarker ($s_0 = s_{\text{off}} = 0$) and for
baseline severity, and fails for the natural-course and placebo
pathways, whose slope is zero at baseline and nonzero afterwards.

%=============================================================
\section{Partitioning the treatment effect}
\label{sec:partition}
%=============================================================

Paper 06 asks what a single-indicator (``lumped'') analysis of the
treatment effect and its biomarker interaction actually estimates when
the true generative process has three distinguishable components.

\subsection{The outcome decomposition}

\begin{equation}
Y_{it} = \mathrm{BL}_i - \big[BR_{it} + PB_{it} + TV_{it}\big] +
  \varepsilon_{it},
\label{eq:decomposition}
\end{equation}
with each component following the modified-Gompertz form
(Equation~\eqref{eq:gompertz-06} as Paper 06 writes it;
Equation~\eqref{eq:gompertz-05} as the code computes it), and $PB_{it}$
additionally scaled by an \emph{expectancy multiplier}
$\eta(\mathrm{phase})$, the phase-level form of the canonical design
expectancy $\eta_t$, that operationalizes belief-driven placebo
response (Kirsch, 1985):
\begin{equation}
PB(t \mid \mathrm{phase}) = \eta(\mathrm{phase})\cdot m_{PB}
  \exp\!\big(-d_{PB}\exp(-r_{PB}t)\big) + \text{(offset)}, \qquad
\eta = \begin{cases} 1.0 & \text{open-label, on drug} \\
  \approx 0.5 & \text{blinded discontinuation} \\
  \in(0,0.5) & \text{open-label placebo.}
\end{cases}
\label{eq:eta-scaling}
\end{equation}
Because $\eta$ scales both the mean and SD of $PB$ by the same factor,
$\mathrm{SD}(PB\mid\mathrm{phase}) = \eta(\mathrm{phase})\cdot
\sigma_{PB}^{\mathrm{baseline}}$, only the \emph{product} $\eta\cdot
m_{PB}$ is identified from a two-phase (open-label + blinded) design;
recovering $m_{PB}$ itself requires an open-label-placebo phase or a
direct expectancy measurement.

A generalized, non-additive form allows a $BR\times PB$ interaction:
\begin{equation}
Y_{it} = \mathrm{BL}_i - \big[BR_{it} + PB_{it} + TV_{it} +
  \gamma\cdot BR_{it}\cdot PB_{it}\big] + \varepsilon_{it},
\label{eq:decomposition-gamma}
\end{equation}
with \emph{subadditivity} of the two response mechanisms defined by
the sign condition $\partial Y/\partial(BR\cdot PB) > 0$ on the
reduction scale. Paper 06 explicitly distinguishes true $\gamma\ne0$
subadditivity from an \emph{apparent} subadditivity that a latent
responder-class gating function $\pi(B)$ (\S\ref{sec:latent}) could
generate through confounding, without any real $BR$--$PB$ interaction
being present.

\subsection{What a one-component analysis actually estimates}

Writing the within-participant symptom reduction as $R_{it} =
\mathrm{BL}_i - Y_{it} = BR_{it} + PB_{it} + TV_{it} - \varepsilon_{it}$
and fitting the one-component model $R_{it} = \beta_0 + \beta_D D_{it} +
s(t) + u_i + e_{it}$, with $s(t)$ a smooth time trend, Paper 06
derives:
\begin{equation}
\operatorname*{plim}\hat\beta_D = m_{BR} + \Delta_{PB} + \Delta_{TV},
\label{eq:plim-betaD}
\end{equation}
where $\Delta_{PB} = E[PB\mid\text{on}] - E[PB\mid\text{off}]$ and
$\Delta_{TV}$ is the natural-history contribution that fails to cancel
under the contrast used (zero for a clean on/off within-participant
contrast; $E[TV]$ itself for a baseline-anchored pre-post analysis).
Both terms are generally non-negative for an improving outcome, so the
one-component estimate systematically \emph{overstates} the
pharmacological effect $m_{BR}$. This is a property of the estimator's
probability limit, unaffected as $N\to\infty$, not a finite-sample
artifact.

\note{2026-10-08: notation. Paper 06 writes $\alpha$, $g(t)$, $b_{PB}$
and $b_{TV}$; these collide with the significance level $\alpha$, the
coupling profile $g_t$ and the standardized biomarker $b_i$, and are
written $\beta_0$, $s(t)$, $\Delta_{PB}$ and $\Delta_{TV}$ here.}

The same logic applied to the biomarker slope gives the compendium's
key attenuation/inflation identity:
\begin{equation}
\beta_{bm}^{\mathrm{lumped}} = \beta_{bm}^{BR} + w_{PB}\,\beta_{bm}^{PB}
  + w_{TV}\,\beta_{bm}^{TV}, \qquad
\beta_{bm}^{C} = \frac{\operatorname{Cov}(m_C, B)}{\operatorname{Var}(B)}
  \ \ (C \in \{BR, PB, TV\}),
\label{eq:lumped-slope}
\end{equation}
with $w_{PB}, w_{TV}\ge0$ design-determined loadings. A biomarker
correlated with placebo responsiveness or with the natural-history
trajectory therefore registers a non-zero lumped slope even when its
true pharmacological association $\beta_{bm}^{BR}$ is exactly zero,
and, because Equation~\eqref{eq:lumped-slope} is an \emph{identity},
no amount of additional sample size can separate a genuinely
pharmacological biomarker signal from a confounded one whenever
$w_{PB}\beta_{bm}^{PB} + w_{TV}\beta_{bm}^{TV}\ne0$. Under
$\gamma\ne0$ subadditivity (Equation~\eqref{eq:decomposition-gamma}),
a further first-order correction proportional to
$\operatorname{Cov}(m_{BR}\cdot m_{PB}, B)$ enters the slope; the
identity above is the leading-order behavior in the additive case.

The operative confound is a biomarker's correlation with individual-
level \emph{variance} in placebo responsiveness, not with its
\emph{mean}:
\begin{equation}
\text{contamination driver} = c_{bm,PB}\cdot\sigma_{PB}, \qquad
  \text{not } c_{bm,PB}\cdot m_{PB}.
\label{eq:contamination-driver}
\end{equation}
Paper 06's calibration target throughout its production studies is
\begin{equation}
\beta_{bm:D} = -\frac{c_{bm}\,\sigma_{BR}}{\sigma_{bm}} = -2.25,
\label{eq:beta-target}
\end{equation}
against which Study A (uncontaminated biomarker, $c_{bm,PB}=0$) shows
bias within one Monte Carlo standard error of zero at every sample
size tested ($N \in \{35,70,200,300\}$, totals), consistent with
Equation~\eqref{eq:lumped-slope} giving zero bias when
$\beta_{bm}^{PB} = \beta_{bm}^{TV} = 0$. A contaminated-biomarker pilot
(100 replicates per cell, $N \in \{70, 300\}$) at $c_{bm,PB} \in \{0,
0.15, 0.30, 0.45\}$ shows, at $N = 300$, bias moving from $-0.06$ and
$-0.12$ to $+0.17$ and $+0.51$ (the last about 30 MCSEs from zero) as
contamination increases. Paper 06 explains the positive sign as
attenuation toward zero: high-$c_{bm,PB}$ participants' elevated
placebo response is partially absorbed by the biomarker main effect,
leaving the interaction coefficient pulled toward zero rather than
pushed away from it.

Paper 06 emphasizes that the bias of Equation~\eqref{eq:plim-betaD}
lives in the \emph{location} of the estimand, not in the test: the
one-component coefficient has a valid Wald test of its own nullity,
which rejects at the rate dictated by the displaced value, so a
significance test cannot detect a displacement away from the null.
Detecting it requires comparing the estimate with $m_{BR}$ (or, for
the slope, with $\beta_{bm}^{BR}$), which only a decomposition or a
phase contrast makes available. For the contaminated interaction the
displacement is \emph{toward} zero, so it lowers power, but a test
still cannot distinguish it from a smaller true effect.

\note{2026-10-08: corrected. This paragraph previously applied Paper
06's statement that ``the bias does not move the coefficient toward
zero'' to $\hat\beta_{bm:D}$ under contamination, which
contradicted the attenuation toward zero reported just above; in
Paper 06 the statement concerns $\operatorname{plim}\hat\beta_D$. A
parenthetical stating that $c_{bm,PB}$ was ``constrained below 0.45 by
positive-definiteness'' was removed: 0.45 is in the reported grid and
the current Paper 06 does not state such a constraint.}

\subsection{Recovery, and its dependence on trial design}

A phase-augmented analysis model is the smallest step toward
component-aware inference:
\begin{equation}
\code{Sx \textasciitilde\ bm + t + Dbc + phase + Dbc:phase + bm:Dbc +
  bm:Dbc:phase}
\label{eq:phase-augmented}
\end{equation}
with random intercept and \code{corCAR1} residual correlation, where
\code{phase} (open-label / blinded / post-crossover) absorbs
systematic expectancy differences and \code{Dbc:phase},
\code{bm:Dbc:phase} test whether the apparent drug effect and its
moderation shrink under blinding. Whether this, or full
component-matched Gompertz-basis fitting, recovers the true
pharmacological slope $\beta_{bm}^{BR}$ is shown to be
\emph{design-dependent}, not a property of decomposition per se: under
the compendium's standard Hybrid design, belief-covariate
decomposition, blinded-stratum contrasts, and full Gompertz-basis
decomposition all fail to recover $\beta_{bm}^{BR}$, performing no
better (and sometimes worse) than the lumped analysis, because
$D_{bc}$ and the expectancy multiplier $\eta$ are positively
correlated ($\approx+0.6$) under that design. A belief-decoupling
\emph{balanced-placebo} design (three open-label, six covert on-drug
with $\eta=0$, and six open-placebo with $\eta=1$ timepoints) reduces
that correlation to $\approx-0.66$ and restores recovery: in the
production run (1{,}000 replicates per cell, single-path design, $N
\in \{70, 150\}$ totals, verified from
\code{study-b-balanced-summary.rds}), decomposition-analysis bias at
$N = 150$ is $-0.022$, $+0.008$, $+0.021$ and $-0.026$ across
$c_{bm,PB}\in\{0,0.1,0.2,0.3\}$ (MCSE about 0.02, so within 1.4 MCSE
of zero), while the lumped estimator's bias grows monotonically,
$-0.014$, $+0.161$, $+0.340$, $+0.476$ (verified from the stored
summary).

\note{2026-10-08: Paper 06 states that every decomposition bias at the
larger $N$ is ``within one MCSE'' of zero; in the stored summary three
of the four lie between 1.0 and 1.4 MCSE (1.05, 1.03 and 1.32). The substantive conclusion is
unaffected.}

\subsection{Rank of the phase-augmented model, and the drug-expectancy
correlation by design}
\label{sec:invb-collinearity}

\textbf{The phase-augmented formula is rank-deficient whenever a phase
is entirely on drug.} Write the phase factor of
Equation~\eqref{eq:phase-augmented} with levels $\ell$ and indicators
$I_\ell$, the open-label level as reference. If every visit of the
open-label level is on drug, $D_{bc,it} = 1$ there, so
\begin{equation}
D_{bc,it} = I_{\mathrm{OL},it} + \sum_{\ell \ne \mathrm{OL}}
  D_{bc,it} I_{\ell,it}
  = 1 - \sum_{\ell \ne \mathrm{OL}} I_{\ell,it} +
  \sum_{\ell \ne \mathrm{OL}} D_{bc,it} I_{\ell,it},
\label{eq:invb-alias}
\end{equation}
an exact linear combination of the intercept, the phase main effects
and the \code{Dbc:phase} columns. The fixed-effects design matrix
therefore loses one rank, whatever the coding of the factor (derived).
The \code{bm:Dbc:phase} columns are not aliased, because the model has
no \code{bm:phase} term. Consequences:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt]
\item With the three phases of \code{docs/24} (open label, blinded
  discontinuation, crossover) the extension adds six columns but only
  five identified parameters, not the six that \code{docs/24} counts.
  In the two-level coding of the Paper 06 driver (phase set by
  $\eta_t \ge 0.99$, in \code{phase\_\allowbreak augmented\_\allowbreak
  fit()} of \code{analysis/\allowbreak scripts/\allowbreak
  component-decomposition/\allowbreak 00-common.R}), three columns add
  two identified parameters. (Verified by the rank of the model matrix
  for the 8-visit Hybrid paths of Table~\ref{tab:cg-paths} and for the
  16-visit two-path design of the Paper 06 recovery study,
  \code{check-gompertz-rmanova-cycles.R}.)
\item \code{nlme::lme} cannot fit the full formula: on the 16-visit
  design it stops with ``Singularity in backsolve'' (verified). The
  driver catches the error and refits without \code{Dbc:phase}, so
  every phase-augmented result is from the reduced formula and is
  recorded with
  \code{formula\_\allowbreak dropped = 'Dbc:phase'} (inspected).
  The rank deficiency encountered at $N = 35$ that \code{docs/28}
  (Section 3.4) attributes to near-collinear component trajectories is
  this exact aliasing of the formula, present at every $N$ (derived);
  for $N < 70$ the driver omits the full formula without attempting
  it.
\item In the reduced formula \code{Sx \textasciitilde\ \ldots\ +
  bm:Dbc + bm:Dbc:phase}, the biomarker slope is $\beta_B + \beta_{bm:D}$
  at open-label visits and $\beta_B + (\beta_{bm:D} +
  \beta_{bm:D:\mathrm{BDC}})D_{bc,it}$ at blinded visits. The reported
  coefficient $\beta_{bm:D}$ is therefore the open-label slope minus
  the blinded slope extrapolated to $D_{bc} = 0$: an open-label versus
  blinded contrast, in which drug state and expectancy change
  together, while the blinded-window interaction is the sum
  $\beta_{bm:D} + \beta_{bm:D:\mathrm{BDC}}$ (derived from the
  parameterization). \note{What this implies for the bias of the
  reported coefficient under a $PB$-coupled biomarker was not
  evaluated here; the sign of the coupling effect depends on the
  covariance construction, including any positive-definiteness repair
  (\S\ref{sec:ce}).}
\end{itemize}

(Source: \code{docs/24}, ``A small extension that usually pays off'';
\code{docs/28}, Sections 3.4 and 4.2.) \note{\code{docs/24} states that
the extension ``adds six fixed-effect parameters (two each for
\code{phase}, \code{Dbc:phase} and \code{bm:Dbc:phase})'' and calls
this ``derived by counting''. The count of columns is six; the count
of identified parameters in any design whose open-label phase is
entirely on drug is five.}

\textbf{Drug-expectancy correlation by design.} For a single-path
design whose visits fall into an on-drug, full-belief block of $n_1$
visits, an on-drug, covert ($\eta = 0$) block of $n_2$, and an
off-drug, full-belief block of $n_3$, with binary drug state, the
pooled correlation of $D$ and $\eta$ over visits is
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Cor}(D, \eta) = \frac{p_{11} - p_D p_\eta}
  {\sqrt{p_D(1 - p_D)\,p_\eta(1 - p_\eta)}}, \qquad
p_D = \frac{n_1 + n_2}{n}, \ p_\eta = \frac{n_1 + n_3}{n},\
p_{11} = \frac{n_1}{n},
\label{eq:invb-cor-de}
\end{equation}
with $n = n_1 + n_2 + n_3$. For the balanced-placebo design of Paper 06
($n_1 = 3$, $n_2 = n_3 = 6$) this is exactly $-2/3$; with the decayed
$D_{bc}$ at the driver's $t_{1/2} = 1$ week and 1.5-week spacing it is
$-0.656$, which matches the ``$\approx -0.66$'' of Paper 06 and
\S\ref{sec:partition} (derived; verified, \code{check-gompertz-rmanova-cycles.R}).
\note{The companion value ``$\approx +0.6$'' for the Hybrid design
was not reproduced. For the 16-visit two-path design of the recovery
study (\code{design\_hybrid\_full()}), pooled over visits with equal
path weights, the correlation is 0.775 with binary $D$ and 0.753 with
$D_{bc}$ at $t_{1/2} = 1$ (0.785 with a baseline row $D = \eta = 0$
added); for the 8-visit four-path Hybrid of Table~\ref{tab:cg-paths}
it is 0.509 and 0.485 (0.645 with the baseline row). How Paper 06
computed its figure, at which stage and over which rows, was not
located.}

%=============================================================
\section{Informative dropout}
\label{sec:dropout}
%=============================================================

Paper 09 evaluates a censoring mechanism whose hazard depends on the
participant's own symptom trajectory, crossed with the four trial
designs of \S\ref{sec:designs}.

\subsection{The dropout hazard}

Marginal per-timepoint censoring probability is the union of two
independent components:
\begin{equation}
\Pr(\mathrm{drop}_{it}) = 1 - \big(1 - p^{\mathrm{flat}}_{it}\big)
  \big(1 - p^{\mathrm{bias}}_{it}\big),
\label{eq:dropout-hazard}
\end{equation}
with $p^{\mathrm{flat}}_{it}\propto t$ depending on elapsed time alone
(calibrated to a marginal dropout fraction of
$\beta_0^{\mathrm{dr}}(1-\beta_1^{\mathrm{dr}})$) and
$p^{\mathrm{bias}}_{it}\propto t\,(\Delta_{Y,i}(t)+100)^{e_{b1}}$
depending on the symptom change $\Delta_{Y,i}(t) = Y_{it} - Y_{i0}$
since baseline (calibrated to
$\beta_0^{\mathrm{dr}}\beta_1^{\mathrm{dr}}$), where
$\beta_0^{\mathrm{dr}}$ is the overall dropout rate,
$\beta_1^{\mathrm{dr}}$ the fraction attributable to the
symptom-biased mechanism, and $e_{b1}=2$ throughout (Paper 09,
\code{R/censordata.R}).

\note{2026-10-08: notation. Paper 09 writes the dropout parameters as
$\beta_0, \beta_1$ and the symptom change as $\Delta_{Sx}(t)$; the
superscript separates the former from regression coefficients, and
$Y$ replaces the code name \code{Sx} (\code{NOTATION.md} Rule 3).} The additive shift of
$100$ inside the biased component keeps its convex argument positive
across the realized range of symptom scores, so that large
improvement lowers dropout probability while worsening (or a small
change) raises it. Once a participant is censored at a timepoint,
every later timepoint for that participant is also set missing
(monotone/absorbing dropout).

\subsection{Missingness classification}

Because the flat component depends only on a fully observed design
variable (elapsed time), it induces missingness that is ignorable for
likelihood-based inference on its own. Because the biased component's
censoring indicator is a function of $\Delta_{Y,i}(t)$, the quantity
realized at, and thereby rendered missing by, that timepoint, Paper 09
argues the mechanism is MNAR for every dropout pattern with
$\beta_1^{\mathrm{dr}} > 0$: missingness of $Y_{it}$ depends on the
value of $Y_{it}$ itself, the defining condition for
missing-not-at-random data. No manuscript in the compendium gives a formal
probability statement for MCAR/MAR/MNAR beyond this argument; the
classification rests on the hazard construction of
Equation~\eqref{eq:dropout-hazard} alone.

\subsection{Interaction with design}

The estimand is the same interaction test as elsewhere,
$\pi = \Pr(p<0.05)$ for $H_0: \beta_{bm:D}=0$, fit post-dropout. Paper
09 documents a ``happy accident'' selection effect, described
qualitatively rather than by a closed-form path-conditional formula:
participants with unambiguous open-label response are the most
readily resolved and most prone to drop out early, which enriches the
later, randomized (crossover/discontinuation) blocks for participants
whose open-label response was ambiguous, the subgroup whose crossover
contrasts are most informative for the interaction test. This selection effect is design-dependent: under the OL design,
where $D_{bc}$ is identically one throughout, there is no within-
subject contrast for $bm{:}D_{bc}$ at all, so dropout there can only
reduce power, never selectively concentrate information. A
quantified, per-path decomposition of the happy-accident effect is
explicitly scoped for a future production-grade rerun and is not
delivered in the version inspected here; as with several other design
comparisons in this compendium (\S\ref{sec:notation}), an earlier
mismatched-$N$ run of this same comparison had produced an apparent
robustness finding (the Hybrid design appearing dropout-immune) that a
matched-$N=70$ re-run showed was a ceiling effect rather than a
property of the design.

\subsection{The MAR and MCAR mechanisms of the stress test}
\label{sec:invf-mar}

The stress test of \code{docs/46} (\S\ref{sec:invd-stress}) uses two
monotone mechanisms distinct from Paper 09's hazard
(Equation~\eqref{eq:dropout-hazard}). With $d_i$ the first missing
post-baseline visit ($d_i = \infty$ if complete), $t$ indexing the
post-baseline visits $1, \dots, 8$, and $U_{it}$ independent uniforms,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\text{MCAR:}\quad & \Pr(d_i < \infty) = 0.2, \qquad d_i \mid d_i < \infty
  \sim \mathrm{Uniform}\{3, \dots, 8\}; \\
\text{MAR:}\quad & \operatorname{logit} p^{\mathrm{MAR}}_{it} = \mu_h
  + \beta_h\, z_{i,t-1}, \quad \beta_h = 1, \quad t = 3, \dots, 8, \\
  & \frac1{n_p}\sum_{i \in p}\Big[1 - \prod_{t=3}^{8}
  \big(1 - p^{\mathrm{MAR}}_{it}\big)\Big] = 0.2,
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:invf-mar}
\end{equation}
where, under MAR, $p^{\mathrm{MAR}}_{it}$ is the hazard at visit $t$
given presence at $t - 1$, $d_i$ is the first $t$ with
$U_{it} < p^{\mathrm{MAR}}_{it}$, $z_{i,t-1}$ is the score at the
previous visit standardized, visit by visit, across the $n_p$
participants of randomization path $p$ in the simulated trial, and
$\mu_h$ is solved numerically for each path of each trial (Sources:
\code{docs/46}, Section 5; \code{docs/51}, Feature 9; inspected,
\code{11-strawman-stress-test.R}, \code{dropout\_visit()}). Because the
hazard depends only on the previously observed score, the mechanism is
MAR and likelihood analysis of the observed data is ignorable for it,
in contrast to the MNAR classification of Section~\ref{sec:dropout}
(derived).
\note{\code{docs/51} writes the slope as $\beta_h$ without a value; the
driver fixes it at 1. Because $\mu_h$ is calibrated within each path of
each trial to the realized scores, the 20\% rate holds in expectation
conditional on those scores rather than as a population parameter,
$\mu_h$ itself varies across trials, and a participant's hazard also
depends on the other participants' observed values: dropout depends
only on observed data, but not through a fixed per-participant
model.}

%#############################################################
\part{Closed-form theory of the interaction test}
%#############################################################

%=============================================================
\section{Biomarker coupling under carryover and the closed-form power
of the interaction test}
\label{sec:cg}
%=============================================================

This section gives the closed-form account of how the carryover
half-life $t_{1/2}$ acts on the power of the interaction test (Source:
\code{docs/37}; Paper~01, Methods and Appendix~A.1--A.3). The estimand
and the coupling rules are those of \S\ref{sec:cg-estimand}, and the
conditional equivalence of the two encodings is
Equation~\eqref{eq:archB-conditional}. Setting: the
published process (commit \code{58b32a9}, configuration A of
\code{docs/36}); the Hybrid design, visits at weeks 4, 8, 9, 10, 11,
12, 16, 20, expectancy $\eta_t = 1$ at the two open-label visits and
$0.5$ afterwards; $N = 70$ allocated $n_p = 18, 18, 17, 17$ to $P = 4$
paths (Table~\ref{tab:cg-paths}); compound symmetry (CS), $\rho_{cs} = 0.8$
within a component, cross-component correlation $c_1 = 0.2$ at the
same visit and $c_\times = 0.1$ otherwise; $\sigma_{TV} = 10$,
$\sigma_{PB,t} = 10\eta_t$, $\sigma_{BR} = 8$, $\sigma_{bm} = 15.36$;
strength $c_{bm} = \beta_{bm} = 0.25$ (\code{NOTATION.md} Rule~2),
below the CS ceiling of $0.256$, so no matrix is repaired. Local
symbols ($a_t$, $g_t$, $h_t$, $\phi_t$, $\sigma_\Delta^2$, $\varpi_p$, $\mathcal G$)
collide with no canonical one. \emph{Verified} below means recomputed
by an independent check script written for this review
(\code{check-coupling-closed-form.R}) and compared with
\code{analysis/data/quick-sim/carryover-closed-form/}
(\code{e9-per-path.csv}, \code{e9-power-vs-gap.csv}); \emph{quoted}
means taken from \code{docs/37} without recomputation.

Every Hybrid off-drug visit follows an exposure, so $t_{sd} \ge 1$
week there; several results below rest on this condition.

\subsection{The paired-difference statistics E9 and E9b, and their
moments}
\label{sec:cg-moments}

E9 (Equations~\eqref{eq:e9-diffs}--\eqref{eq:e9-ols}, with $\beta_1$
written $\beta_{bm:D}$) forms
$\Delta_i = \sum_t a_t Y_{it}$ with $a_t = 1/n_{\mathrm{on}}$ on drug
and $-1/n_{\mathrm{off}}$ off drug, regresses $\Delta_i$ on $B_i$ by
least squares over all $N$ participants, and tests the slope on $N-2$
degrees of freedom. E9b counts baseline as one more off-drug visit
(weight $-1/(n_{\mathrm{off}}+1)$ on each off-drug row), as the
published \code{lmer} analysis codes it. All weights sum to zero, so
$\mathrm{BL}_i$ cancels and $\Delta = -\sum_{t\ge1} a_t S_t$,
$S_t = TV_t + PB_t + BR_t$; the post-baseline weights sum to $0$ (E9)
or $1/(n_{\mathrm{off}}+1)$ (E9b) (derived). Within a path $\Delta$
and $B$ are jointly normal, so
$E(\Delta \mid B) = \mu + \beta_{bm:D}(B - \mu_{bm})$ exactly, with
residual variance $\sigma_\Delta^2$. \note{2026-10-09: notation. The
residual variance of the contrast was written $\tau^2$ (and $\tau_p^2$
per path) here and in \code{docs/37} to \code{docs/39} and Paper 01;
it is $\sigma_\Delta^2$, $\sigma_{\Delta,p}^2$, because $\tau^2$ is the
canonical between-participant variance of the drug effect
(\S\ref{sec:maineffect}). The path share $q_p = n_p/N$ is now
$\varpi_p$, since $q$ is the canonical path index.}

\textbf{Proposition 1 (variance).} With $m_1 = \sum a_t$,
$m_2 = \sum a_t^2$, $m_e = \sum a_t\eta_t$,
$m_{ee} = \sum a_t^2\eta_t^2$, $m_{2e} = \sum a_t^2\eta_t$ and
$\sigma_{PB} = 10$,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{Var}(\Delta) = {} & (\sigma_{TV}^2 + \sigma_{BR}^2)
  \big[\rho_{cs} m_1^2 + (1-\rho_{cs}) m_2\big]
  + \sigma_{PB}^2\big[\rho_{cs} m_e^2 + (1-\rho_{cs}) m_{ee}\big] \\
& + 2\sigma_{TV}\sigma_{BR}\big[c_\times m_1^2 + (c_1 - c_\times) m_2\big]
  + 2(\sigma_{TV} + \sigma_{BR})\sigma_{PB}
  \big[c_\times m_1 m_e + (c_1 - c_\times) m_{2e}\big],
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:cg-var}
\end{equation}
which is free of $t_{1/2}$: the half-life enters only through the
means and the coupling, and neither appears (Source: \code{docs/37},
Section 4.1; Paper~01, Appendix~A.1; derived). This holds for any
construct whose response block is free of $t_{1/2}$, hence for
unrepaired matrices only. Values: $44.13$ (paths 1--2) and $45.03$
(paths 3--4) for E9, $51.04$ and $51.77$ for E9b (verified:
Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-var} equals $a^\top\Sigma a$ from the full
$24\times24$ response covariance).

\textbf{Proposition 2 (slope).} Only $BR_t$ covaries with $B$, so
$\beta_{bm:D} = -c_{bm}(\sigma_{BR}/\sigma_{bm})\sum_t a_t g_t$ and,
with $\bar g_{\mathrm{off}}$ the mean coupling over the post-baseline
off-drug visits,
\begin{equation}
\beta_{bm:D}^{\mathrm{E9}} = -c_{bm}\frac{\sigma_{BR}}{\sigma_{bm}}
  \big(1 - \bar g_{\mathrm{off}}\big), \qquad
\beta_{bm:D}^{\mathrm{E9b}} = -c_{bm}\frac{\sigma_{BR}}{\sigma_{bm}}
  \Big(1 - \tfrac{n_{\mathrm{off}}}{n_{\mathrm{off}}+1}\,
  \bar g_{\mathrm{off}}\Big)
\label{eq:cg-slope}
\end{equation}
(Source: \code{docs/37}, Section 4.2; derived, and verified per path by
direct covariance against \code{e9-per-path.csv}). Under the mean rules
replace $c_{bm}$ by $\beta_{bm}$ and $\bar g_{\mathrm{off}}$ by
$\bar h_{\mathrm{off}}$, which is $0$ under plain mean moderation. The
pooled slope is $\bar\beta_{bm:D} = \sum_p \varpi_p \beta_{bm:D,p}$,
$\varpi_p = n_p/N$.

\textbf{Residual variance.} Covariance binding explains part of the
existing variance; mean binding adds to it:
\begin{equation}
\sigma^2_{\Delta,\mathrm{cov}} = \mathrm{Var}(\Delta) - \beta_{bm:D}^2\sigma_{bm}^2,
\qquad \sigma^2_{\Delta,\mathrm{mean}} = \mathrm{Var}(\Delta),
\label{eq:cg-tau}
\end{equation}
with $\mathrm{Var}(\Delta)$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-var} in both
(derived). Power depends on these through
$r_{\Delta B} = \beta_{bm:D}\sigma_{bm}/\mathrm{SD}(\Delta)$, about
$-0.30$ without carryover (verified, $-0.301$ on path 1;
\code{docs/37} writes $\rho_{\Delta B}$, renamed here to keep $\rho$
for AR(1)).

\textbf{Power.} On a single path of $n$ participants,
$\mathrm{SS}_{bm} = \sum_i(B_i - \bar B)^2 \sim \sigma_{bm}^2\chi^2_{n-1}$;
given the biomarker values the $t$-statistic is exactly noncentral $t$
on $n-2$ degrees of freedom with noncentrality
$\beta_{bm:D}\sqrt{\mathrm{SS}_{bm}}/\sigma_\Delta$, power is its tail
probability averaged over the $\chi^2$ law, and
$\mathrm{Var}(\hat\beta_{bm:D}) = \sigma_\Delta^2/\{\sigma_{bm}^2(n-3)\}$. At
$\mathrm{SS}_{bm} = \sigma_{bm}^2(n-1)$,
\begin{equation}
\Lambda = \sqrt{n-1}\; r_{\Delta B}\big/\sqrt{1 - r_{\Delta B}^2}
\label{eq:cg-ncp}
\end{equation}
(Paper~01, Appendix~A.2; derived; $\Lambda$, the noncentrality, as in
Paper~01 and \code{docs/37}; this section previously wrote
$\mathrm{ncp}$).
Pooling paths with a common intercept gives, to first order in the
path differences (argued), a noncentral $t$ on $N-2$ degrees of
freedom with noncentrality
$\bar\beta_{bm:D}\sqrt{\mathrm{SS}_{bm}}/\sigma_{\mathrm{res}}$ and
\begin{equation}
\sigma^2_{\mathrm{res}} = \sum_p \varpi_p\big[\sigma_{\Delta,p}^2 + (\mu_p - \bar\mu)^2
  + \sigma_{bm}^2(\beta_{bm:D,p} - \bar\beta_{bm:D})^2\big].
\label{eq:cg-pooled}
\end{equation}
The path-mean term $(\mu_p - \bar\mu)^2$ is under $0.7\%$ of
$\sigma^2_{\mathrm{res}}$ in Hybrid but $54\%$ in CO, where it is a
period effect that a mixed model's time term absorbs and E9's common
intercept does not (\code{docs/37}, Sections 4.4 and 5.7; the CO
share is recomputed in \S\ref{sec:inve-cg-designs}).

\subsection{Power through the coupling gap}
\label{sec:cg-gap}

Let $\mathcal G = \bar g_{\mathrm{on}} - \bar g_{\mathrm{off}}
= 1 - \bar g_{\mathrm{off}}$, the \emph{coupling gap}. Under covariance
binding and E9, $\mathrm{Var}(\Delta)$ is fixed and
$r_{\Delta B} = -c_{bm}\sigma_{BR}\,\mathcal G/\mathrm{SD}(\Delta)$ is
exactly proportional to $\mathcal G$, so the coupling profile acts on
power only through $\mathcal G$; which off-drug visits carry the
coupling does not matter, since E9 weights them equally (derived).
Fixing $\Lambda$ at a target $\Lambda^\ast$ in
Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-ncp} gives the exact single-path sample size
\begin{equation}
n - 1 = \Lambda^{\ast2}\Big(\frac{1}{r_{\Delta B}^2} - 1\Big)
  \;\approx\; \frac{\Lambda^{\ast2}\,\mathrm{Var}(\Delta)}
  {c_{bm}^2\sigma_{BR}^2\,\mathcal G^{2}}
  \qquad (r_{\Delta B}^2 \ll 1),
\label{eq:cg-n-gap}
\end{equation}
so the required $N$ scales roughly as $1/\mathcal G^2$ (derived). On
Hybrid path 1, halving the gap from 1 to 0.5 raises
$1/r_{\Delta B}^2 - 1$ from $10.03$ to $43.13$, a factor of $4.3$
(verified). Table~\ref{tab:cg-gap} gives power along the gap.

\note{\code{docs/37}, Section 5.4, says the noncentrality is
proportional to the gap. By Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-ncp} only
$r_{\Delta B}$ is; the noncentrality is approximately proportional
when $r_{\Delta B}^2$ is small, as Paper~01 states, and the
$1/\mathcal G^2$ rule is approximate to the same degree.}

reproduced to within $0.002$).}
\label{tab:cg-gap}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}

\midrule

\subsection{Step coupling: the gap is zero at any positive half-life}
\label{sec:cg-step}

With $t_{1/2} > 0$ the off-drug mean
$\mu_{BR,t} = \mu_{BR,t-1}2^{-t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}}$ is strictly positive
at every off-drug visit after an exposure, so
Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-step} gives $g_t = 1$ there. In Hybrid (and
OL+BDC) every off-drug visit follows an exposure, so
$\bar g_{\mathrm{off}} = 1$, $\mathcal G = 0$, and by
Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-slope}
\begin{equation}
\beta_{bm:D}^{\mathrm{E9}} = 0, \qquad
\beta_{bm:D}^{\mathrm{E9b}} = -c_{bm}\frac{\sigma_{BR}}{\sigma_{bm}}
  \cdot\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{off}}+1}
  \qquad \text{for every } t_{1/2} > 0
\label{eq:cg-step-slope}
\end{equation}
(Source: \code{docs/37}, Section 5.1; derived). Power is a step
function of the half-life: $0.721$ (E9) and $0.654$ (E9b) at
$t_{1/2} = 0$; $0.050$ and $0.081$ at every positive value (verified).
The E9b remainder ($-0.0325$ on paths 1--2, $-0.0260$ on paths 3--4,
pooled $-0.029$) is carried by the baseline row alone and matches the
simulated published analysis ($-0.026$ to $-0.032$; quoted). The step
rule is the $t_{1/2}\to\infty$ limit of graded coupling, applied at
every positive half-life. The condition matters: in CO the
placebo-first path's off-drug visits precede exposure, keep $g_t = 0$,
and keep their full slope, so pooled E9 power falls only to $0.20$
(\code{docs/37}, Section 5.7; recomputed as 0.199 in
Table~\ref{tab:inve-co}, \S\ref{sec:inve-cg-designs}).

\subsection{Graded coupling: monotone decline}
\label{sec:cg-graded}

Under Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-graded} (Paper~01, Appendix~A.3),
\begin{equation}
\bar g_{\mathrm{off}}(t_{1/2}) = \frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{off}}}
  \sum_{t\,\mathrm{off}} 2^{-t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}}, \qquad
\frac{d\bar g_{\mathrm{off}}}{dt_{1/2}} =
  \frac{\ln 2}{n_{\mathrm{off}}\,t_{1/2}^2}
  \sum_{t\,\mathrm{off}} t_{sd,t}\,2^{-t_{sd,t}/t_{1/2}} > 0
\label{eq:cg-gbar}
\end{equation}
because every off-drug visit has $t_{sd,t} > 0$ (derived; derivative
verified by finite difference). \textbf{Proposition 3.} On every path
$|\beta_{bm:D,p}|$ is strictly decreasing in $t_{1/2}$ on $(0,\infty)$
and $\sigma_{\Delta,p}^2 = \mathrm{Var}(\Delta) - \beta_{bm:D,p}^2\sigma_{bm}^2$
strictly increasing, since $\mathrm{Var}(\Delta)$ is fixed; so the
noncentrality and the power of the path-stratified test are strictly
decreasing (derived). For the pooled statistic the path-mean term also
moves with $t_{1/2}$; power was found non-increasing on a 120-point
grid from $0.03$ to $4$ weeks for E9 and E9b (\code{docs/37},
Section 5.2; quoted). Monotonicity is thus proved for the stratified
statistic and checked numerically, not proved, for the pooled one.
\textbf{Limits.} With $\min t_{sd} \ge 1$,
$\bar g_{\mathrm{off}} \le 2^{-1/t_{1/2}}$, so the loss vanishes
faster than any power of $t_{1/2}$ as $t_{1/2}\to0^+$ (at most
$2^{-10}$ at $0.1$ week); and $\bar g_{\mathrm{off}}\to1$ as
$t_{1/2}\to\infty$. The patient-weighted gap is $0.991$, $0.906$,
$0.754$ and $0.551$ at $t_{1/2} = 0.2$, $0.5$, $1$, $2$ weeks
(verified).

\label{tab:cg-power}

\subsection{Anchored versus compounding means in the closed form}
\label{sec:cg-anchor-closed}

For the closed form the choice between the anchored and the
compounding means of Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-anchor} is nearly
immaterial: the means enter
neither $\mathrm{Var}(\Delta)$ nor $\beta_{bm:D}$, only $\mu_p$ and so
only the path-mean term of Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-pooled}; the step
rule is unchanged because both forms are positive after exposure; and
graded coupling uses $\phi_t$, not the recursive mean (derived). The
closed-form and Monte Carlo results of this part now use the anchored
mean; against the compounding mean they change by at most $0.002$ in
closed-form power in Hybrid and OL+BDC and up to $0.041$ in CO, where
the means enter through the period effect (verified, rerun of
\code{01-e9-closed-form.R}, 2026-10-09). Its effect on the published
mixed model has now been tested: in RH's pipeline the correction alone
changes power by at most $0.006$ (\code{docs/53}, Section 8.3).

\subsection{Covariance binding as mean moderation}
\label{sec:cg-equiv}

Conditioning the joint normal on the biomarker gives
Equation~\eqref{eq:archB-conditional} (\S\ref{sec:architectures}).
Hence, conditionally on the biomarker,
\emph{covariance binding with coupling $g$ is mean moderation with
profile $h_t = g_t$ and strength $\beta_{bm} = c_{bm}$, with the
drug-response covariance reduced by the rank-one term
$c_{bm}^2\sigma_{BR}^2 g g^\top$}. Consequences (derived unless
marked):
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*), itemsep=2pt]
\item Graded coupling and decayed mean moderation have identical
  per-path slopes at every half-life (Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-slope} with
  $h_t = g_t$).
\item Their residual variances differ by exactly
  $\beta_{bm:D}^2\sigma_{bm}^2$ (Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-tau}; $42.007$
  against $44.133$ on path 1 at $t_{1/2} = 1$, verified). The
  covariance encoding thus has slightly more power at the same slope
  ($0.721$ against $0.681$ without carryover), and decayed mean
  moderation loses power almost exactly as graded coupling does
  (Table~\ref{tab:cg-power}). The carryover loss is set by the
  exposure profile of the interaction, not by the channel that carries
  it.
\item The ceiling is the condition that the conditional covariance of
  the whole response vector, $M - c_{bm}^2 uu^\top$ in Paper~01's
  correlation notation ($M$ the response block, $c_{bm}u$ the biomarker
  correlations), be positive definite; given $M$ positive definite this
  is equivalent to $c_{bm}^2 u^\top M^{-1}u < 1$, both being
  Schur-complement conditions on the full matrix. Without carryover it
  is 0.256 under the published compound-symmetry construct (Hybrid,
  $\rho = 0.8$), and 0.440 under separable AR(1) at $\rho = 0.8$
  (0.474 at $\rho = 0.7$, 0.480 in the Hybrid design) (Paper~01,
  Results; \code{docs/34}; Table~\ref{tab:ce-ceilings}).
\item Under graded coupling $g_t$ equals an exposure-decayed indicator
  at the true half-life, provided that indicator is $0$ before first
  exposure, so the conditional mean of $BR$ is linear in
  $b_i D_{bc,it}$ with the on-drug coefficient of
  Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-estimand}. This is the basis of Paper~01's
  statement that $D_{bc,it}$ at the true half-life recovers the
  on-drug moderation; recovery by a fitted \code{lme} also needs the
  remaining mean structure to be adequate (argued, not shown).
\end{enumerate}

\note{This bears on \S\ref{sec:architectures}, whose 2026-08 text
attributed the divergence between the architectures under carryover to
a structural asymmetry (exponential versus proportional decay of the
biomarker signal); the 2026-10-08 revision of that section marks the
comparison superseded. By Equation~\eqref{eq:archB-conditional} the divergence is a difference
of exposure profiles ($h_t = D_{it}$ against $g_t = \phi_t$ off drug),
not of channels; a comparison reporting larger losses under covariance
binding compares a fading interaction with a non-fading one (Paper~01,
Discussion).}

\subsection{Further moments of the paired-difference statistic}
\label{sec:inve-cg-moments}

\textbf{Pooled-slope variance.} With the fixed allocation of the
published code (path $p$ receives exactly $n_p$ participants), write
$\beta_{bm:D,p} = \bar\beta_{bm:D} + \tilde\beta_p$, so that
$\sum_p \varpi_p\tilde\beta_p = 0$ \note{2026-10-09: notation. The path
deviation was written $\delta_p$, which collides with the standardized
bias $\delta$; it is $\tilde\beta_p$ here.}. The pooled least-squares slope then has, to
first order in $1/N$,
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Var}(\hat\beta_{bm:D}) \approx
\frac{\sum_p \varpi_p\big[\sigma_{\Delta,p}^2 + (\mu_p - \bar\mu)^2\big]}
     {(N - 3)\,\sigma_{bm}^2}
+ \frac{2}{N}\sum_p \varpi_p\,\tilde\beta_p^2
\label{eq:inve-pooled-var}
\end{equation}
(Source: \code{docs/37}, Section 4.4; derived). The first term uses
$E(1/\mathrm{SS}_{bm}) = 1/\{\sigma_{bm}^2(N-3)\}$ for
$\mathrm{SS}_{bm} \sim \sigma_{bm}^2\chi^2_{N-1}$. The second arises
because, conditionally on the biomarker values, the pooled slope is the
$(B_i - \bar B)^2$-weighted mean of the path slopes; with the allocation
fixed its mean term is exactly $\bar\beta_{bm:D}$ and its variance is
$\sum_i \tilde\beta_{p(i)}^2 \mathrm{Var}\{(B_i-\mu_{bm})^2\}/(N\sigma_{bm}^2)^2
= 2\sum_p \varpi_p\tilde\beta_p^2/N$, since a $\sigma_{bm}^2\chi^2_1$ variable has
variance $2\sigma_{bm}^4$. A Monte Carlo of the two-stage regression
($4\times10^4$ trials, four paths of $18, 18, 17, 17$, slope
heterogeneity far larger than in any design here) gave $0.002959$
against $0.002992$ from Equation~\eqref{eq:inve-pooled-var}, a relative
difference of $1.1\%$ (verified). \note{The noncentrality of
Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-pooled} carries the slope heterogeneity as
$\sigma_{bm}^2\tilde\beta_p^2$ inside $\sigma^2_{\mathrm{res}}$, which
corresponds to a factor $1/N$ rather than the $2/N$ of
Equation~\eqref{eq:inve-pooled-var}. The two treatments agree to first
order only in the sense that the term is negligible in every design
evaluated: in Hybrid the path-mean term is at most $0.30$ against
residual variances of $41$ to $49$, and in OL+BDC at most $0.6\%$ of the
total (\code{docs/37}, Section 4.4; quoted).}

\textbf{Power quadrature.} Power is computed by averaging the
noncentral-$t$ tail probability over $400$ quantiles of
$\chi^2_{N-1}$. \code{docs/37} (Section 4.4) records that a first
version, which used $N$ in place of $N-3$ and fixed $\mathrm{SS}_{bm}$
at its mean, overstated power by about $0.01$ to $0.02$, an error the
direct simulation detected (quoted).

\textbf{E9b variance.} Counting baseline as an off-drug row raises
$\mathrm{Var}(\Delta)$ by about $15\%$ (on paths 1--2,
$51.04/44.13 = 1.157$), because the post-baseline weights no longer sum to zero
($m_1 = 1/(n_{\mathrm{off}}+1)$) and the person-level $\rho_{cs}$ terms of
Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-var} no longer cancel (derived; the exact split
of the increase is Equation~\eqref{eq:invf-e9b-exact}). This is why E9
has more power than E9b under short carryover, while beyond about one
week E9b has more, since its baseline row keeps zero coupling at every
half-life (Table~\ref{tab:cg-power}; \code{docs/37}, Section 5.2).

\textbf{Mean slope.} Table~\ref{tab:inve-slope} gives the pooled slope
$\bar\beta_{bm:D}$ that accompanies Table~\ref{tab:cg-power}, together
with the E9b rows of the two mean rules (verified; the decayed-mean E9b
row is computed here and is not in the source).

\note{The graded E9b slope at $t_{1/2} = 1$ is $-0.105475$, so
$-0.105$ to three places; \code{docs/37} printed $-0.106$, a double
rounding, corrected there on 2026-10-08.}

\textbf{The shape of the gap curve.} Along Table~\ref{tab:cg-gap} the
curve is steepest near gap 1 and flattest near 0: the first $0.1$ of
gap lost costs $0.097$ of E9 power ($0.721$ to $0.624$), the last $0.1$
only $0.007$ ($0.057$ to $0.050$) (Source: \code{docs/37}, Section 5.4;
verified, $0.722$, $0.625$, $0.057$, $0.050$ with the path-mean term
omitted). Short carryover costs almost nothing under graded coupling
because the gap barely moves ($0.991$ at $t_{1/2} = 0.2$), not because
the curve is flat there.

\textbf{Floating-point underflow of the step rule.} At very short
half-lives the off-drug mean at the latest Hybrid off-drug visits
underflows to exactly zero in double precision, so the step rule
switches those visits' coupling off. Under the anchored mean used for
the closed-form results this happens below about $0.0065$ weeks
(Hybrid, largest $t_{sd} = 7$) and $0.0093$ weeks (CO); under the
published compounding recursion, below about $0.012$ weeks (about
$2^{-1300}$ at the last off-drug visit of path 4 when $t_{1/2} = 0.01$)
and $0.023$ weeks (\code{docs/37}, Section 5.1, and its script;
computed 2026-10-09).
This is a property of the arithmetic, not of the model; the same
mechanism under fast Weibull washout is treated in
Section~\ref{sec:inve-ce-shape}.

\subsection{Power by interaction strength}
\label{sec:inve-cg-strength}

The closed form extends to any strength below the ceiling.
Table~\ref{tab:inve-strength} gives it at $0.2$ and $0.3$, with the
compound-symmetry ceiling of each covariance rule; cells at or above the
ceiling have no joint distribution and are marked infeasible (Source:
\code{docs/37}, Sections 5.5 and 5.6, Figure 3; verified). At zero
strength every rule gives the test size, $0.05$.
