reading the \code{us-KR} estimates as these limits (verified for the
limits; the agreement is with quoted simulation means).
\note{\code{docs/45}, Section 7, states the recovery at $t_{1/2}^a =
t_{1/2}$ and the overshoot from simulation only; Equation~\eqref{eq:invf-dbc-exact}
and the table are derived here.}

%=============================================================
\section{Inference: from a fitted coefficient to a calibrated test}
\label{sec:inference}
%=============================================================

\subsection{A closed-form comparator: repeated-measures ANOVA}
\label{sec:rmanova}

Paper 08's principal analytic contribution, stated for comparison
against the compendium's simulation-based test procedures, is a full
closed-form treatment of the classical split-plot RM-ANOVA under a
dichotomized biomarker. In the symbols of \code{docs/26} (renamed on
2026-10-07 so that none collides with a canonical symbol), the model is
\begin{equation}
Y_{gjit} = \mu + \xi_g + \phi_j + (\xi\phi)_{gj} +
  u_{i(g)} + \upsilon_t + (\xi\upsilon)_{gt} + (\phi\upsilon)_{jt} +
  (\xi\phi\upsilon)_{gjt} + \varepsilon_{gjit},
\label{eq:rmanova-model}
\end{equation}
for biomarker stratum $g \in \{1,\dots,a\}$, treatment phase $j \in
\{1,\dots,b\}$, participant $i \in \{1,\dots,n\}$ within stratum, and
within-phase timepoint $t \in \{1,\dots,T\}$, with $u_{i(g)}\sim
\mathcal N(0,\sigma_u^2)$ the random participant effect and
$\varepsilon_{gjit}\sim \mathcal N(0,\sigma_\varepsilon^2)$ independent
within-participant error. The effect symbols $\xi$, $\phi$, $\upsilon$
are local to this subsection ($\phi_j$ is not the decay function
$\phi$ of \S\ref{sec:designs}). \note{2026-10-09: notation. The time
effect was written $\tau_t$, as in \code{docs/26}; $\tau^2$ is the
canonical heterogeneity variance, so it is $\upsilon_t$ here.} Here $n$ is the number of participants
\emph{per stratum}, so the total is $N = an$. Model
\eqref{eq:rmanova-model} implies compound symmetry; the $F$-tests need
only the weaker condition of sphericity of each within-participant
factor, and a factor with two levels is always spherical. The
biomarker-treatment interaction $(\xi\phi)_{gj}$ is the target; its
test statistic is
\begin{equation}
F_{AB} = \frac{MS_{AB}}{MS_{B\times S(A)}} \sim F(df_{AB},\,
  df_{B\times S(A)}) \text{ under } H_0,
\label{eq:rmanova-f}
\end{equation}
which specializes, for $a=b=2$, to a closed form. Writing the
interaction contrast as $\hat L = \bar\Delta_1 - \bar\Delta_2$, with
$\bar\Delta_g$ the stratum-$g$ mean of each participant's
phase-1-minus-phase-2 difference of within-phase means $\Delta_{gi}$,
for which $\mathrm{Var}(\Delta_{gi}) = 2\sigma_\varepsilon^2/T$ and
$\mathrm{Var}(\hat L) = 4\sigma_\varepsilon^2/(nT)$,
\begin{equation}
F_{AB} = \frac{nT\,\hat L^2}{4\,\hat\sigma_\varepsilon^2}, \qquad
  t_{AB} = \sqrt{\tfrac{nT}{4}}\cdot\frac{\hat L}{\hat\sigma_\varepsilon},
  \qquad t_{AB}^2 = F_{AB}, \qquad df_1 = 1,\ df_2 = 2(n-1).
\label{eq:rmanova-f-22}
\end{equation}
The $2\times2$ interaction $F$-test is therefore the ordinary pooled
two-sample $t$-test on the within-participant differences
$\Delta_{gi}$. Because that $t$-test needs only that the $\Delta_{gi}$
be iid normal with a common variance in the two strata, the identity
and the exactness of the test hold for \emph{any} within-participant
covariance, AR(1) included, once each phase is reduced to its mean
(\code{docs/26}, Section 5b; derived). Under AR(1) errors the variance
of $\Delta_{gi}$ is no longer $2\sigma_\varepsilon^2/T$, so the power
formula below must use the actual variance of the phase difference.
Power follows the noncentral $F$ distribution with noncentrality
\begin{equation}
\Lambda = \frac{nT\,L_1^2}{4\sigma_\varepsilon^2}, \qquad \pi =
  \Pr\!\big[F_{AB} > F_{1,\,2(n-1),\,1-\alpha} \mid \Lambda\big],
\label{eq:rmanova-power}
\end{equation}
where $L_1$ is the value of the contrast under $H_1$, an effect size
on the raw outcome scale (\code{docs/26}, eqs.~17--18). The variance
identities and the nominal size of the pooled $t$ at $df_2 = 2(n-1)$
were also checked by a short simulation (\code{check-existing-sections.R}:
$n = 30$, $T = 3$; simulated $\mathrm{Var}(\hat L) = 0.0433$ against
0.0444, rejection rate 0.050 over 2{,}000 replicates).

\note{2026-10-08: corrected and renamed. This subsection previously
used Paper 08's classical symbols $\alpha_i$, $\beta_j$, $\pi_{l(i)}$,
$e_{ijlk}$, $\lambda$ and $1-\beta$, which collide with the canonical
$\alpha$, $\pi$ and $\lambda$; Paper 08 itself still uses them, with
$\Delta_0$ for the effect size, written $L_1$ here as in
\code{docs/26}. It also described the error as satisfying
``compound-symmetric sphericity'', conflating the two conditions; Paper
08 still states that sphericity means a compound-symmetric
within-subject covariance. Finally, it repeated Paper 08's claim that
this is the compendium's only fully analytic power result. That no
longer holds: the restated Paper 01 (Appendix A.1--A.2) and
\code{docs/37} give the moments and noncentral-$t$ power of the
paired-difference statistic E9 in closed form under both coupling
rules. GEE, Mancl--DeRouen and the LME/\code{corCAR1} model are still
evaluated by simulation, with the sandwich/CR2 machinery of
\S\ref{sec:sandwich} providing the small-sample correction.}

\subsection{RM-ANOVA: the sum-of-squares decomposition, sphericity, and
sample size}
\label{sec:invb-rmanova-ss}

This subsection completes \S\ref{sec:rmanova} in the same local
symbols: stratum $g \le a$, phase $j \le b$, participant $i \le n$
within stratum, within-phase time $t \le T$, and a dot for averaging
over a subscript. (Source: \code{docs/26}, Sections 4 to 7.)

\textbf{Sums of squares.} The between-participant and the
phase-related within-participant terms of the balanced split-plot
decomposition, with their degrees of freedom, are
\begin{align}
SS_A &= bnT \sum_{g} (\bar Y_{g\cdot\cdot\cdot} -
  \bar Y_{\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot})^2,
  & df_A &= a - 1, \notag \\
SS_{S(A)} &= bT \sum_{g,i} (\bar Y_{g\cdot i\cdot} -
  \bar Y_{g\cdot\cdot\cdot})^2,
  & df_{S(A)} &= a(n - 1), \notag \\
SS_B &= anT \sum_{j} (\bar Y_{\cdot j\cdot\cdot} -
  \bar Y_{\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot})^2,
  & df_B &= b - 1, \notag \\
SS_{AB} &= nT \sum_{g,j} (\bar Y_{gj\cdot\cdot} -
  \bar Y_{g\cdot\cdot\cdot} - \bar Y_{\cdot j\cdot\cdot} +
  \bar Y_{\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot})^2,
  & df_{AB} &= (a-1)(b-1), \notag \\
SS_{B\times S(A)} &= T \sum_{g,j,i} (\bar Y_{gji\cdot} -
  \bar Y_{g\cdot i\cdot} - \bar Y_{gj\cdot\cdot} +
  \bar Y_{g\cdot\cdot\cdot})^2,
  & df_{B\times S(A)} &= a(n-1)(b-1),
\label{eq:invb-ss}
\end{align}
and the interaction test of Equation~\eqref{eq:rmanova-f} is
\begin{equation}
F_{AB} = \frac{SS_{AB}/[(a-1)(b-1)]}{SS_{B\times S(A)}/[a(n-1)(b-1)]}
  \sim F\big((a-1)(b-1),\, a(n-1)(b-1)\big) \ \text{under } H_0
\label{eq:invb-fab-general}
\end{equation}
and sphericity of the phase factor, which holds automatically when
$b = 2$. Under Model~\eqref{eq:rmanova-model} and balance,
$E[MS_{B\times S(A)}] = \sigma_\varepsilon^2$, because each
participant's phase means have residual variance
$\sigma_\varepsilon^2/T$ and the factor $T$ rescales it (derived). For
$a = b = 2$, the sum-to-zero interaction estimates are
$\widehat{(\xi\phi)}_{gj} = \pm\hat L/4$, so
\begin{equation}
SS_{AB} = nT \cdot 4\,(\hat L/4)^2 = \frac{nT\,\hat L^2}{4},
\label{eq:invb-ssab-22}
\end{equation}
which with $df_{AB} = 1$ and $df_{B\times S(A)} = 2(n-1)$ gives
Equation~\eqref{eq:rmanova-f-22}. (Derived. Every sum of squares, the
degrees of freedom and the $F$ ratio agree with \code{aov()} fitted
with error strata \code{subj/\allowbreak(phase*time)}, for
$(a, b, n, T)$ equal to $(2, 2, 10, 3)$ and to $(3, 3, 8, 4)$; the
estimate $\widehat{(\xi\phi)}_{11}$ equals $\hat L/4$ to machine
precision; \code{check-gompertz-rmanova-cycles.R}.)

\textbf{The time-resolved test.} Whether the biomarker modifies the
treatment effect differently over within-phase time is the three-way
term $(\xi\phi\upsilon)_{gjt}$, tested by
\begin{equation}
F_{AB\upsilon} = \frac{MS_{AB\upsilon}}{MS_{B\upsilon\times S(A)}}, \qquad
df_1 = (a-1)(b-1)(T-1), \quad df_2 = a(n-1)(b-1)(T-1)
\label{eq:invb-fabt}
\end{equation}
(classical split-plot; the degrees of freedom agree with the
\code{subj:phase:time} stratum of \code{aov()}, verified). Its
within-participant factor has $T$ levels, so for $T > 2$ the exactness
of \S\ref{sec:rmanova} does not carry over and sphericity is required.

\textbf{Sphericity and Box's measure.} For a within-participant factor
with $m$ levels and covariance $\Sigma$, let $C$ be an $m \times (m-1)$
matrix of orthonormal contrasts and $S = C^\top \Sigma C$. Box's
measure is
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_{\mathrm{Box}} = \frac{(\operatorname{tr} S)^2}
  {(m-1)\operatorname{tr}(S^2)}, \qquad
  \frac{1}{m-1} \le \epsilon_{\mathrm{Box}} \le 1,
\label{eq:invb-box}
\end{equation}
with $\epsilon_{\mathrm{Box}} = 1$ if and only if $S \propto I$
(sphericity) and the lower bound attained when $S$ has rank one
(derived, Cauchy--Schwarz on the eigenvalues of $S$). Sphericity is
equivalent to all pairwise differences of levels having equal
variance. Under AR(1), $\mathrm{Var}(y_j - y_k) = 2\sigma^2(1 -
\rho^{|j-k|})$ varies with $|j - k|$ once $m \ge 3$, so
$\epsilon_{\mathrm{Box}} < 1$ for every $0 < \rho < 1$, while $m = 2$
is always spherical (derived). Table~\ref{tab:invb-box} gives values at
unit spacing. The Greenhouse--Geisser correction multiplies both
degrees of freedom by $\hat\epsilon_{\mathrm{Box}}$; the Huynh--Feldt
variant is less conservative and is the conventional choice when
$\hat\epsilon_{\mathrm{Box}} > 0.75$ (Source: \code{docs/26},
Section 6; the 0.75 threshold is a rule of thumb).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering\small
\caption{Box's $\epsilon_{\mathrm{Box}}$ under AR(1) at unit spacing,
and its lower bound (computed here, \code{check-gompertz-rmanova-cycles.R}; compound
symmetry gives 1 at every $m$).}
\label{tab:invb-box}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrr@{}}
\toprule
$m$ & $\rho = 0.5$ & $\rho = 0.8$ & $1/(m-1)$ \\
\midrule
2 & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 \\
3 & 0.925 & 0.849 & 0.500 \\
4 & 0.859 & 0.725 & 0.333 \\
8 & 0.722 & 0.469 & 0.143 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\note{\code{docs/26} states the range of $\epsilon_{\mathrm{Box}}$ and
that it is strictly below 1 under AR(1) for more than two levels, but
gives neither the formula nor values; Equation~\eqref{eq:invb-box} and
Table~\ref{tab:invb-box} are supplied here.}

\textbf{Sample size.} Inverting the noncentrality of
Equation~\eqref{eq:rmanova-power},
\begin{equation}
n = \frac{4\sigma_\varepsilon^2\,\Lambda}{T L_1^2},
\label{eq:invb-rmanova-n}
\end{equation}
per stratum, where $\Lambda$ must deliver the target power at
$df_2 = 2(n-1)$. Because the critical value depends on $n$, the
equation is solved by iteration (derived; Source: \code{docs/26},
Section 7, eq.~19). For 80\% power at $\alpha = 0.05$: $L_1 /
\sigma_\varepsilon = 1$ needs $n = 33$ per stratum at $T = 1$
($\Lambda = 8.25$) and $n = 9$ at $T = 4$; $L_1/\sigma_\varepsilon =
0.5$ needs $n = 127$ at $T = 1$ (computed here by direct search over
$n$ with the noncentral $F$, \code{check-gompertz-rmanova-cycles.R}). Under AR(1)
errors $\sigma_\varepsilon^2/T$ must be replaced by the actual
variance of a within-phase mean, as in \S\ref{sec:rmanova}.

\textbf{Relation to the mixed-model test.} \code{docs/26} (Section 9)
argues, without derivation, that the strict RM-ANOVA $F$ and the Wald
$t$ on \code{bm:Dbc} are asymptotically equivalent when the biomarker
enters as the stratum indicator, the within-participant covariance is
exactly compound-symmetric, and the drug indicator is binary (E1),
and that the mixed model otherwise uses more information (argued).

\subsection{The efficiency cost of dichotomizing the biomarker}
\label{sec:inva-dichot}

The $2\times2$ RM-ANOVA of Equation~\eqref{eq:rmanova-f-22} is a
two-sample $t$-test of the within-participant contrast $\Delta_i$
across a median split of the biomarker, whereas E9
(Equation~\eqref{eq:e9-ols}) regresses $\Delta_i$ on the continuous
biomarker. For a normal biomarker and $\Delta_i = \beta b_i + e_i$, the
split indicator $x_i = \mathbf 1(b_i > 0)$ has
$\mathrm{Cov}(b_i, x_i) = \varphi(0)$ and $\mathrm{Var}(x_i) = 1/4$, so
\begin{equation}
\frac{\mathrm{Corr}(\Delta_i, x_i)}{\mathrm{Corr}(\Delta_i, b_i)}
  = 2\varphi(0) = \sqrt{2/\pi} \approx 0.798,
\qquad
\text{relative efficiency} = \frac{2}{\pi} \approx 0.637,
\label{eq:inva-dichot}
\end{equation}
when the moderation is small relative to the residual variance: the
dichotomized test has about 0.80 of the noncentrality and needs about
$\pi/2 \approx 1.57$ times the sample size (derived; a 4,000-replicate
simulation at $N = 200$ gave a mean $t$-ratio of 0.798, block I).
(Source: \code{docs/23}, Section~13.1, items 2 and 6, from
\code{docs/17}, which state the 36 percent loss and the 0.64 relative
efficiency without derivation.) The constant is the one of
Equation~\eqref{eq:pd-threshold} for the reverse situation, a threshold
moderation analyzed with a linear term. \note{\code{docs/17} adds that
sphericity corrections reduce RM-ANOVA power further; for $a = b = 2$
the within-participant factor has two levels and is always spherical
(\S\ref{sec:rmanova}), so no correction applies in that case.}

\subsection{A general decomposition of realized Type~I error}
\label{sec:calibration}

Paper 10, written in deliberately general Wald-test notation
(applicable to any fixed-effect interaction coefficient, not only
$\beta_{bm:D}$), decomposes an estimator into a standardized component:
\begin{equation}
\hat\theta = \mu + \sigma Z^\ast, \qquad \mu = E(\hat\theta), \qquad
  \sigma = \mathrm{SD}(\hat\theta), \qquad Z^\ast =
  \frac{\hat\theta - \mu}{\sigma},
\label{eq:theta-decomp}
\end{equation}
and rewrites the Wald statistic $T = \hat\theta/\widehat{\mathrm{SE}}$
in terms of two dimensionless channels,
\begin{equation}
T \approx \frac{\mu + \sigma Z^\ast}{\kappa\sigma} =
  \frac{1}{\kappa}\big(Z^\ast + \delta\big), \qquad
  \kappa = \frac{\overline{\widehat{\mathrm{SE}}}}{\sigma}, \qquad
  \delta = \frac{\mu}{\sigma}.
\label{eq:theta-wald}
\end{equation}
Realized Type~I error follows directly:
\begin{equation}
\alpha_{\mathrm{real}} = \Pr\big(|T| > t_{\nu,1-\alpha/2}\big) \approx
  \Pr\big(|Z^\ast + \delta| > \kappa\, t_{\nu,1-\alpha/2}\big),
\label{eq:theta-alpha}
\end{equation}
and exactly \emph{four} channels can move it away from nominal: a
standardized bias $\delta$, a scale-distortion $\kappa$ (model-based
SE too large or small relative to the true sampling SD), a
degrees-of-freedom channel $\nu$, and the shape of $Z^\ast$'s
distribution (departures from normality). Calibration is recovered
exactly when $\delta=0$, $\kappa=1$, $\nu=\infty$, and $Z^\ast\sim
\mathcal N(0,1)$. With the bias and reference-distribution channels inert, this
reduces to a closed form in $\kappa$ alone,
\begin{equation}
\alpha_{\mathrm{real}} \approx 2\,\Phi\big(-\kappa\, t_{\nu,1-\alpha/2}\big)
  \;\approx\; 0.05 - 0.23\,(\kappa - 1) \ \text{near } \kappa=1,
\label{eq:theta-closed}
\end{equation}
with elasticity $d\log\alpha_{\mathrm{real}}/d\log\kappa \approx -4.6$
at $\kappa=1$ (both constants verified numerically,
\code{check-existing-sections.R}: slope $-0.229$, elasticity $-4.58$): a small
overstatement of the model-based standard error produces a
disproportionate fall in realized Type~I error. Paper 10 uses this to
explain why ordinary least squares, which ignores the participant
random effect, is strongly \emph{conservative} for this within-
participant interaction (standard-error ratio near 1.3), not
anti-conservative as the familiar warning about ignored clustering
suggests. OLS charges a within-participant contrast the full residual
variance, and in the idealized case of a pure within-participant
contrast with exchangeable errors its scale-distortion factor is
\begin{equation}
\kappa_{\mathrm{OLS}}^2 \approx \frac{\sigma_u^2 +
  \sigma_\varepsilon^2}{\sigma_\varepsilon^2},
\label{eq:kappa-ols}
\end{equation}
which exceeds one by the ratio $\sigma_u^2/\sigma_\varepsilon^2$ of
random-intercept to residual variance. The anti-conservative case
(the Moulton pitfall) concerns between-cluster effects.

\note{2026-10-08: corrected. This paragraph previously stated that the
OLS analysis is anti-conservative, which contradicts both
Equation~\eqref{eq:kappa-ols} ($\kappa > 1$ is conservative) and Paper
10, Section on the working-covariance ladder; it also said the excess
over one is ``exactly the random-intercept variance'', where it is
that variance relative to $\sigma_\varepsilon^2$. Paper 10 writes the
random-intercept variance as $\sigma_b^2$; the canonical symbol is
$\sigma_u^2$, since $b_i$ is the standardized biomarker.}

Paper 02 gives the compendium's operational estimator of $\kappa$
directly on the interaction coefficient, using the notation
$\hat\beta_{bm:X}$ for the fitted coefficient under carryover
specification $X$:
\begin{equation}
\kappa = \frac{\overline{\widehat{SE}}}{\mathrm{SD}(\hat\beta_{bm:X})}
  = \frac{\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{sim}}}\sum_{r=1}^{n_{\mathrm{sim}}}
  \widehat{SE}_r}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{sim}}-1}
  \sum_{r=1}^{n_{\mathrm{sim}}} (\hat\beta_{bm:X,r} -
  \bar{\hat\beta}_{bm:X})^2}},
\label{eq:kappa-estimator}
\end{equation}
and the calibrated true value against which bias is computed
throughout the carryover-specification manuscript,
\begin{equation}
\theta_{\mathrm{true}} = -c_{bm}\,\sigma_{BR} = -3.6
  \quad (c_{bm} = 0.45,\ \sigma_{BR} = 8).
\label{eq:theta-true}
\end{equation}
This is the general estimand $-c_{bm}\sigma_{BR}/\sigma_{bm}$ of Paper
01 with $\sigma_{bm} = 1$, the biomarker SD in Paper 02's driver
(\code{default\_baseline\_param()} in
\code{analysis/\allowbreak scripts/\allowbreak
carryover-sensitivity/\allowbreak simulation-core.R},
inspected). It is the target of the exposure-weighted specification
only; E1 and E3 estimate the coefficient on the binary $D_{it}$, so
their bias against $\theta_{\mathrm{true}}$ reflects an estimand
mismatch (Paper 02, headline table).

\subsection{What a random intercept absorbs under AR(1)}
\label{sec:inva-ri}

Let the within-participant deviations have variance $\sigma_T^2$ and
correlation $\rho^{h}$ at lag $h$ (weeks). A working model with a random
intercept of variance $\sigma_u^2 < \sigma_T^2$ and independent
residuals assigns the remainder $\Sigma - \sigma_u^2 J$ to the
residual, with variance $\sigma_\varepsilon^2 = \sigma_T^2 - \sigma_u^2$
and correlation
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Corr}(\varepsilon_{it}, \varepsilon_{is}) =
  \frac{\sigma_T^2\rho^{h} - \sigma_u^2}{\sigma_\varepsilon^2}
  = \rho^{h} - \big(1 - \rho^{h}\big)\frac{\sigma_u^2}{\sigma_\varepsilon^2},
  \qquad h = |w_t - w_s|,
\label{eq:inva-ri}
\end{equation}
positive when $\rho^{h} > \sigma_u^2/\sigma_T^2$ (nearby visits) and
negative beyond (derived; verified, \code{check-historical-results.R}, block E;
Source: \code{docs/23}, Section~4.3, which corrected an earlier form
without the rescaling by $\sigma_\varepsilon^2$). A random intercept
therefore absorbs only the long-lag part of AR(1) correlation, and a
model that treats the remainder as independent misstates the variance of
any contrast between visits. \note{\code{docs/23} concludes that such a
model ``underestimates standard errors''. The direction depends on the
contrast: for contiguous on and off blocks the AR(1) contrast variance
exceeds the compound-symmetry one (ratio 3.25 at $p = q = 4$,
Equation~\eqref{eq:bp-var}), so the standard error is understated and
the test anti-conservative, as in the quoted \code{lmer} crossover sizes
of 0.13 to 0.17 (200 replicates; central 95\% binomial range
$[0.020, 0.080]$ at size 0.05, verified, block F); for interleaved
designs the ratio is smaller (Section~\ref{sec:bp-design}). This is
argued from Equation~\eqref{eq:bp-var}, not computed for each design.}

\note{A related claim in \code{docs/23}, Section~13.1, item 5 (from
\code{docs/17}), that ordinary least squares inflates Type~I error to
about 15 to 25 percent at an intraclass correlation of 0.3 with $T = 8$,
holds for a \emph{between}-participant effect: the design effect is
$1 + (T - 1)\,0.3 = 3.1$, giving a realized size of
$2\Phi(-1.96/\sqrt{3.1}) = 0.266$ (verified, block K). For the
within-participant interaction $\beta_{bm:D}$ OLS is conservative,
Equation~\eqref{eq:kappa-ols}.}

\subsection{Cluster-robust sandwich variance and the CR2 correction}
\label{sec:sandwich}

The naive (Liang--Zeger) sandwich estimator, clustering on participant,
\begin{equation}
\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathrm{sandwich}}(\hat\beta) =
  \Big(\textstyle\sum_i X_i' \hat V_i^{-1} X_i\Big)^{-1}
  \Big(\textstyle\sum_i X_i' \hat V_i^{-1} \hat e_i \hat e_i'
  \hat V_i^{-1} X_i\Big)
  \Big(\textstyle\sum_i X_i' \hat V_i^{-1} X_i\Big)^{-1},
\label{eq:sandwich}
\end{equation}
is downward-biased at moderate cluster counts. Paper 10 gives the
general CR family in the GLS-transformed metric,
\begin{equation}
\hat V_{\mathrm{CR}} = (\tilde X'\tilde X)^{-1}
  \Big(\textstyle\sum_i \tilde X_i' A_i \tilde e_i \tilde e_i' A_i
  \tilde X_i\Big) (\tilde X'\tilde X)^{-1},
\label{eq:cr-family}
\end{equation}
indexed by the adjustment matrix $A_i$: $A_i = I$ (CR0, unadjusted);
$A_i = (I-H_{ii})^{-1}$ (the jackknife form, equal to the
Mancl--DeRouen correction in the marginal/GEE setting); and
\begin{equation}
A_i = (I - H_{ii})^{-1/2} \qquad \text{(CR2)},
\label{eq:cr2}
\end{equation}
the symmetric square root that renders $\hat V_{\mathrm{CR}}$ exactly
unbiased when the working covariance is correctly specified, the
cluster generalization of the HC2 estimator of MacKinnon and White.
Its Satterthwaite degrees of freedom are obtained by matching the
first two moments of a weighted sum of $\chi^2_1$ variables with
weights $\ell_g$, the eigenvalues of the relevant quadratic form,
\begin{equation}
\nu = \frac{\big(\sum_g \ell_g\big)^2}{\sum_g \ell_g^2}.
\label{eq:cr2-df}
\end{equation}
\note{2026-10-08: the eigenvalues were written $\lambda_g$; $\lambda$ is
the canonical carryover decay rate, so they are written $\ell_g$.}

Paper 02's null-cell diagnostics find $\kappa$ from $1.05$ to $1.10$
across specifications (empirical Type~I error $0.029$ to $0.039$
against nominal $0.05$) for the model-based standard errors, and
$\kappa$ from $1.07$ to $1.26$ in the information-rich cells of its
block S6, restored to about one by CR2. These values are consistent,
via Equation~\eqref{eq:theta-closed} (which gives 0.040 at
$\kappa = 1.05$ and 0.031 at $\kappa = 1.10$; verified numerically),
with the mild conservatism of the compendium's model-based Wald tests.
Paper 02 attributes it to the mismatch between the random-intercept
plus \code{corCAR1} working covariance and the covariance the
data-generating process induces. The conservatism is not general: in
the restated Paper 01's stress test (configuration C at $\rho = 0.7$,
the covariance construct of the refined process), the
published random-intercept analysis with model-based inference rejected
at 0.069 pooled over 11 non-prognostic null cells (0.081 at $N = 35$),
and analyses sharing the biomarker effect with baseline were
anti-conservative when the biomarker was also prognostic (Paper 01,
Results).

%=============================================================
\section{The stress-test construct and its fourteen analyses}
\label{sec:invd-stress}
%=============================================================

This section states as model formulas the data-generating process and
the fourteen analyses of the exploratory stress test of the Hybrid
design (Sources: \code{docs/46}, Sections 4 and 5 and Amendments 1 and
2; \code{docs/47}; \code{docs/43}, Section 3.9), read against the
driver \code{11-strawman-\allowbreak stress-\allowbreak test.R} in
\code{analysis/\allowbreak scripts/\allowbreak quick-sim/\allowbreak
carryover-closed-form/} (inspected 2026-10-08). The selection criteria are in
\S\ref{sec:invd-criteria}. Local symbols: $B^{c}_i = B_i - \bar B$, the
biomarker centered at the trial's sample mean (code \code{bmc});
$X_{it}$, the analysis exposure regressor; $\iota_t = \mathbf 1\{t =
0\}$ (code \code{bl0}); $k(t) \in \{0, \mathrm{OL}, \mathrm{bl}\}$, the
phase of visit $t$ (baseline, open label, blinded); $\rho_a$, the
working CAR(1) parameter; $\sigma_\varepsilon$, $\sigma_{\varepsilon,k}$, working residual
standard deviations; $\mathcal T_a$, the grid of analysis half-lives.

\subsection{The data-generating process}
\label{sec:invd-dgp}

Visits are at weeks $w \in \{0, 4, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 16, 20\}$, $t = 0$
being baseline, on the four Hybrid paths of Table~\ref{tab:cg-paths},
with $N_p = \lfloor N/4 \rfloor + \mathbf 1\{p \le N \bmod 4\}$ (18, 18,
17, 17 at $N = 70$). For participant $i$ on path $p$,
\begin{equation}
(B_i, \mathrm{BL}_i, TV_{i\cdot}, PB_{i\cdot}, BR_{i\cdot})
  \sim \mathcal N\big(\mu_p,\ S_p R_p S_p\big),
\label{eq:invd-joint}
\end{equation}
with $\mu_{bm} = 124.33$, $\sigma_{bm} = 15.36$, $\mu_{BL} = 83.07$,
$\sigma_{BL} = 18.48$; $\sigma_{TV} = 10$, $\sigma_{PB,t} = 10\eta_t$
and $\sigma_{BR} = 8$ at every visit; $E(TV_{it}) = G_{TV}(w_t)$ and
$E(PB_{it}) = \eta_t G_{TV}(t_{pb,t})$, with $G_{TV}$ the modified
Gompertz curve of Equation~\eqref{eq:gompertz-05} at $(m,
d, r) = (6.506, 5, 0.35)$; and $E(BR_{it})$
from $G_{BR}(t_{od,t})$ at $(10.986, 5, 0.42)$ with the anchored
carryover of Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-anchor}
($\mu_{BR}^{\mathrm{anch}}$ form; Paper 01, Refinement 3),
$\mu_{BR,t} = G_{BR}(t_{od,t}) + \mu_{BR,\mathrm{last}}\,\phi(t_{sd,t})$
off drug after exposure. The correlation matrix $R_p$ is Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-R}
with the response block $M = K \otimes A$ (configuration C, $\rho =
0.7$, $c_1 = 0.2$) or $M_{\mathrm{CS}}$ (Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-cs-decomp},
$\rho_{cs} = 0.8$, $c_1 = 0.2$, $c_\times = 0.1$), and the biomarker row
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
\mathrm{Cor}(B_i, BR_{it}) = c_{bm}\, g_t, \qquad
\mathrm{Cor}(B_i, TV_{it}) = c_{bm,TV}, \\
\mathrm{Cor}(B_i, PB_{it}) = c_{bm,PB}, \qquad
\mathrm{Cor}(B_i, \mathrm{BL}_i) = c_{bm,BL},
\end{gathered}
\label{eq:invd-bmrow}
\end{equation}
where $g_t$ is graded coupling (Equation~\eqref{eq:cor-carryover}) with
$\phi$ exponential or Weibull of shape $k \in \{0.5, 2\}$
(Equation~\eqref{eq:decay-weib}), the same $\phi$ as in the mean. A cell
whose $R_p$ is not positive definite stops; none did. The outcome is
\begin{equation}
Y_{i0} = \mathrm{BL}_i, \qquad
Y_{it} = \mathrm{BL}_i - \big(TV_{it} + \beta_{bm}^{TV}\sigma_{TV}\, b_i\,
  \tilde G_{TV}(w_t) + PB_{it} + BR_{it}\big) + \varepsilon^{\mathrm{me}}_{it},
\quad t \ge 1,
\label{eq:invd-outcome}
\end{equation}
with $b_i = (B_i - \mu_{bm})/\sigma_{bm}$,
$\tilde G_{TV} = G_{TV}/\max_t G_{TV}(w_t)$, and
$\varepsilon^{\mathrm{me}}_{it} \sim \mathcal N(0, s_{k(t)}^2)$ independent,
$s_{\mathrm{OL}} = 3$ and $s_{\mathrm{bl}} = 6$ in the measurement-error
cell and zero otherwise. Dropout is monotone, MCAR or MAR as in
Equation~\eqref{eq:invf-mar} of \S\ref{sec:invf-mar} (inspected). The
41 cells vary one factor at a time from the base cell
(configuration C, $N = 70$, $t_{1/2} = 0.5$ week, exponential decay,
no dropout, no prognostic coupling): $c_{bm}$ along the two power
curves, measurement error, $N \in \{35, 150\}$, $t_{1/2} \in \{0, 1\}$,
Weibull shape, MCAR and MAR dropout, and one of $c_{bm,TV}$,
$\beta^{TV}_{bm}$, $c_{bm,PB}$, $c_{bm,BL}$ set to 0.3 (\code{docs/46},
Section 5). The slopes these pathways imply are those of
Table~\ref{tab:bp-pathways}, and the generalized ceiling is
Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-general}. At the base half-life of 0.5 week the
Hybrid ceiling is 0.518 under configuration C and 0.274 under compound
symmetry (verified, \code{check-stress-test-construct.R}, Section 1), so the curve
maxima 0.45 and 0.25 are feasible and 0.30 under compound symmetry is
not.

\note{\code{docs/46}, Section 3, bounds the compound-symmetry curve by
``that construct's ceiling of 0.256''; that is the value at
$t_{1/2} = 0$. At the curve's $t_{1/2} = 0.5$ week the graded ceiling
is 0.274; the grid and the exclusion of 0.30 are unaffected.}
\note{The base construct (configuration C, $\rho = 0.7$) keeps the
published compounding recursion on the drug-response mean, not the
anchored form of Equation~\eqref{eq:carryover-anchored}
(\code{docs/44}, Step 3; inspected), and allocates participants to
paths in fixed numbers. Configuration C is the covariance construct of
the refined process of Paper 01, but the stress test did not run the
refined process in full (inspected, \code{11-strawman-stress-test.R}).
Updated 2026-10-15: the driver now defaults to anchored carryover and
randomized allocation, and the test has been rerun in that form
(\code{docs/47}, section ``Rerun under the refined process''); the
figures in this part are from the stored run.}

\subsection{The fourteen analyses}
\label{sec:invd-analyses}

Every analysis fits a sub-model of
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Y_{it} = {} & \beta_0 + \beta_B B^{c}_i + \beta_T t + \beta_D X_{it} +
  \beta_{bm:D}\, B^{c}_i X_{it} \\
& + \underbrace{\beta_\eta \eta_t + \beta_{bm:\eta}\, B^{c}_i \eta_t}_{\text{F1, F1A, G1, G2}}
  + \underbrace{\beta_{bm:T}\, B^{c}_i t}_{\text{F2}}
  + \underbrace{\beta_{B,0}\, B^{c}_i \iota_t}_{\text{ES, EA}}
  + e_{it},
\qquad t \in \{0, 4, \dots, 20\},
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:invd-mean}
\end{equation}
over all nine rows, baseline included, and tests $H_0\colon
\beta_{bm:D} = 0$ two-sided at $\alpha = 0.05$. The regressor $X_{it}$
is $D_{bc,it}$ of Equation~\eqref{eq:dbc} at an assumed half-life
$t_{1/2}^{a}$, with $\lambda = \ln 2/t_{1/2}^{a}$, zero at baseline and
before exposure, and $X_{it} = D_{it}$ when $t_{1/2}^{a} = 0$. The
working covariances of $\mathbf Y_i$, with $u_i$ the random intercept
where present, are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\text{RC:}\quad & \mathrm{Cov}(Y_{it}, Y_{is}) = \sigma_u^2 +
  \sigma_\varepsilon^2\, \rho_a^{|t - s|}, \\
\text{RCH:}\quad & \mathrm{Cov}(Y_{it}, Y_{is}) = \sigma_u^2 +
  \sigma_{\varepsilon,k(t)}\,\sigma_{\varepsilon,k(s)}\, \rho_a^{|t - s|}, \qquad
  k \in \{0, \mathrm{OL}, \mathrm{bl}\}, \\
\text{US:}\quad & \mathrm{Cov}(\mathbf Y_i) = \Sigma, \text{ unrestricted
  over the nine visits}, \\
\text{CS:}\quad & \mathrm{Cov}(Y_{it}, Y_{is}) = \sigma^2\big[(1 -
  r)\mathbf 1\{t = s\} + r\big],
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:invd-cov}
\end{equation}
RC being \code{nlme::lme} with \code{random = \textasciitilde 1 | ptID}
and \code{corCAR1(form = \textasciitilde t | ptID)} on weeks, RCH the
same with \code{varIdent(form = \textasciitilde 1 | phase)}, and US
and CS the \code{mmrm} structures \code{us(visit | ptID)} and
\code{cs(visit | ptID)} without a random intercept.
Table~\ref{tab:invd-analyses} lists the analyses.

$\beta_0, \beta_B, \beta_T, \beta_D, \beta_{bm:D}$. $p$, $q$: numbers
of fixed-effect and covariance parameters. AIC: $t^{a}_{1/2}$ chosen
over $\mathcal T_a = \{0, 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2\}$ weeks
(\S\ref{sec:invd-aic}).}
\label{tab:invd-analyses}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llllllcc@{}}
\toprule
ID & Terms & $t^{a}_{1/2}$ & Covariance & Inference & Status & $p$ & $q$ \\
\midrule
S   & core only & 0.5 & RC  & CR2 & protocol (strawman) & 5 & 3 \\
DS  & core only & AIC & RC  & CR2 & protocol; reference, Amend.~1 & 5 & 3 \\
C1  & core only & 0.5 & RCH & CR2 & protocol & 5 & 5 \\
C2  & core only & 0.5 & RCH & model-based & protocol & 5 & 5 \\
F1  & $\eta_t$, $B^{c}_i\eta_t$ & 0.5 & RC & CR2 & protocol; selected & 7 & 3 \\
F2  & $B^{c}_i t$ & 0.5 & RC & CR2 & protocol & 6 & 3 \\
ES  & $B^{c}_i\iota_t$ & 0.5 & RC & CR2 & protocol & 6 & 3 \\

DA  & core only & AIC & US & Kenward--Roger & protocol & 5 & 45 \\
EA  & $B^{c}_i\iota_t$ & 0.5 & US & Kenward--Roger & protocol & 6 & 45 \\
R   & core only & 0 ($X = D_{it}$) & CS & Satterthwaite & protocol (published ref.) & 5 & 2 \\
G1  & $\eta_t$, $B^{c}_i\eta_t$ & 0.5 & RCH & CR2 & post hoc & 7 & 5 \\
G2  & $\eta_t$, $B^{c}_i\eta_t$ & 0.5 & RCH & model-based & post hoc & 7 & 5 \\

\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbf{Inference.} CR2 is Equation~\eqref{eq:cr2} with the
Satterthwaite degrees of freedom of Equation~\eqref{eq:cr2-df}
(\code{clubSandwich::coef\_test}, clustered on participant).
Model-based is the \code{nlme} Wald $t$ on its between-within
(containment) degrees of freedom; \code{nlme} offers no Satterthwaite
option for these models. Kenward--Roger is the small-sample covariance
adjustment and degrees of freedom of Kenward and Roger (1997) as
implemented in \code{mmrm} in its linear variant
(\code{Kenward-Roger-Linear}), which omits the second-derivative terms
of the covariance parameterization. Satterthwaite is the \code{mmrm}
model-based standard error with Satterthwaite degrees of freedom. The
covariance-parameter counts $q$ are those behind the tie rule of
\code{docs/46}, rule 5: F1 has 3 and G2 has 5 (inspected).

\note{\code{docs/47} describes \code{bmc} as the standardized biomarker;
it is centered but not scaled (inspected). Test statistics and
$p$-values are invariant to rescaling $B^{c}_i$, so no reported rate
depends on this, but $\beta_{bm:D}$ in these fits is per unit of the
biomarker, the raw-scale estimand of Equation~\eqref{eq:cg-estimand}.
\code{docs/46} describes R as a random intercept with model-based
inference; the driver fits the marginal compound-symmetry structure of
\code{mmrm} with Satterthwaite degrees of freedom, which equals a random
intercept with independent errors when $r \ge 0$ (inspected).}

\textbf{The expectancy terms and the drug indicator.} The collinearity
of $\eta_t$ with $D_{it}$ in the Hybrid design ($r = 0.588$ over the
nine rows, 0.507 over the post-baseline rows; verified,
\code{check-stress-test-construct.R}, Section 6) is reconciled with the variance
inflation factors quoted in the sources in
\S\ref{sec:invc-hybrid-construct}; \code{docs/40}, quoted in
\code{docs/43}, Section 3.1, gives it as the reason Hendrickson et al.\
dropped \code{De}. The biomarker block of F1, $B^{c}_i(1, X_{it},
\eta_t)$, is nonetheless identified, since the rows fall into four
groups (baseline; open label, on drug; blinded, on drug; blinded, off
drug) with distinct $(X, \eta)$ (derived). Its leak properties are
Equations~\eqref{eq:bp-plim} and~\eqref{eq:bp-f1-leak}.

the uncentered $B_i$ and \code{lmer}, baseline as a row.}

  \S\ref{sec:analysis} \\

  biomarker-by-time slope, confounded with natural course and
  expectancy \\
E4 & $X = D_{it}$ plus a main effect of $t_{sd,it}$
  (\code{simplecarryover}) & retired \\
D6 & random intercept, random post-baseline shift and random
  expectancy coefficient, residual variance by phase & proposed; G1, G2
  realize it with fixed expectancy terms and no random shift \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{table}[h]

the package's covariance vignette).}
\label{tab:invd-wcov}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lp{9.6cm}@{}}
\toprule

\midrule

\subsection{AIC selection of the analysis half-life}
\label{sec:invd-aic}

\noindent\textbf{Eight methods.} \code{docs/57} Section~4 describes
eight procedures for using a data-chosen half-life while controlling
size: F1A (select-then-test, liberal), F1A0 (test at the null-model
choice), F1A0B (parametric bootstrap; recommended), F1AB
(select-then-bootstrap-calibrated), and the paired alternatives DS,
DA, ES, EA. The section below covers AIC selection; full bootstrap
simulation (F1A0B, GP-15) is deferred.

DS, DA and F1A choose
\begin{equation}
\hat t^{a}_{1/2} = \operatorname*{arg\,min}_{h \in \mathcal T_a}
  \mathrm{AIC}_{\mathrm{ML}}(h), \qquad \mathcal T_a = \{0, 0.25, 0.5,
  1, 2\} \text{ weeks},
\label{eq:invd-aic}
\end{equation}
with the candidate models fitted by maximum likelihood, and refit the
selected model by REML for inference; $h = 0$ is the binary $D_{it}$
(\code{docs/46}, Section 4; inspected). The candidates differ only in
the values of the one regressor $X_{it}$, so they have the same number
of parameters and $\mathrm{AIC}_{\mathrm{ML}}(h) - \mathrm{AIC}_{\mathrm{ML}}(h')
= -2\{\ell(h) - \ell(h')\}$: the AIC rule is the maximum-likelihood
estimate of $t^{a}_{1/2}$ over the grid (derived). The reported test
then treats an estimated nonlinear parameter as known, the
post-selection problem noted for E7 at Equation~\eqref{eq:e7-aic}.
\note{The grid differs from E7's $\{0.25, 0.5, 1, 2\}$ of
Table~\ref{tab:especs} by including 0. The size excess the selection
produces (pooled 0.053 for DS, 0.057 for F1A, 0.065 for DA, against
0.048, 0.051 and 0.057 at the fixed 0.5 week; \code{docs/47},
Section 3) is simulated, not derived.}

\subsection{The implied outcome covariance and phase-specific
variances}
\label{sec:invd-phase}

Under configuration C ($\rho = 0.7$), with constant component standard
deviations within a phase, Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-outcome-cov} gives three variance
levels and one cross-phase factor. With $s_t = (10, 10\eta_t, 8)$ and
$q_{ts} = s_t^{\top}Ks_s$,
\begin{equation}
q_{\mathrm{OL}} = 368, \qquad q_{\mathrm{bl}} = 257, \qquad
q_{\mathrm{OL,bl}} = 300, \qquad
\frac{q_{\mathrm{OL,bl}}}{\sqrt{q_{\mathrm{OL}}\, q_{\mathrm{bl}}}} = 0.9755,
\label{eq:invd-q}
\end{equation}
so $\mathrm{Var}(Y_{it})$ takes the three values of
Table~\ref{tab:invc-outcome-cov} (341.6, 709.6 and 598.6; variances do
not depend on the correlation structure), plus 9 and 36 in the
measurement-error cell (derived; verified, \code{check-stress-test-construct.R},
Section 5). The factor 0.9755 confirms the correlation factor of 0.976
quoted from \code{docs/35} in \S\ref{sec:ce}
(Equation~\eqref{eq:ce-outcome-cov}). The same $q_{\mathrm{OL}}$ and
$q_{\mathrm{bl}}$ are the conditional variances of
Table~\ref{tab:invc-outcome-cov}, since conditioning on baseline
removes exactly $\sigma_{BL}^2$. Hence the RCH
structure of Equation~\eqref{eq:invd-cov} with $\sigma_u^2 =
\sigma_{BL}^2$, $\rho_a = \rho$, $\sigma_{\varepsilon,\mathrm{OL}}^2 = 368$,
$\sigma_{\varepsilon,\mathrm{bl}}^2 = 257$ and $\sigma_{\varepsilon,0} \to 0$ reproduces
every element of the construct's $9 \times 9$ outcome covariance except
the open-label by blinded pairs, which it overstates by the factor
$1/0.9755$ (largest discrepancy 5.3; verified). The baseline row has no
noise of its own (Proposition 5 of \S\ref{sec:bp-prog}), which RCH can
represent by $\sigma_{\varepsilon,0} \to 0$ and RC cannot (derived). With
measurement error the construct adds a white-noise term that neither
structure contains.

The leak factors of Equation~\eqref{eq:bp-plim} under this covariance
are in Table~\ref{tab:invd-leakC}. Under working independence the
computation reproduces the ``any, independence'' rows of
Table~\ref{tab:bp-leak} (S, ES, F1: 0.271, 0, $-0.020$ for the
constant natural course and 0.369, 0.239, 0 for the placebo pathway),
which
checks it; those rows are not repeated.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering\small
\caption{Spurious interaction per unit prognostic slope,
$\operatorname{plim}\hat\beta_{bm:D}/s_{\mathrm{prog}}$, in
configuration C ($\rho = 0.7$, Hybrid, $N = 70$, $X$ at
$t^{a}_{1/2} = 0.5$ week), by the mean model and the working covariance
$V$ (derived from Equation~\eqref{eq:bp-plim}; verified,
\code{check-stress-test-construct.R}, Section 5). ``True'' is the construct's
outcome covariance; ``RCH limit'' the structure described above.}
\label{tab:invd-leakC}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrrrr@{}}
\toprule
 & \multicolumn{3}{c}{Natural course, constant} &
   \multicolumn{3}{c}{Placebo, $\propto\eta_t$} \\
Working $V$ & S & ES & F1 & S & ES & F1 \\
\midrule
true         & 0.629 & 0 & 0.162 & 0.423 & 0.070 & 0 \\
RCH limit    & 0.628 & 0 & 0.172 & 0.420 & 0.069 & 0 \\

\end{table}

\noindent A working covariance that can approach the truth (RCH in C1,
C2, G1, G2; US in A, DA, EA) makes the leak of the shared baseline
slope larger, not smaller: 0.63 for S's mean model under the
constant natural-course pathway, against 0.27 under independence. The
stress-test null rejection rates in that cell order the same way (S
0.099, C1 0.382, A 0.416; G1 0.055 and G2 0.062 against F1 0.047;
\code{docs/47}, Section 3), which supports, but does not prove, this
account of why the phase-variance models fail the prognostic cells
(argued). It also gives content to the statement of \code{docs/50} that
the phase-variance models ``weight the shared baseline slope heavily'':
a near-zero baseline residual variance gives the baseline rows large
weight in $\hat\beta_B$. \note{The factor under the RC fit itself was
not computed for configuration C, because the converged RC parameters
for this construct are not stored; Table~\ref{tab:bp-leak} gives it for
the published process (0.276). Rejection rates also depend on the
standard errors, which this calculation does not address.}

\subsection{Sign symmetry of power}
\label{sec:invd-symmetry}

Suppose the biomarker enters the data-generating process only through
its correlation with $BR$, the cells without prognostic coupling, and
is normal (more generally, symmetric about its mean). Let
$B^{\ast}_i = 2\mu_{bm} - B_i$. It has the law of $B_i$, and its
covariance with every other variable changes sign: with
$D_{\mathrm r} = \operatorname{diag}(-1, 1, \dots, 1)$ acting on the
biomarker coordinate of the joint covariance $\Sigma$ of
Equation~\eqref{eq:invd-joint},
\begin{equation}
\Sigma(-c_{bm}) = D_{\mathrm r}\,\Sigma(c_{bm})\,D_{\mathrm r},
\label{eq:invf-reflect}
\end{equation}
so by joint normality the law of $(B^{\ast}_i, \text{the rest})$ under
$c_{bm}$ is that of $(B_i, \text{the rest})$ under $-c_{bm}$, with the
outcomes and the dropout indicators unchanged. Every analysis of
Table~\ref{tab:invd-analyses} uses the biomarker only through columns
linear in $B^{c}_i$, and $B^{\ast c}_i = -B^{c}_i$ whatever the
centering; negating those columns negates their coefficients and
leaves fitted values, likelihood, working covariance, every standard
error (model-based, CR2 or Kenward--Roger), the degrees of freedom and
the two-sided $p$-value unchanged. Hence
\begin{equation}
\hat\beta_{bm:D}(-c_{bm}) \overset{d}{=} -\hat\beta_{bm:D}(c_{bm}),
\qquad \pi(-c_{bm}) = \pi(c_{bm}),
\label{eq:invd-symmetry}
\end{equation}
for every analysis, unadjusted and size-adjusted, since both signs share
the null cell (derived; Source: \code{docs/48}, Section 4, cited by
\code{docs/46}, Amendment 1, and \code{docs/47}, Section 5). Checks:
Equation~\eqref{eq:invf-reflect} holds to machine precision for the
Hybrid compound-symmetry construct (\code{check-baseline-design-search.R}); on one
simulated trial, refitting S and F1 with the reflected biomarker
reproduced the CR2 and model-based $p$-values to six decimals and
negated the estimate (\code{check-stress-test-construct.R}, Section 8); and an
\code{lme} fit with random intercept and \code{corCAR1} on simulated
Hybrid data gave identical model-based ($0.0293568$) and CR2
($0.0210449$) $p$-values with the reflected biomarker and a
log-likelihood difference of $5\times10^{-13}$ (\code{check-baseline-design-search.R})
(all verified).

With a prognostic coupling the reflection negates $c_{bm,TV}$,
$c_{bm,PB}$, $c_{bm,BL}$ and $\beta^{TV}_{bm}$ as well, so the symmetry
is the joint one,
$\pi(-c_{bm}, -c_{\mathrm{prog}}) = \pi(c_{bm}, c_{\mathrm{prog}})$,
not symmetry in $c_{bm}$ alone (derived). It fails if only some
couplings are reflected (for example a predictive coupling reversed
while a prognostic one is not), and, for the distributional step, if
the biomarker is not symmetric about its mean. \note{\code{docs/48}
states the identity for cells in which the biomarker is correlated only
with the drug response; the joint condition is its general form.} The
symmetry screen of \code{docs/46}, Section 3, tested this identity with
independent replicates, and its failures were Monte Carlo error.
Generating the $-c_{bm}$ trials from the $+c_{bm}$ draws with the
biomarker reflected would make sampling asymmetry impossible (argued;
\code{docs/48}, Section 4).

%#############################################################
\part{The treatment main-effect track and shared performance measures}
%#############################################################

%=============================================================
\section{The treatment main-effect track}
\label{sec:maineffect}
%=============================================================

Paper 04 targets a different estimand under a different, simpler DGP.
The analysis IDs for the main-effect track (M-S, M-F1, M-VF, M-RV,
M-F1A) and the theoretical decomposition (Propositions~1 to~7) are
documented in \code{docs/58}.

The estimand is the treatment \emph{main} effect $\beta_D$, with
\textbf{no biomarker moderation channel at all} and a linear (not
Gompertz) data-generating model.

\subsection{Data-generating model}

For the N-of-1 arm,
\begin{equation}
Y_{ij} = \beta_0 + \beta_D\, D_{bc,ij} + \beta_T\, t_{ij} + u_i +
  \varepsilon_{ij}, \qquad u_i \sim \mathcal N(0,\sigma_u^2),
\label{eq:me-nof1}
\end{equation}
with $\boldsymbol\varepsilon_i$ following a continuous-time AR(1)
covariance $\mathrm{Cov}(\varepsilon_j,\varepsilon_k) = \sigma_w^2
\rho^{|t_j-t_k|}$, $\sigma_w = 2.3$, $\rho = 0.3$, with $t_j$ the period
index; carryover is applied as an additive exponential-decay term to
the period immediately following a drug period,
$\beta_D^{\mathrm{co}} = \beta_D \exp\{-\ln(2)\,L/t_{1/2}\}$, with $L
= 7$ days the period length and $t_{1/2} = 3$ days at the primary cell.
For the comparator parallel-group RCT arm, drawing patient baselines
from a shared pool,
\begin{equation}
Y_i = \mu_0 + u_i + \beta_D Z_i + \varepsilon_i, \qquad u_i \sim
  \mathcal N(0, \sigma_u^2), \qquad \varepsilon_i \sim \mathcal N(0, \sigma_w^2),
  \qquad Z_i \in \{0,1\},
\label{eq:me-rct}
\end{equation}
with $\mu_0 = 8.0$ nightmares per week and $\sigma_u = 1.5$
(\note{2026-10-09: notation. Paper 04 writes this SD $\sigma_\tau$;
$\tau^2$ is the canonical heterogeneity variance of
Equation~\eqref{eq:senn-model}, and $u_i$ is the random intercept, so
its SD is $\sigma_u$.}). The
two arms share the same $N=35$ patient pool (participant-count-matched,
not observation-count-matched, a limitation the paper itself notes),
fit respectively by \code{nlme::lme(Sx \textasciitilde\ Dbc + t\_wk,
..., correlation = corCAR1(...))} and by ANCOVA, \code{lm(endpoint
\textasciitilde\ trt + baseline)}.

\note{2026-10-08: three corrections. (i) The carryover term was written
$\delta_c$, which collides with the standardized bias $\delta$; it is
$\beta_D^{\mathrm{co}}$ here. (ii) Paper 04 draws the baselines from
$\mathcal N(\mu_0, \sigma_u^2)$ and calls $u_i$ ``the patient's baseline
draw'' while also adding $\mu_0$ in the model, which counts $\mu_0$
twice; the consistent reading, used above, is $u_i \sim \mathcal N(0,
\sigma_u^2)$ with baseline $\mu_0 + u_i$. (iii) Paper 04 describes
its test as using Satterthwaite degrees of freedom ``from
\code{nlme}''; \code{nlme::lme} reports containment (between/within)
degrees of freedom, not Satterthwaite. Which the driver uses was not
checked.}

\note{Internal inconsistency, resolved.} Paper 04 previously stated
$\sigma_w = 1.0$ in the AR(1) covariance definition of its DGP
(\S2.1) while separately citing $\sigma_w = 2.3$ as ``moderate
within-patient measurement noise'' in its optimal-design-conditions
discussion (\S6), with the two values never reconciled in the source
text. Inspection of the production driver
(\code{analysis/scripts/treatment-main-effect/production-run.R},
constant \code{SIGMA\_WITHIN <- 2.3}) confirms $\sigma_w = 2.3$ is
the value the simulation actually uses; \S2.1's $\sigma_w = 1.0$ was
the error, and has been corrected in the manuscript to match both
\S6 and the code.

\subsection{Classical closed-form power theory (background, not the
paper's own simulation)}

Paper 04's literature-review section reproduces the closed-form
power apparatus that most of this compendium replaces with simulation,
due to Senn (2018) and Zucker et al.:
\begin{align}
d_{ij} &= \Delta_i + \varepsilon_{ij}, \qquad \Delta_i \sim
  \mathcal N(\Delta,\tau^2), \qquad \varepsilon_{ij}\sim \mathcal N(0, 2\sigma^2)
  \label{eq:senn-model} \\
\mathrm{Var}(\hat\Delta) &= \frac{\tau^2 + 2\sigma^2/k}{n} \qquad
  \text{(random-effects, patient-mean analysis)}
  \label{eq:senn-var-re} \\
\Lambda_{\mathrm{RE}} &= \frac{\Delta}{\sqrt{(\tau^2+2\sigma^2/k)/n}},
  \quad \nu = n-1
  \label{eq:senn-ncp-re} \\
\Lambda_{\mathrm{FE}} &= \frac{\Delta}{\sqrt{2\sigma^2/(nk)}}, \quad
  \nu = n(k-1) \qquad \text{(fixed-effects, pools within-patient SS)}
  \label{eq:senn-ncp-fe}
\end{align}
\note{2026-10-08: the patient-level effect and the noncentrality
parameters were written $\delta_i$ and $\lambda$, which collide with
the canonical $\delta$ and $\lambda$; they are $\Delta_i$ and
$\Lambda$ here. $\Lambda_{\mathrm{RE}}$ and $\Lambda_{\mathrm{FE}}$ are
noncentral-$t$ parameters, not the noncentral-$F$ $\Lambda$ of
Equation~\eqref{eq:rmanova-power}.}
for $n$ patients and $k$ paired treatment cycles each, with $\tau^2$
the between-patient (treatment-by-patient interaction) variance and
$2\sigma^2$ the within-patient, within-cycle variance of paired
differences. The paper notes standard software assuming $n-1$
denominator df underestimates power here whenever $k>2$, because the
fixed-effects analysis correctly pools within-patient degrees of
freedom that the random-effects analysis discards. Zucker et al.'s
equivalent precision formula,
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Var}(\hat\Delta) = \frac{\tau^2 + \sigma_w^2/k}{n},
\label{eq:zucker-var}
\end{equation}
which is Equation~\eqref{eq:senn-var-re} with $\sigma_w^2 = 2\sigma^2$
the within-patient variance of a paired difference (here $\sigma_w$
is not Paper 04's AR(1) residual SD of
Equation~\eqref{eq:me-nof1}), makes explicit that the marginal benefit of additional cycles $k$
diminishes once $\sigma_w^2/k \ll \tau^2$: past that point, adding
cycles to existing patients buys far less precision than adding new
patients, because the between-patient variance floor dominates.

Paper 04's own simulation (2{,}000 replicates per cell, effect-size
grid $\beta_D\in\{0,-0.5,-1.0,-2.0\}$ nightmares/week, carryover
half-life sweep $t_{1/2}\in\{0.5,1.0,3.0,5.0,7.0\}$ days) does not
invoke Equations~\eqref{eq:senn-var-re}--\eqref{eq:zucker-var}
directly; it estimates power and Type~I error empirically, at the
MCSE rates given in \S\ref{sec:performance} below.

%=============================================================
\section{Shared performance measures}
\label{sec:performance}
%=============================================================

The manuscripts of the compendium report the same Monte Carlo
performance quantities, following the ADEMP framework of Morris,
White, and Crowther (2019):

\begin{align}
\hat\pi &= \frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{reps}}}\sum_{r=1}^{n_{\mathrm{reps}}}
  \mathbf 1[p_r < \alpha] \qquad \text{(empirical power / Type~I error)}
  \label{eq:perf-power} \\
\mathrm{MCSE}(\hat\pi) &= \sqrt{\hat\pi(1-\hat\pi)/n_{\mathrm{reps}}}
  \label{eq:perf-mcse-power} \\
\mathrm{Bias} &= \frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{reps}}}\sum_r \hat\theta_r -
  \theta_{\mathrm{true}}, \qquad \mathrm{MCSE(Bias)} =
  \frac{s_{\hat\theta}}{\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{reps}}}}
  \label{eq:perf-bias} \\
\mathrm{MCSE}(\mathrm{EmpSE}) &= \frac{s_{\hat\theta}}
  {\sqrt{2(n_{\mathrm{reps}}-1)}}
  \label{eq:perf-mcse-se}
\end{align}
with $s_{\hat\theta}$ the empirical standard deviation of the
replicate-level point estimates, $\mathrm{EmpSE} = s_{\hat\theta}$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:perf-mcse-se} is the Monte Carlo standard error of
the \emph{empirical} standard error (Morris et al., Table 6), not of
the mean model-based standard error $\overline{\widehat{SE}}$. Here
$\hat\theta_r$ denotes a generic replicate estimate; in Paper 10's
sense it is the estimator of \S\ref{sec:calibration}. A replicate count
of $n_{\mathrm{reps}} = 625$ is the recurring reference, the smallest
for which $\mathrm{MCSE}(\hat\pi) \le 0.02$ at the worst case
$\hat\pi=0.5$ ($0.25/0.02^2 = 625$). Production runs use various
counts, typically 500 to 1{,}000 replicates for power and bias cells
and up to 5{,}000 for null cells. In null cells the MCSE at
$\hat\pi \approx 0.05$ is smaller for a given $n_{\mathrm{reps}}$
(0.0069 at 1{,}000), but a size check must resolve departures that
are small relative to 0.05, which is why null cells receive more
replicates.

\note{2026-10-08: corrected. Equation~\eqref{eq:perf-mcse-se} was
labeled $\mathrm{MCSE}(\widehat{SE})$; the paragraph previously said
that a smaller rejection rate near $\alpha$ ``tightens the MCSE
requirement'', where the binomial MCSE in fact falls as $\hat\pi$
moves from 0.5 toward 0.05. ``Every manuscript reports the same three
quantities'' was softened: not every paper reports bias (Paper 07
reports it only at the null).}

Some sources also refer to the mean squared error (one of the
whitepapers inventoried for this revision states MSE = variance +
squared bias in prose),
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
\mathrm{MSE} = \frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{reps}}}\sum_r
  \big(\hat\theta_r - \theta_{\mathrm{true}}\big)^2
  = \mathrm{Bias}^2 + \frac{n_{\mathrm{reps}} - 1}{n_{\mathrm{reps}}}\,
    s_{\hat\theta}^2, \\
\mathrm{MCSE}(\mathrm{MSE}) = \sqrt{\frac{\sum_r \big[(\hat\theta_r -
  \theta_{\mathrm{true}})^2 - \mathrm{MSE}\big]^2}
  {n_{\mathrm{reps}}(n_{\mathrm{reps}} - 1)}}
\end{gathered}
\label{eq:perf-mse}
\end{equation}
(definition and MCSE as in Morris et al., Table 6; the decomposition is
derived). \note{2026-10-08: added, because the term is used without
definition in the sources; which papers report it in their tables was
not surveyed.}

For a difference-of-differences comparison across two conditions, the
pre-restatement version of Paper 01 gives
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{SE}\big(\Delta_B - \Delta_A\big) = \sqrt{
  \frac{\hat\pi_{B,0}(1-\hat\pi_{B,0})}{n} +
  \frac{\hat\pi_{B,1}(1-\hat\pi_{B,1})}{n} +
  \frac{\hat\pi_{A,0}(1-\hat\pi_{A,0})}{n} +
  \frac{\hat\pi_{A,1}(1-\hat\pi_{A,1})}{n}}, \qquad \Delta_X =
  \hat\pi_{X,0} - \hat\pi_{X,1},
\label{eq:perf-dod}
\end{equation}
the standard error of the difference between two within-condition
carryover losses, each estimated from $n$ independent replicates per
cell. The form assumes the four power estimates are independent; with
shared seeds across cells (paired designs, as in the restated Paper
01's study 2) it is conservative or must be replaced by a paired
estimate (argued). It is used to test whether two designs',
architectures', or specifications' power differences are
distinguishable from Monte Carlo noise.

\note{2026-10-08: the restated Paper 01 no longer uses
Equation~\eqref{eq:perf-dod}; the formula is retained from the
pre-restatement version (\code{archive/\allowbreak
report-2026-10-07-pre-restatement.Rmd}).}


\subsection{Size-adjusted power}
\label{sec:invc-size-adjusted}

When a test's realized size departs from $\alpha$, power at the nominal
critical value mixes sensitivity with miscalibration. Size-adjusted
power replaces the nominal critical value by an empirical one from the
matching null cell $c_0$, the cell that shares design, architecture and
$t_{1/2}$ (or the same stress, or the zero point of the same curve).
Two forms are in use:
\begin{equation}
\tilde\pi = \frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{reps}}}\sum_{r=1}^{n_{\mathrm{reps}}}
  \mathbf 1\bigl[\,|z_r| > q_{1-\alpha}\bigr]
\quad\text{or}\quad
\tilde\pi = \frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{reps}}}\sum_{r=1}^{n_{\mathrm{reps}}}
  \mathbf 1\bigl[\,p_r < \hat q_{\alpha}\bigr],
\label{eq:invc-size-adjusted}
\end{equation}
with $z = \hat\beta_{bm:D}/\widehat{SE}$, $q_{1-\alpha}$ the
$(1-\alpha)$ quantile of $|z|$ over the null-cell replicates
(\code{docs/33}, glossary and Section 2.4, and its Appendix 1 script),
and $\hat q_\alpha$ the empirical $\alpha$ quantile of the analysis's
two-sided $p$-values in the null cell (\code{docs/46}, Section 6;
\code{docs/47}, glossary; the decision script
\code{12-strawman-\allowbreak stress-\allowbreak decision.R},
inspected). The two forms coincide when every replicate's test uses the
same reference distribution; with replicate-specific degrees of freedom
(CR2 with Satterthwaite degrees of freedom, Kenward--Roger) $p$ is not a
fixed function of $|z|$ and they can differ slightly (derived). Both
inherit the Monte Carlo error of the estimated quantile, which with
1,000 null replicates rests on about 50 exceedances, so \code{docs/33}
treats its size-adjusted figures as indicating magnitude only; no MCSE
for $\tilde\pi$ is given there. \note{An MCSE that accounts for the
estimated quantile would need, for example, a bootstrap over the null
replicates; none has been computed. The binomial MCSE that the stress
test uses (\S\ref{sec:invd-criteria}) ignores this component.} Applied to the pre-restatement
Paper~01 output, size adjustment roughly halved the
covariance-minus-mean gap in carryover loss (0.071 to about 0.029 in
Hybrid, 0.217 to about 0.097 in OL+BDC), because the ratio $\kappa$ of
model-based to empirical standard error rose with carryover from 1.12
to 1.26 in the OL+BDC covariance cells (quoted from \code{docs/33},
Section 2.4; reanalysis of stored replicates, not rechecked here).

\subsection{Size, power screens and the selection criteria of the
stress test}
\label{sec:invd-criteria}

The exploratory stress test (\S\ref{sec:invd-stress}) judges each
analysis $a$ by the following quantities (Sources: \code{docs/46},
Sections 3, 6 and 7; \code{docs/47}, glossary; inspected in the
decision script
\code{12-strawman-\allowbreak stress-\allowbreak decision.R}).

\textbf{Nominal size.} With $\hat\pi_{a,0}$ the rejection rate pooled
over the $R$ replicates of the non-prognostic null cells,
\begin{equation}
\big|\hat\pi_{a,0} - 0.05\big| \le 2\sqrt{0.05 \times 0.95/R}
\quad\text{and}\quad \max_{\text{null cells}} \hat\pi_{a,0,\text{cell}}
  \le 0.075,
\label{eq:invd-nominal}
\end{equation}
the binomial MCSE of Equation~\eqref{eq:perf-mcse-power} evaluated at
the nominal 0.05. For the 11 non-prognostic null cells of 1,000
replicates, $R = 11{,}000$ and the band is $0.0458$ to $0.0542$; for the
eight null cells of 500 replicates in \code{docs/45}, the band is
$0.0431$ to $0.0569$, against which \code{docs/46}, Section 8, judges
the mean of 0.058 (verified, \code{check-stress-test-construct.R}, Section 9). An
analysis is flagged if any prognostic null cell exceeds 0.075.

\textbf{Size-adjusted power.} $\tilde\pi_a(c)$ is the $p$-value form
of Equation~\eqref{eq:invc-size-adjusted} for analysis $a$ in
alternative cell $c$, with $\hat q_{a,c_0}$ the empirical 5\% quantile
of $a$'s $p$-values in the matching null cell $c_0$ (the same stress,
or the zero point of the same curve) and $R_c$ replicates.
Its MCSE is computed as binomial, at most $\sqrt{0.25/500} = 0.022$ for
500 replicates, which ignores the sampling error of $\hat q_{a,c_0}$;
the difference between two independent cells of 500 then has an MCSE of
at most $0.032$ (\code{docs/47}, Section 5, ``about 0.03''; verified).

\textbf{Shortfall and its minimax.} Over the set $\mathcal S$ of
analyses passing the size and sensitivity screens, flagged ones
included,
\begin{equation}
\Delta_a(c) = \max_{a' \in \mathcal S}\tilde\pi_{a'}(c) - \tilde\pi_a(c),
\qquad m_a = \max_{c \in \mathcal C}\Delta_a(c),
\label{eq:invd-shortfall}
\end{equation}
with $\mathcal C$ the non-prognostic alternative cells (or, in the
second reading, all 26), and the rule selects $\arg\min_a m_a$ among
the unflagged survivors, with ties within 0.02 broken by the detectable
coupling and then by the smaller $q$ of Table~\ref{tab:invd-analyses}.
\note{The sources call $m_a$ the ``maximin shortfall''; selecting the
analysis with the smallest largest shortfall is a minimax-regret rule.
The best analysis in each cell is taken over $\mathcal S$, which
includes the flagged C1 and C2 (inspected), so the shortfalls of the
robust analyses are measured against the phase-variance models, which
have the highest power in the cells \code{docs/47} tabulates (inferred
from \code{docs/47}, Section 6).}
\note{2026-10-15: the tie margin is small against Monte Carlo error. In
the rerun under the refined process, the paired bootstrap standard
error of the differences in $m_a$ among F1, G1 and G2 is 0.012 to 0.016
with 2,000 trials per alternative cell, and roughly twice that with
500. On 500 trials per cell G2 led F1 by 0.026; on the pooled 2,000,
F1, G1 and G2 lie within 0.005 and the tie-break selects F1
(\code{docs/47}; verified there).}

\textbf{Sensitivity.} On the size-adjusted power curve over the grid
$0 = c_0 < c_1 < \cdots$ of positive couplings, with
$\tilde\pi(0) = 0.05$: monotonicity requires $\tilde\pi(c_{j+1}) -
\tilde\pi(c_j) \ge -2\,\mathrm{MCSE}$ of the difference for every $j$;
the steepness is $\{\tilde\pi(0.30) - \tilde\pi(0.15)\}/0.15$; and the
detectable coupling is the linear interpolation
\begin{equation}
c_{0.8} = c_{j-1} + \frac{(0.8 - \tilde\pi(c_{j-1}))(c_j -
  c_{j-1})}{\tilde\pi(c_j) - \tilde\pi(c_{j-1})},
\qquad j = \min\{j : \tilde\pi(c_j) \ge 0.8\},
\label{eq:invd-detect}
\end{equation}
undefined when no grid point reaches 0.8, as for every robust analysis
(\code{docs/47}, Section 5). \note{\code{docs/46} states monotonicity
``on either side of zero''; the decision script checks the positive
side only, the negative side having the single point $-0.30$ (or
$-0.20$). The symmetry component was dropped in Amendment 1; by
Equation~\eqref{eq:invd-symmetry} it tested an identity.}

\textbf{Paired comparison.} Two analyses fitted to the same trials are
compared in an alternative cell by McNemar's test on the paired
size-adjusted rejections, in its exact form: with $n_{10}$ and $n_{01}$
the trials rejected by one analysis only,
\begin{equation}
p = \Pr\big(|K - n/2| \ge |n_{10} - n/2|\big), \qquad
K \sim \mathrm{Binomial}(n, 1/2), \quad n = n_{10} + n_{01}
\label{eq:invd-mcnemar}
\end{equation}
(two-sided exact binomial test; inspected). The strawman rule of
\code{docs/46} replaces the reference analysis only if a survivor beats
it on $m_a$ by more than 0.05 with $p < 0.05$ in at least half the
alternative cells.
